LAW301 Essay Outlines

All 45 essays on the essay sheet, in the order of the NotebookLM videos. Each essay has the exam question, a practice hypo, the full answer written out in plain sentences, and 210 quick questions in all to check yourself.

How to use this page

  1. Read the question and the hypo, and plan your three sections before you look.
  2. Open Read the full answer to check your plan. It explains where the statement goes wrong, what each theorist says, the best case against you, and the repaired statement.
  3. Answer the questions under Check yourself. Every option is explained once you answer. Your answers stay in this browser.

How a LAW301 essay works. You are given a statement and asked to comment on it. Find the crux, the one word or step where the statement goes wrong. Test that step with three to five theorists, saying what each implies for these exact words. Define each key term once, early. Give the best case for the statement and admit your own weak point. End with a repaired statement, the version you can defend, and one consequence for the person the statement is about.

Key: TSH is Tan Seow Hon. A block number such as E1.2 is block 2 under essay E1 on the essay sheet. ★ very highly likely, ■ highly likely, ▲ likely, as marked on the sheet.

Video 1Private choice and the morals lawLaw-making

E1

Liberty against morals law

★ very highly likely

The question 2017-18 Q2 · 2020-21 Q2

"Individuals have the right to make the most fundamental decisions about their own lives, such as whether to bear a child and with whom to have an intimate relationship. Therefore, legislators should not enforce morality or regulate private behaviour." Critically comment, and say how far Hart, Devlin, Rawls and Finnis would agree.

The hypo Hypo 3, MP limb

An MP's bill makes adultery an offence punishable by up to two years' imprisonment; the Minister says police will not proactively enforce it, as it is "a moral signpost". Grounds: every major religion condemns adultery; children of marriages broken by infidelity fare worse; a divided consultation, but a clear majority for the law. How would you vote?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

Everything turns on the word "therefore". The first sentence states a right. The second draws a sweeping conclusion: no morals law at all. Something must bridge the gap, and the bridge is usually left unsaid: that only harm to others justifies coercion. Name that bridge yourself, then test it. A second trap is the word "enforce", which can mean three different things.

The answer in one breath

Start with two definitions. To enforce morality means using law to secure conduct because it is right, or to stop it because it is wrong. Private behaviour means conduct that affects no one who has not consented. The statement is right that private consensual conduct should not be punished for its immorality alone. It is wrong that a right to intimate choices bars every law touching morals, for two reasons: the right is itself a moral claim with moral limits, and law can regulate or promote without punishing. Take the right first, then harm as its supposed means, then the conclusion about morals law.

Section 1: "a right to decide"

The first claim is right in what it protects and wrong in what it assumes. To say that private consensual conduct is not wrong hides a positive judgement: that consensual acts harming no one are not wrong. That is a moral view, from the lectures.

Now the four theorists, using the kit on the sheet (E1.1).

  • Hart largely agrees. He defends a strong presumption, not an absolute right. He keeps paternalism and public decency, and he treats whether to punish and how severely as two separate questions. Free choice may be valuable in itself, or because it lets people "experiment" in living.
  • Devlin rejects the claim in principle but often agrees in practice. No private sphere is closed to the law; privacy is an elastic principle, not a right. Yet he demands toleration up to the limits of tolerance, refuses to count heads, and eventually backed decriminalisation.
  • Rawls partly agrees. He protects the basic liberties, but not on the statement's ground. Moral autonomy as the highest value is a comprehensive ideal, one view of the good life among many, so it cannot be the basis of political rights. Political autonomy, by contrast, is a political value, and denying basic liberties "normally" breaks reciprocity.
  • Finnis partly agrees. Self-determination matters, but the statement's argument fails on its own terms. To appeal to subjective preference, and then ground it in the intrinsic value of self-governing, is "outright self-contradiction". And where a choice touches someone else's basic good, he asks who belongs in the circle of people owed fair terms.

TSH adds a neat test: autonomy's primacy rests on the equal worth of persons. Unlimited, it leaves everyone "equally worthless", because no choice would be better than any other.

Close the section like this: the right bars punishment for immorality alone, but it cannot settle cases where a third party is at stake. Begetting or rearing a child involves someone other than the chooser, as TSH points out, which is why the right cannot settle surrogacy or adoption. On abortion, either answer on who counts settles a moral question; TSH says a "fairly close call" is no neutral midpoint.

The hinge: what "enforce" means

Picture a ladder with three rungs (block E1.2). The bottom rung is promote: the state encourages something, for example by protecting and promoting marriage. The middle rung is regulate: rules, licences and limits. The top rung is punish: the criminal law. The justification needed rises as you climb, because coercion rises. That is why Hart against Devlin, which is about the criminal law, is only one corner of the wider question Rawls and Finnis ask.

Hart's insight survives here: coercion is "prima facie objectionable". It punishes the offender and also constrains everyone else, who now obeys out of fear. So a good reason to regulate is not yet a reason to imprison. But promotion is not free either. School curricula, media rules and adoption law all bear on citizens, so promotion also needs public reasons.

Singapore's 2022 package shows the ladder working. The criminal law withdrew from private consensual conduct when s 377A was repealed, while Article 156 lets Parliament protect and promote marriage. One rung came down; another stayed.

Section 2: "harm to others"

Here you name the hidden bridge: the "therefore" needs the premise that only harm to others justifies coercion (block E1.3).

  • Hart says "only harm to others" is too narrow, though bare moral condemnation never suffices. He accepts paternalism where consent is defective, and public decency. He goes beyond Mill, whose anti-paternalism he says may now seem fantastic; he would punish the supply of drugs even to adults, except on prescription, and he would punish bigamy as a public nuisance, not as immoral.
  • Devlin rejects the limit. There are no theoretical limits to the law's power against immorality, which, like subversion, can by its nature threaten a society. That is why consent is no defence to the gravest offences against the person: the offence is against society. His limits are practical: maximum freedom consistent with society's integrity, slowness, privacy.
  • Rawls says it is the wrong test. He tests the kind of reason, not the kind of conduct. A harm-only rule belongs to Mill's comprehensive liberalism, and imposing it would enforce Mill's ideal of individuality on citizens who reject it. A majority acting on public reasons enacts "legitimate law".
  • Finnis partly agrees. Protecting others is a legitimate ground, not the only one: the state may restrict conduct that damages the basic goods, even acts that are simply senseless. But that is a permission, not a programme, used inside a limited state that keeps peace and justice, on principles accessible to everyone, whatever their religion.

Close: harm is the right presumption for punishment, not the only ground, and what counts as harm is itself a moral question. Even the Court of Appeal in Lim Meng Suang called the meaning of harm contested and left it to Parliament. A picture makes it clear: a trader ruined by a fair competitor is harmed, yet by no wrongdoer. So "harm" already contains a judgement about what is wrong. In 2022 Shanmugam said a crime generally needs a public order or public interest issue, "broadly similar" to the Wolfenden approach, so non-consensual acts, acts involving the young and public indecency stay crimes.

Section 3: "morals law"

The conclusion fails because every law enforces some morality. Hart's target is narrower: punishing immorality as such. There the statement is right. Use the kit on legitimacy (E1.4).

  • Hart: enforcing on critical-morality grounds (harm, defective consent, public offence) is legitimate; punishing immorality as such is not. His objections are moral and prudential, not sceptical. Devlin's claim that society would disintegrate has no evidence behind it, and, in Hart's comparison, deserves no more respect than the Emperor Justinian's old statement about earthquakes.
  • Devlin agrees that society may use law against conduct beyond the limits of tolerance, with restraints that are prudential: toleration, slowness, privacy, each offence weighed on its own. He adds one secular limit: the law cannot rely on doctrines citizens may disbelieve.
  • Rawls: enforcing a comprehensive doctrine as such is illegitimate, but his objection is itself moral, since reciprocity asks for reasons others might reasonably accept.
  • Finnis: the state may favour the basic goods and takes a public stand whatever it does. Its limits are moral (peace and justice, the absolute rules, respect for every basic good) and prudential, like weighing chilling effects on free speech in a particular place.

Close: what can make enforcement unjust is its reasons, its coercion and who bears it, not the bare fact that it is moral. Even repeal speaks. Article 156 came with the 2022 repeal precisely to block the inference that public morality had become neutral. TSH puts it well: we "simply cannot shun" the moral question.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case starts from reasonable pluralism (E1.6). Political power is the power of free and equal citizens collectively. We enter society only by birth and leave only by death, so using that power to impose one faction's view of the good life treats dissenters as subjects, not partners. That is Rawls. Hart adds that liberty may be valuable in itself, and humility adds that we have been wrong before. This shows that the state must not make one comprehensive doctrine official. It does not show that every morals law is barred.

My weak point (E1.7): my answer leans on Finnis's basic goods. They are accessible to reason, but not accepted by everyone, and their self-evidence arguably rests on intuitions shaped by upbringing. Does my view impose his conception? It would if his goods alone could justify coercion. They cannot: reasonable disagreement limits what the state may coerce, even where Finnis is right about the good.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right about punishment and wrong about much else the law does. All four accept that private choices deserve some protection (Devlin through toleration and privacy) and that harm to others may be punished. They split on why the choice matters, and on whether immorality as such may ever be punished. Repaired: “Choices about one's intimate life deserve strong protection from punishment, but not immunity from all regulation, or from the state's promotion of other goods.” For the legislator, this means regulating or promoting before punishing, and punishing only on reasons as strong as the coercion.

Two variants. If the statement only asks legislators to "respect" liberty in intimate matters, open differently: every theorist accepts that in some form, which is why it settles little; they agree that intimate choices deserve protection and part on why (a comprehensive ideal, a political value, or one good among others) and on what "respect" forbids. If the statement says liberty is "inevitably" sacrificed, use essay E6 instead. If its conclusion is about neutrality, section 3 uses the neutrality kit (E1.5). Watch one thing: Hart can easily be explained three times in this essay. Give him one sentence in each place.

Using it in the hypo

Start with the right to decide (section 1). Adultery is an intimate choice, but it is not purely private: the spouse and the children are third parties, so the right alone cannot settle the bill. Then use the ladder (the hinge). Punishing is the top rung and needs the strongest reasons; promoting marriage would cost liberty far less. In section 3, attack the "moral signpost" idea. A law kept on the books but not enforced still carries the stigma, and it hands police and prosecutors what Hart calls "formidable discriminatory powers" (E2.3). Vote against the offence.

Memory line

A right to decide is a moral claim; enforcing is a ladder; harm is the bridge you must name.

Check yourself

0 / 5
cruxIn E1 ("a right to decide … therefore the law must not enforce morality"), what does the "therefore" need that the statement often leaves unsaid?
  1. Useful in the intro, but it is not the missing link between the right and the conclusion.
  2. The statement does not rest on the majority; that is E5's theme.
  3. Correct: section 2's opener. Name that premise yourself, then test it in section 2.
  4. The essay is about what legislators should do, not constitutional text.
What links a right to decide to a ban on all morals law?
key ideaThe hinge block E1.2 says "enforce morality" can mean three things. Which order runs from least to most coercive?
  1. Correct. The justification needed rises with the coercion used, so Hart v Devlin, about the criminal law, is only one corner of the question Rawls and Finnis ask.
  2. That runs from most to least coercive.
  3. Promotion coerces less than regulation, so it comes first.
  4. Regulation sits between promotion and punishment.
Think of a ladder of force.

  1. Correct: E1.1's Finnis cell. He also asks why Rawls draws reciprocity's boundary at birth.
  2. That is Rawls's objection, not Finnis's.
  3. His basic goods have no objective hierarchy, so no good is highest.
  4. That mixes in Rawls's test; Finnis's objection is internal inconsistency.
His charge is that the argument contradicts itself.

  2. Art 156 promotes one definition; permitting decides too.
  3. Parliament repealed it; Lim Meng Suang had left the question to Parliament.
  4. Shanmugam called the approach "broadly similar" to Wolfenden's.
Two rungs moved in opposite directions.
key ideaHow does section 1 of E1 close on the limits of the right to decide?
  1. The essay asks what legislators should do, not what the courts have held.
  2. The right does not depend on majority approval; that confuses E1 with E5.
  3. Correct. Begetting or rearing a child involves someone other than the chooser (TSH), so surrogacy and adoption stay open.
  4. That is the statement's overreach; the right is itself a moral claim with moral limits.
Who else is affected when a child is involved?
E5

The majority against private choice

The question exam-style

Form (a): "In a democracy the majority may enforce its morality against private choices it condemns." Form (b): "Private choices must prevail over the majority's morality unless they harm others." Discuss.

The hypo new, same Ruritania

An MP's bill fines unmarried adult couples who live together. A poll finds 72% "disapprove" of cohabitation; she cites studies that children of cohabiting parents fare worse on average. A second MP says private choices are no business of the state. How would you vote?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

This is a false dilemma. The statement puts the majority on one side, the individual on the other, and harm as the line between them, as if nothing else stood between them. Both sides overstate.

The answer in one breath

The majority may decide, but its disapproval alone is no reason to punish. The right to private choice is real, but it is itself a moral claim. And harm is the right line for punishment, but not a neutral one. By private choice I mean a choice about one's own intimate life that affects no one who has not consented. Take the majority, then private choice, then harm.

Section 1: "the majority"

The majority's morality decides who makes the law, not what makes it right (kit E2.3).

  • Hart: the bare fact that the majority's morality condemns conduct gives no reason to punish it. Critical morality must test the majority's judgement. He also counts what the majority imposes: punishment, constraint on everyone else, and, with laws kept but not enforced, "formidable discriminatory powers" for police and prosecutors.
  • Devlin, the statement's natural ally, refuses to count heads. His test is what twelve people chosen at random would, after discussion, be expected to accept unanimously, like a jury. A majority's dislike is "not nearly enough", and disgust counts only if it is deeply felt and not manufactured.
  • Rawls: legitimacy needs more than a majority and less than unanimity. When officials and citizens follow public reason, the majority's enactment is "legitimate law", but because of its reasons, not because it is a majority.

Singapore shows the gap between disapproval and punishment. In 2007 the consultation feedback was "emotional, divided", with a majority for keeping s 377A (Ho Peng Kee). Yet Hri Kumar pointed out that the 70% who "frowned on" homosexuality were never asked whether it should be a crime. In 2022 Masagos said MPs must decide.

Close: nor is the majority always on the side of punishment. In 2022 Xie Yao Quan cited the prevailing morality both for repeal and for Article 156. And where fundamental rights are at stake, a majority morality that derogates from them, one that treats some citizens as lesser by race for example, may not be enforced (TSH). So majority disapproval alone is no reason to punish.

Section 2: "private choice"

The right to private choice is real, but it is itself a moral claim (kit E1.1). Hart defends a strong presumption, not an absolute. Rawls protects basic liberties as political values, not as a comprehensive ideal of autonomy. If the statement grounds the choice in autonomy, add Finnis: appealing to subjective preference and then grounding it in the value of self-governance is "outright self-contradiction".

Close: the right bars punishment for immorality alone; it cannot settle cases where a third party is at stake. And the choice must be truly free. Hart discounts choices made without reflection, from passing desires, with clouded judgement, under inner compulsion or under subtle pressure. Even Perera, arguing for repeal in 2022, accepted that a minor cannot meaningfully consent.

Section 3: "harm"

Harm is the right line for punishment, but not a neutral one (kit E1.3). Hart accepts harm plus paternalism and public decency, never moralism alone. Devlin sets no theoretical limit. So harm is argued over, not a line you can simply read off. Add the first sentence of the ladder (E1.2): the justification needed rises with the coercion used.

Close: Hart draws the line tightly. Distress at the bare knowledge that others are acting wrongly cannot count as harm, or the only liberty left would be to do what no one seriously objects to. What counts as harm, and who counts as an "other", are moral questions, so the line needs reasons, not a poll. Singapore drew it this way in 2022: a crime generally needs a public order or public interest issue, so private consensual conduct left the criminal law.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

For form (a), the best case is democratic (E2.5). Majority rule treats citizens as equals amid disagreement: in 2007 Indranee Rajah said she would usually follow the majority unless there was reason to protect the minority. The legislature is the proper forum: Lim Meng Suang left the Hart and Devlin debate to Parliament, and TSH fears a juristocracy, rule by judges. And every society takes a public stand, as Finnis says. This shows the legislature may decide moral questions. It does not show that majority feeling makes its decision legitimate, let alone right.

For form (b), use the liberty and humility case from E1 (E1.6): pluralism shows that no one comprehensive doctrine may be made official, not that every morals law is barred.

My weak point (E2.6): requiring reasons beyond majority feeling is itself a contested moral position. Rawls's filter of the "reasonable" is political, yet moral, and TSH asks what standard grounds it. I concede it. But that confirms my thesis: every answer takes a moral stand, so legislators cannot avoid defending theirs in public.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right that harm marks the line for punishment, and wrong to set the majority and the individual against each other as if nothing stood between them. The majority decides, but on reasons; private choice deserves protection, but as a moral claim with moral limits. All four let the legislature decide. They split on what must stand behind its decision: critical morality (Hart), deliberated feeling (Devlin), public reasons (Rawls) or the basic goods (Finnis). Pluralism disciplines three things: the reasons, which must be accessible and sound; the coercion, graded to them; and the forum, a legislature deciding as a trustee, not a stenographer. Repaired: “The majority decides, but may punish private choices only on reasons it can defend to the minority, and harm to others is the strongest of them.” For the legislator, this means punishing last, and only on reasons the minority can examine.

Using it in the hypo

The poll does not settle it. The 72% who "disapprove" are like Hri Kumar's 70% in 2007: disapproving of conduct is not the same as wanting it made a crime, so the figure is evidence, not a mandate. The studies speak of children faring worse "on average", which is a reason to support families and promote stable relationships, not a reason to fine couples. Vote against the fines, and offer promotion and support instead.

Memory line

The majority decides who; reasons decide what; harm is the strongest reason, not a neutral line.

Check yourself

0 / 5
cruxE5 sets the majority's morality against private choice, with harm as the line between them. What is the main flaw?
  1. Correct. The majority may decide, but its disapproval alone is no reason to punish.
  2. What counts as harm, and who counts as an "other", are moral questions.
  3. It does decide; the point is that its dislike is not a reason.
  4. The right to private choice is itself a moral claim, with moral limits.
Neither side is neutral.
theoristsWhat is Devlin's test for what the law-maker may enforce?
  1. He is a cultural relativist: he enforces a society's actual morality.
  2. For Devlin a majority's dislike is "not nearly enough"; he does not count heads.
  3. Correct. He refuses to count heads. Deliberated feeling can still be prejudice (E15.1), but it is not a head-count.
  4. That is Hart's test, not Devlin's. Devlin does not limit the law to harm; he asks what reasonable people would accept after discussion.
A jury, not a poll.
singaporeWhat was Hri Kumar's point in 2007 about the 70%?
  1. There is no such claim; most favoured retention.
  2. The referendum request came from Poa, in 2022.
  3. They were the majority in the feedback.
  4. Correct. Disapproving of conduct is not wanting it punished.
Disapproval is not the same answer as criminalisation.
key ideaHow does section 3 of E5 use Hart to draw the harm line tightly?
  1. Hart's line is a moral one for the legislator, not a test borrowed from tort.
  2. That is closer to Devlin's reasonable man; Hart excludes bare distress.
  3. Correct. Otherwise the only liberty left would be to do what no one seriously objects to.
  4. That is Finnis's permission, not Hart's harm line.
Whose distress counts?
hypoIn the cohabitation-fine hypo, how should you use the study that children of cohabiting parents "fare worse on average"?
  1. Hume's gap is only logical: facts fix the stakes.
  2. A poll is evidence, not a mandate, and the study does not change that.
  3. Correct. Average outcomes fix the stakes; they do not justify the top rung.
  4. "On average" does not make each couple a wrongdoer; regulate or promote first.
Match the coercion to the strength of the reason.

How these essays fit together

E1 and E5 share three kits: the right to decide (E1.1), harm to others (E1.3) and the ladder (E1.2). E1 leads with the right; E5 leads with the majority (E2.3) and only then turns to the right. If both appeared on one paper, which they will not in the same section, keep each kit's theorist sentences in one essay and give the other a single clause. The big shared idea: neither the individual's choice nor the majority's feeling is neutral, so both must be defended with reasons, and the more coercive the law, the stronger those reasons must be.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • E1: everything turns on "therefore"; name the hidden bridge, harm to others.
  • The right to decide is real but moral; it bars punishment for immorality alone, not cases with a third party.
  • Enforce is a ladder: promote, regulate, punish. Justification rises with coercion.
  • Harm is the right presumption, not the only ground, and not neutral: the fair competitor harms no one wrongly.
  • Every law enforces some morality; what matters is reasons, coercion and who bears it.
  • E5: the majority decides who makes the law, not what makes it right. Devlin refuses to count heads.
  • Private choice must be truly free; harm must be argued for.
  • Repaired: the majority may punish private choices only on reasons it can defend to the minority.

Video 2Whose morality: the majority, the legislator or no oneLaw-making

E2

Whose morality may the law enforce

■ highly likely

The question 2016-17 Q2

"Enforcing morality through law is not necessarily unjust or illegitimate. The only valid objections to it are moral and prudential, not sceptical." Discuss, with the theorists' views and current controversies. (2014-15 Q2 form (b): "Majority reasons are not necessarily legitimate or correct. However, the legislature may enforce the morality of society.")

The hypo Hypo 1, MP limb

A bill bans all gambling, including at the two licensed casinos, with long prison terms and no exception save the charity lottery. Grounds: several religions call gambling a sin; problem gamblers fall into debt and crime; 78% "disapprove". How would you vote?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

This statement is largely right, which makes it harder. The marker wants you to agree with most of it and find the one place it slips. The slip is the word "sceptical", which has two senses. Denying that there is any moral truth defeats itself. But doubting who can know the moral truth is a real reason for caution.

The answer in one breath

Two definitions first. Illegitimate means a use of power the state has no right to make, whatever its content. Unjust means a law whose content wrongs those it binds. Enforcing morality is not illegitimate as such, since every law enforces some morality, and none of the four theorists objects on sceptical grounds: Hart's and Rawls's limits are moral, Devlin's are prudential, Finnis's are both. A scepticism that denies moral truth defeats itself. But "not sceptical" is too quick. Doubt about which moral claims are true, short of denying that any are, is a real reason for restraint. It governs how the law enforces morality, not whether it may.

Section 1: "enforcing morality"

Enforcing morality cannot be illegitimate as such, because no law avoids it. Use the legitimacy kit (E1.4).

  • Devlin agrees: society may use law against conduct beyond the limits of tolerance. His restraints are prudential: toleration, slowness, privacy, and no logic carrying from one offence to the next. He adds one secular limit: the law cannot rely on doctrines citizens may disbelieve.
  • Finnis agrees: the state may favour the basic goods, and it takes a public stand whatever it does. Its limits are moral (peace and justice, the absolute rules) and prudential, like weighing chilling effects on speech in a particular place.
  • Hart partly agrees. Enforcing on critical-morality grounds, such as harm, defective consent and public offence, is legitimate; punishing immorality as such is not. His objections are moral and prudential: coercion is "prima facie objectionable".
  • Rawls partly agrees. Enforcing a comprehensive doctrine as such is illegitimate, but that objection is itself moral, since reciprocity asks for reasons others might reasonably accept.

Then add a point on sin and crime. In 2005 Yaacob Ibrahim observed that drinking and other activities Muslims consider vices are not banned. In 2022 an opposition leader said that in a secular society sin and crime are separate. Even Devlin concedes the law cannot rely on doctrines citizens may disbelieve.

Close: what can make enforcement unjust is its reasons, its coercion and who bears it. Even withdrawing a law takes a moral stand: Shanmugam called repealing s 377A the right thing to do. And Devlin himself keeps apart two questions: whether society may pass a moral judgement, and whether the law should enforce it in a given case. The second is where reasons and prudence do their work.

Section 2: "not sceptical"

The statement is right to rule out the sceptical objection, because it undoes itself (block E2.2). If all moral opinions are equally valid, then the demand that legislators tolerate them is one more opinion, with no claim on anyone. A relativist who wants to bind outsiders needs a standard above all cultures, and if that standard honours equal moral worth, TSH says, he is really a moral realist. Disagreement no more shows there is nothing to be right about than disputes about the planets show there are no planets. And "we have been wrong before" presupposes a right answer that was missed. The sceptic's real insight is fallibility, which favours deliberation, slowness and graded coercion, not abstention. Concede that this shows only that the case for subjectivism is "inconclusive"; each coercive law still needs its own reasons.

Then the kit on equal validity (E2.1). Hart rejects it: his whole question presupposes that moral opinions can be better or worse, since a society's shared feeling, a racist one for example, can be tested and found to be mere prejudice. Rawls is no sceptic either: no doctrine is privileged in advance, but unreasonable doctrines are then set aside. "Unreasonable" means refusing fair terms of cooperation, not being false. He brackets moral truth for political purposes; he does not deny it.

Close: the sceptic's demand for tolerance is itself a moral claim, and scepticism does not even favour tolerance. On Devlin's relativist footing, each society's morality is equally entitled to enforcement, so equal validity leads to enforcing majority morality as easily as to tolerance.

Section 3: "sceptical", the second sense

Here is the essay's own contribution. "Sceptical" has two senses. Scepticism about whether there is any moral truth defeats itself. Doubt about which moral claims are true does not. This is TSH's second kind of sceptic, who doubts that an objective standard can be known, and the doubt is legitimate. Finnis's goods are accessible but not accepted by everyone. And in 2007 both camps in the debate were sure they were right.

But this doubt works through prudence, which the statement allows. It counsels a manner of enforcing, not abstention: slowness and privacy (Devlin), reasons beyond feeling (Hart), reasons others could accept (Rawls), and coercion graded to the strength of the reasons (the ladder in E1.2). Concede that this doubt has no end point. No legislator can ever be sure, so the question is always how much coercion uncertain reasons can bear.

Add one case point: in 2007 the feedback was "emotional, divided", with a majority for keeping s 377A (Ho Peng Kee), and the police would not proactively enforce it. Majority feeling prevailed, tempered by Devlin's slowness and privacy.

Form (b): "the legislature may enforce the morality of society"

The intro is different: the statement is right on both counts but leaves the link unexplained. The majority settles who decides, not what is right. The legislature may enforce society's morality because it is the forum, but majority reasons are not thereby correct, so it must decide on reasons it can defend to the minority.

Section 1 here is "however the majority feels": majority feeling is evidence the legislature must weigh, not an instruction it must follow. Use the majority kit (E2.3) for Devlin and Rawls: Devlin refuses to count heads, and Rawls asks more than a majority and less than unanimity. Then block E2.4 from its Devlin sentence: Finnis condemns leaving the gravest questions to sheer numbers, and TSH's dilemma shows why. If there is a right to the conduct, following the majority is oppression; if not, it is democracy. So the moral question returns either way. Majority opinion may indicate objective morality, but majorities have been wrong before. So the legislator judges as a trustee, not a stenographer. When a referendum was sought in 2022, the reply was that MPs must decide. Close: even the Attorney-General, defending s 377A in Lim Meng Suang, relied on Devlin's public morality as opposed to popular morality, so the legislature may decide against the majority's feeling.

Section 2 is "the legislature may decide": it is the right forum, because every law enforces some morality and someone must choose which. Close: Lim Meng Suang left the Hart and Devlin debate to Parliament because judges are no "mini-legislature", not because Parliament's answer would be right. If asked for local cases, Tan Seng Kee (2022) found no standing, because s 377A was unenforceable until the Attorney-General gave clear notice, so the 2007 promise of non-enforcement gained legal force. Minority protection then depends on legislators' reasons.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

For form (a), the strongest opposing case is liberty and humility (E1.6), opened as "the best case against": political power belongs to free and equal citizens together, so imposing one faction's view of the good treats dissenters as subjects. It shows the state must not make one doctrine official, not that it may enforce no morality.

For form (b), the best case is democratic (E2.5): majority rule treats citizens as equals amid disagreement, the legislature is the proper forum, and TSH fears a juristocracy. It shows the legislature may decide moral questions, not that majority feeling makes its decision right.

My weak point for form (a): my answer rests on a line between denying moral truth and doubting who knows it, and in practice the two can look alike. A legislator unsure of every moral claim acts as a sceptic would. I concede the overlap in outcome. But the line still matters: the doubter can be asked for reasons and shown wrong, while the sceptic has no reasons to give. For form (b), the weak point is E2.6: demanding public reasons is itself a contested moral position, which only confirms that every answer takes a moral stand.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is largely right; its one fault is to treat every scepticism alike. The four agree that the limits are not sceptical, and split on what disciplines enforcement: harm and critical morality (Hart), the limits of tolerance (Devlin), reasons others could accept (Rawls), or the basic goods and prudence (Finnis). Repaired: “Enforcing morality is legitimate when its reasons are accessible and sound and its coercion proportionate; scepticism is no objection, but fallibility is a reason for restraint.” For the legislator, this means a manner of enforcing, not abstention. For form (b): "The majority decides, but must decide on reasons it can defend to the minority."

Using it in the hypo

Sin is not crime. Yaacob Ibrahim (2005) noted that drinking and other activities Muslims consider vices are not banned, and Pritam Singh (2022) said that in a secular society sin and crime are separate. A poll in which 78% "disapprove" does not show that they want the conduct made a crime. The harms the MP cites, debt and crime, are real, but they support licensing, limits and help for gamblers, not jail for the patron. If asked for a current controversy, use the casinos (is gambling a form of play, E45.4).

Memory line

Denying moral truth defeats itself; doubting who knows it counsels care, not silence.

E4

Majority, neutrality and whose morality

■ highly likely

The question FQ1 · 2018-19 Q1 · 2012-13 Q1

"Legislators should follow majority opinion on moral questions. Otherwise, they merely impose their own subjective views." Or: "Enforcing the moral judgements of certain groups must be avoided, lest minority rights be trampled." Or: "A pluralist democracy should not endorse moral values, so legislators should not try to resolve intractable moral conflicts."

The hypo Hypo 7

A bill reserves housing grants, baby bonuses and tax reliefs for married parents, and makes every school teach that children are best raised by a married mother and father. Grounds: the main faiths hold marriage sacred; children of married parents do better on average; the Constitution invites Parliament to promote marriage. An opposition MP wants a referendum instead. How would you vote on the bill and on the referendum?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

Each form tells the legislator whose morality to follow: the majority's, nobody's, or never a group's. The flaw is a false dilemma. "Otherwise" hides a third option. "Lest" protects minorities with a rule that would disarm them. "Should not resolve" slides from not endorsing values to not deciding at all, and leaving the law in place is itself a decision.

The answer in one breath

For the "otherwise" form: the statement offers two sources, majority opinion or the legislator's own subjective views. By majority opinion I mean what most citizens now think right, as a poll would find it. By subjective I mean a view for which no reason can be given that others could examine. Each theorist supplies a third source, so the dilemma is false. If the statement speaks of free and equal persons, it borrows Rawls's premise and draws a conclusion he rejects: free and equal citizens are owed reasons they could reasonably accept, which a head-count does not supply. The majority settles who decides, not what is right.

The other intros, in short. "Lest minorities suffer": every law enforces some group's moral judgement, and the belief that minority rights deserve protection is one, so the rule cannot be obeyed; the real danger is enforcing a group's morality on reasons the minority cannot share, or with too much coercion. "Decline to decide": declining to resolve a conflict leaves the existing law in place, which is itself a resolution, and every society takes a public stand (Finnis).

Section 1: "majority opinion"

The majority's view is evidence, not a reason (kit E2.3). Devlin, its natural ally, does not count heads: his test is twelve people after discussion. Rawls asks for reasons all citizens may reasonably be expected to endorse.

Close: in 2007 the 70% who "frowned on" homosexuality were never asked whether it should be a crime (Hri Kumar), so even the head-count did not answer the legislator's question. And a head-count binds only because each person counted has equal dignity, which is a moral premise, not a number. That point is from the lectures.

Section 2: "subjective views"

A legislator's view is not subjective just because he holds it (kit E2.1). Hart's question is one of critical morality: a society's consensus can be tested and found wanting. Finnis's basic goods are self-evident and grasped, not inferred, but have no fixed ranking, so people may rank them differently within a range of reasonable answers.

If the statement says "otherwise", use the third-option block (E4.2) here. All four theorists supply a third source. Devlin's law-maker asks what twelve people at random would, after discussion, accept unanimously: the weakest of the four, since deliberated feeling can still be prejudice, but not a head-count. Hart's law-maker tests every opinion, the majority's and his own, by critical morality: harm, defective consent, public offence. Rawls's votes on political values others might reasonably accept. Finnis's reasons from goods accessible to all, whatever their religion. Concede that the legislator's own judgement must apply each test, which is why he must give his reasons in public, as a trustee, not a stenographer.

Close: a view is subjective only if no reason can be given for it. Rawls asks officials to explain their positions by the political conception they think most reasonable, and citizens to vote as if they were legislators. A view given such reasons is no longer the legislator's taste.

Section 3: "neutral" or "no group"

Refusing every group's morality is itself a moral stand. Use the neutrality kit (E1.5) for Rawls and Devlin: Rawls's neutrality is of justification, not outcome, of aim and not of effect; Devlin says law sets the minimum of morality, not the maximum, and protects the "invisible bonds" of common thought.

Then the key block: permitting decides too (E4.1). No legislative stance leaves a moral question unanswered. The sheet's example: moving the 1969 Abortion Bill, Chua said learned men could not agree whether the foetus is human life, and then declared that an early conception is no human being, so permission settled the very question he said no one could answer (TSH). TSH's hunter, unsure what moves behind a bush, should not fire: uncertainty is a reason for care, not a licence to stand aside. Repeal is no escape: calling a repeal the right thing to do is a moral verdict. Every society takes a public stand (Finnis), and even Rawls concedes there may be no way to avoid implying that a doctrine is untrue. So neutrality cannot be met. What can be met is narrower: each stand must rest on reasons every citizen can examine.

Close: minority protection is a moral judgement, not an escape from one. TSH's dilemma again: if there is a right to the conduct, following the majority is oppression; if not, it is democracy.

If the statement runs the other way

Sometimes the statement says: "No law is neutral, so the legislature may enact the morality it or the majority believes true." Then the intro says the statement is right that no law on a moral question is neutral, and wrong about what follows. That every law takes a stand does not show which stand, on whose say-so, or with how much coercion. Even Finnis, who holds that the good can be known, keeps the state to peace and justice. So the legislature may take a moral stand, but must give reasons the minority can examine and grade its coercion to them. Concede with E4.1, then use E2.4 (who decides is not what is right), the ladder (E1.2), Finnis as liberal in means (E1.5), and the last sentence of E1.7.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is democratic (E2.5): majority rule treats citizens as equals amid disagreement, and the legislature is the proper forum. For a neutrality statement, use liberty and humility (E1.6) instead. Either shows the legislature may decide; neither shows that a head-count or neutrality settles what it should decide.

My weak point (E2.6): requiring reasons beyond majority feeling is itself contested. I concede it, which confirms that every answer takes a moral stand.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right that neither a head-count nor the legislator's taste can settle the law, and wrong that nothing else is left. Every theory supplies a third standard: reasons every citizen can examine. All four reject the head-count, even Devlin, its natural ally. They split on what the third standard is: deliberated feeling (Devlin), critical morality (Hart), public reason (Rawls) or the basic goods (Finnis). Repaired: “The majority decides, but must decide on reasons it can defend to the minority.” For the legislator, this means judging as a trustee, not a stenographer. For the "lest", "decline to decide" and "no law is neutral" forms: "The legislature may take a moral stand, but only on reasons the minority can examine, with coercion graded to those reasons."

If the task asks what the theorists would say, give each a verdict on both sentences, then what the four share and where they split. Watch: E2.4 and E4.2 both restate the majority kit, so never write both in full.

Using it in the hypo

On the call for a referendum, a head-count is not a reason, and MPs must decide (Masagos). The bill is low on the ladder, since it promotes rather than punishes, which counts in its favour. But its school mandate reaches religious schools and the children of single parents, so its reasons must be ones those families can examine and answer, not simply the majority's view.

Memory line

Not the crowd, not my taste: reasons anyone can examine. Not deciding is a decision.

How these essays fit together

E2 asks whether law may enforce morality and what kind of objection counts. E4 asks whose morality the legislator follows. They share the majority kit (E2.3), the equal-validity kit (E2.1) and the democratic best case (E2.5). E2's distinctive move is the two senses of "sceptical"; E4's is the third option (E4.2) and "permitting decides too" (E4.1). If both are on one paper, give E2 the scepticism material and E4 the third option, and write the trustee line once.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • E2 is largely right; its one fault is treating every scepticism alike.
  • Denying moral truth defeats itself (the planets; "wrong before" presupposes a right answer).
  • Doubting who knows it is TSH's second sceptic: a real reason for restraint, working through prudence.
  • Form (b): the majority settles who decides, not what is right; trustee, not stenographer.
  • E4: "otherwise" hides a third option, and each theorist supplies one.
  • A view is subjective only if no reason can be given for it.
  • Permitting decides too; not deciding is a decision.
  • Repaired: the majority decides, on reasons it can defend to the minority.

Video 3Is morality just opinion: scepticism and the liberal chainLaw-making

E9

Moral scepticism as its own topic

The question exam-style

"Morality is just a matter of opinion; nobody can prove one moral view better than another. The law should therefore tolerate every choice and stay out of moral questions." (Or: a social construct; unprovable.)

The hypo Hypo 14

A Law Reform Commission debates commercial surrogacy. A: "Morality is just opinion, like durian. Who are we to judge?" B: "Nobody agrees, and we were wrong about slavery, so stay neutral." C: "Hume shows the studies on children tell us nothing." D: "Maximise happiness; human nature drives people to want children." G: "It is consensual and harms no one, so it is not wrong." Evaluate each.

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

The premise is about morality: there is no moral truth, or none we can know. The conclusion is a demand: tolerate, stay neutral, keep morality out of law. But that demand is itself a moral claim, of exactly the kind the premise says cannot be true. So the statement takes the stand it forbids. That is the self-refuting flaw.

The answer in one breath

By moral truth I mean that moral facts hold whatever anyone believes or feels, so moral claims can be true or false, and where two people disagree, one of them is wrong (TSH). The sceptic denies this, or denies that we can know which. The statement fails: disagreement and unprovability do not show that there is no moral truth, and believing in moral truth demands deliberation, not imposition.

Section 1: "just opinion"

The statement runs several different doubts together (block E9.1). Map them:

  • Relativism: right and wrong vary by culture or situation.
  • Scepticism: there is no standard, or none we can know.
  • Non-cognitivism: moral claims are mere sentiment. On Hume's view, saying something is wrong is like saying you dislike durian. It is neither true nor false.
  • The claim that morals are an ideological tool, a point from the lectures.

All are forms of subjectivism, though not every subjectivist is a non-cognitivist: some hold moral opinions true or false for each individual. And "nobody has proved it" bites only if proof means strict deduction, a test natural science fails too. Two traps from the lectures: subjective does not mean controversial (the sun-centred solar system was once controversial, and never subjective); and moral realism has nothing to do with legal realism.

Then pick the one reply from the card of stock objections (E9.2) that matches the statement's premise:

  • "A social construct": there are two versions. If we are not bound by it, that is true scepticism, consistent but unable to argue for anything. If we are bound by consensus, the reason we are bound, each person's dignity or fairness, is a realist premise. And whose construct? In a plural society, a culture's norm is usually the majority's, which ignores minority cultures (TSH).
  • "It cannot be proved": the unproven is not the false.
  • "Wrong for me, not really wrong": on what ground "really"? A stick in water looks bent, yet we judge it straight because we know about refraction: our judgement corrects the first impression. A moral view reached by argument, as the subjectivist's own view is, claims that kind of correction, so it is odd to deny it tracks anything real (TSH).
  • "Nobody can agree": disagreement shows that someone is wrong, not that there is nothing to be right about. TSH explains why people disagree: the mores of the times pervert reason, weakness yields to passion, and self-interest hardened by habit dulls conscience. Beneath the disagreement there is also a core of agreement.

Close: none of these doubts shows that moral claims cannot be true or false.

Section 2: "therefore tolerate"

The sceptic's conclusion is a moral demand of the kind the premise denies (block E2.2). If all moral opinions are equally valid, the demand to tolerate them is one more opinion with no claim on anyone. A relativist who binds outsiders needs a standard above all cultures, and if it honours equal moral worth, he is a realist (TSH). Disagreement no more shows there is nothing to be right about than disputes about the planets do. "We have been wrong before" presupposes a right answer that was missed. The sceptic's real insight is fallibility, which favours deliberation, slowness and graded coercion.

Hart and Rawls are no sceptics (kit E2.1). Hart's question presupposes that moral opinions can be better or worse. Rawls brackets moral truth for political purposes; he does not deny it.

Close: the statement defeats itself, though that settles no particular moral question. Nor does suspending judgement escape: refusing to act on a judgement that something is wrong is acting as if it were not wrong (TSH), so neutrality takes a side too. Even Devlin, a relativist, draws no tolerance from it: on his footing each society's morality is equally entitled to enforcement.

Section 3: facts and nature

Hume makes two arguments (block E9.3). First, wrongness lies in our sentiments, not in the act, since reason knows only relations of ideas and matters of fact. But that claim is itself neither, so arguably, by its own test, it is neither true nor false. Second, no "ought" follows from an "is". That gap holds, but it is only logical: agreed ought-premises, such as the dignity of man, plus facts do yield oughts. So "facts tell us nothing" overstates. Hume himself was no extreme relativist: he held the rules of justice artificial but not arbitrary.

Nor does "following nature" mean obeying urges. The Stoics saw man as having natural ends known by the reflective intellect, though Finnis concedes they drew norms from nature and so confused fact and value. Finnis infers nothing from facts or urges: the basic goods are self-evident and grasped, and facts show only how they apply.

Close: facts fix the stakes once an ought-premise is agreed; they never supply it.

Section 4: if not morals, then what?

Use this only if the statement offers a substitute for morality. The alternatives smuggle moral judgement back in. Utility first (E17.3): Finnis calls the greatest happiness incoherent outside limited technical contexts, because the basic goods cannot be measured on one scale, so utilitarianism is not so much false as senseless. Nozick's experience machine counts against pleasure as the point of it all, since a sensible person would not plug in. Or "law is whatever is enacted" (E37.6): what people made is not what they should make.

Close: counting happiness, or calling law whatever is enacted, each rests on a moral claim. And if law may have any content, some people become the ultimate lawgivers over others, a claim of authority that itself needs a moral defence. That point is from the lectures.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is liberty and humility (E1.6): political power belongs to free and equal citizens together, and we have been wrong before, so the state must not make one comprehensive doctrine official. That is true, but it rests on moral values, not on scepticism.

My weak point: the realist's weak point is access, not existence. Consensus can err, and in 2007 both camps in the debate arguably claimed moral truth. I concede it. Access is a reason for humility about which moral claims are true, not for denying that any are.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement fails. Hart, Rawls, Finnis and TSH reject the sceptic's premise or bracket it, and none rests tolerance on it. They split on what grounds tolerance instead: liberty as a critical value (Hart), reasonable pluralism (Rawls), or the limits of the state's role (Finnis). A sceptic who rests tolerance on autonomy has become a realist after all, and what is left is a dispute among realists about content (TSH). Repaired: “Moral claims can be true or false, but our judgements of them are fallible, so the law should rest on them provisionally, demanding more of them the more coercive it is.” For the legislator, this means caution in coercion, not silence.

Using it in the hypo

Take each commissioner in turn. A ("just opinion, like durian") is a non-cognitivist, and her call to "tolerate" is itself a moral claim. B ("we were wrong before") presupposes that there is a right answer to be wrong about, and her history supports slowness, not abstention. C relies on Hume's gap, which is only logical: agreed moral premises plus facts do yield conclusions. D treats urges as if they were goods and happiness as one scale, which Finnis denies. G's hidden premise is that autonomy comes first, and TSH shows that autonomy's primacy rests on the equal worth of persons, a moral claim.

Memory line

"So tolerate" is a moral demand the sceptic cannot ground; doubt counsels care, not silence.

  2. Hume's argument does not rely on religion at all. The problem is that it fails its own test.

applyCommissioner G: "Surrogacy is consensual and harms no one, so it is not wrong." What is hidden?

key ideaThe statement calls morality "a social construct". What is E9.2's reply?
  1. The reply turns on why a construct would bind, not on comparing it to nature.
  2. That is another sceptic on the map (E9.1), not the reply to this one.

  4. Constructs do change; the reply is about what would make one binding.
Two versions: bound or not bound.
E3

The liberal chain (Sample Q1)

★ very highly likely

The question Sample Q1 · 2025-26 Q1

"Legislators should treat all moral opinions as equally valid. This can be achieved by restricting liberty only when it is objectively necessary to protect others from a wrongdoer. Only then will the law avoid imposing a fixed conception of the good life." Discuss with reference to Rawls, Finnis, Hart and Devlin, with illustrations.

The hypo Hypo 16, MP limb

Men of one minority faith may take up to four wives; everyone else faces seven years for bigamy. An MP's bill allows any three or more consenting adults to register a plural marriage. Grounds: the state has no business preferring one form of family life; plural unions harm no one and studies are inconclusive; the present law is arbitrary. An opponent says monogamy is "built into the house we live in", citing a 74% poll. How would you vote?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

This is a three-link chain. Sentence 1 is a goal: treat all moral opinions as equally valid. Sentence 2 is a means: coerce only to protect others. Sentence 3 is a consequence: only then no fixed conception of the good life. The marker wants you to test each link. Watch the small words: "objectively", "wrongdoer", "only then".

The answer in one breath

The chain is weakest where its links join. It is right that the state must not make one comprehensive doctrine official. It is wrong that a harm-only rule achieves equal validity, because "objectively" and "wrongdoer" presuppose the very moral judgements the goal says to hold equal. And it is wrong that this is the only way to avoid a fixed conception. Pluralism requires equal respect for persons, not equal validity for opinions.

Section 1: the goal, "treat as equally valid"

"Treat as equally valid" has two readings. If it means every opinion is equally true, it defeats itself, as in E9. If it means no doctrine is privileged in advance, Rawls accepts it, but only until unreasonable doctrines are set aside. Finnis allows a range of reasonable answers, not every answer.

Then the kit on equal validity (E2.1), giving Devlin and a sentence of Hart. Devlin is a dangerous ally: he treats a society's morality as a question of fact, honest belief "right or wrong", so on his footing every society's morality is equally entitled to enforcement. Equal validity leads to enforcing the majority's morality as easily as to tolerance. Hart's question presupposes that opinions can be better or worse. Add the first two sentences of E2.2: the demand to tolerate is itself one more opinion.

Close: what survives is fallibility, which counsels caution in coercion, not silence. The fear behind the goal is intolerance, but calling a view merely subjective also cuts off debate, and a legislator who acts on equal validity acts on his own view too. In 2007 both camps arguably claimed to be absolutely right: the task was to manage rival certainties, not to declare all opinions equal.

Section 2: the means, "only to protect others"

Even granting the goal, the means does not achieve it, and it is not neutral. Use the harm kit (E1.3). Hart: "only harm to others" is too narrow, though bare moral condemnation never suffices; he accepts paternalism where consent is defective, and public decency. Devlin, briefly: there are no theoretical limits to the law's power against immorality. Rawls last, as the bridge: he tests the kind of reason, not the kind of conduct.

Close: Rawls, the statement's natural ally, files the harm principle among comprehensive doctrines, so the means imposes Mill's ideal of life. Nor can a harm-only rule explain why consent is no defence to the gravest offences against the person, which even Hart explains by paternalism. And paternalism brings morals back: the law protects people from some risks to themselves and not others, dangerous sports far less than removing a healthy limb, and that choice is moral (TSH). Singapore point: in 2022 the Government said a crime generally needs a public order or public interest issue, "broadly similar" to the Wolfenden approach.

Section 3: the consequence, "only then … fixed conception"

Avoiding a fixed conception is a sound hope; it is the "only then" that fails. Devlin rejects the hope itself: a common morality is part of "the price of society", so some fixed conception is its condition, not its danger.

Use the neutrality kit for Finnis (E1.5): he is non-neutral in ends but liberal in means. Every society takes a public stand, yet the state keeps only peace and justice, and its common good is a set of conditions, not a life-plan.

Then "fixed is a matter of degree" (block E3.1). If "fixed" means one complete life-plan, no theorist here imposes one, not even Devlin. If it means some settled limits on how people may live, every theorist imposes one, even Rawls. Finnis fixes the basic goods and the absolute rules, yet his common good is a set of conditions. Devlin enforces a shared morality, yet asks for the maximum freedom consistent with society's integrity. Hart keeps paternalism and public decency. Rawls excludes unreasonable doctrines. His reply is that a rule made through public reason is no alien imposition, since every reasonable citizen can see its point; yet "live and let live" is itself a conception some citizens reject. So the live question is not whether a conception is fixed, but how much of it, by what reasons, and with how much coercion.

Close: the harm rule is not the only route. Rawls avoids a fixed conception by filtering reasons, not conduct, and Finnis by a state that keeps only peace and justice. Singapore shows the degrees: the 2022 package withdrew the criminal law from private conduct, yet fixed the definition of marriage in Article 156, a settled limit without a complete life-plan.

If the means is a right to decide

Some versions put a right to one's own destiny in sentence 2. Then run the right-to-decide kit (E1.1, Hart and Finnis) with section 2 and section 3, or with section 1 and section 3. Drop a section; never move one.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is liberty and humility (E1.6), cut short: political power belongs to free and equal citizens together, so imposing one faction's view of the good treats dissenters as subjects. It shows the state must not make one doctrine official, not that a harm-only rule is required.

My weak point (E1.7): my answer leans on Finnis's goods, which are accessible but not accepted. It would impose his conception only if his goods alone could justify coercion, and they cannot: reasonable disagreement limits what the state may coerce.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right that the state should not make one doctrine official, and wrong that a harm-only rule achieves equal validity or is the only way to avoid a fixed conception. Its means is neither necessary nor neutral. Finnis supplies substance, Rawls discipline and Hart a presumption against coercion. Hart, Rawls and Finnis all reject "only", each for a different reason, and Devlin rejects the goal itself. Repaired: “Legislators should treat all citizens, not all opinions, as equal, and coerce only on reasons every citizen can examine.” For the legislator, this means giving reasons, not claiming neutrality.

Using it in the hypo

"No business preferring" one form of marriage asks for neutrality of outcome, which is impossible, since permitting and refusing both take a side. Rawls says monogamy "as such" is a comprehensive view, but the equality of women is a political reason the state may use. Devlin's "house we live in" is relativist, and it assumes one house when the law already recognises two forms. "Harms no one" raises the question of who counts: wives and children are third parties. Finally, separate the steps: stopping punishment, recognising a union and promoting it are three different moves (E45.10).

Memory line

Equal respect for persons, not equal validity for opinions; "fixed" is a matter of degree.

  1. Not primarily; the joints between links are the target.
  2. It has three linked parts, and "only" appears twice.
  3. Correct. The 2025-26 marker wanted the links tested.
  4. That approach was marked down in 2025-26.
A chain breaks at its joints.

  1. That assumes the very judgement the word smuggles in.
  2. Correct. The harm rule presupposes moral judgements.
  3. Her example shows a harm with no wrongdoer.
  4. Not the point; the word is a moral judgement, not a legal one.

  1. Order is for argument, not importance.
  2. He accepts coercion on public reasons.
  3. He tests reasons, not conduct.
  4. Correct. It would impose Mill's ideal of individuality.

How much, not whether.

How these essays fit together

E9 is the full answer to scepticism; E3 uses it in one paragraph, its first section. Both rely on E2.1 and E2.2. If a paper has a scepticism statement and a liberal-chain statement in different sections, E2.1 and E2.2 belong to one answer only; the other runs on its own blocks (E9.1 to E9.3 for E9, E3.1 and the harm kit for E3). The shared lesson: every route to tolerance, neutrality or a harm-only rule rests on moral values, so the state must give reasons, not claim to have none.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • E9: the premise is about morality; "so tolerate" is a moral demand. It refutes itself.
  • Map the sceptic: relativism, scepticism, non-cognitivism (the durian), ideology.
  • One reply per objection: the planets, the bent stick, "wrong before" presupposes a right answer.
  • Hume's is–ought gap is only logical; facts fix the stakes.
  • E3: a goal, a means, a consequence. Test each link.
  • "Objectively" and "wrongdoer" smuggle in moral judgements; Rawls files the harm principle as comprehensive.
  • "Fixed" is a matter of degree; every theorist fixes some limits.
  • Repaired: treat all citizens, not all opinions, as equal.

Video 4Can law stay neutral: neutrality and moral truthLaw-making

E8

Neutrality alone

▲ likely

The question exam-style

"In a pluralist society, lawmakers should be as neutral as possible between rival views of the good life." Discuss, assessing the soundness of the theories you apply.

The hypo Hypo 17, MP limb

After a video of goats slaughtered without stunning at a minority faith's festival, an MP's bill makes killing any animal without stunning an offence (up to two years), "for any purpose, religious or otherwise"; fishing and pest control are exempt. Grounds: cruelty is wrong whoever inflicts it; 81% felt "disgust"; the bill is "neutral", naming no religion. How would you vote?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

This is one claim run deep. Everything turns on the word "neutral", which has two readings. The marker wants you to separate them, show that one is impossible and the other is possible only in a weak and moral form, and then say what should replace neutrality.

The answer in one breath

The statement asks for something no legislature can deliver in full, and something every legislature should respect in part. By rival views of the good life I mean comprehensive doctrines, religious or secular: conceptions of what is of value, of personal virtue and of character. Neutrality of outcome is impossible, since permitting, punishing and promoting all take a stand. Neutrality of justification is possible only in a weak form, and even that rests on moral values. So lawmakers should be even-handed between citizens, not between opinions.

Section 1: "neutral"

The statement needs neutrality to be possible, and only one kind is (block E8.1). Neutral has two senses.

  • Neutrality of outcome asks that a law leave every way of life equally well placed. No law can. Even the Education Ministry's "secular" sexuality curriculum reflects the national posture on the family, and withdrawing the criminal law rests on a moral judgement about what the state may punish.
  • Neutrality of justification asks only that the reasons for a law presuppose no one comprehensive doctrine. That is Rawls's position: neutrality of aim, in his own terms, not of effect, so some ways of life will fare worse under it. Even that is moral, and liberal neutrality arguably favours doctrines that are already well established in institutions and society. That last point is from the lectures.

So "as neutral as possible" can only mean the second sense, and the honest claim is narrower: make no doctrine official, and coerce on reasons others can examine.

Then add Finnis from the neutrality kit (E1.5). He is non-neutral in ends but liberal in means. Every society takes a public stand, yet the state keeps only peace and justice, and its common good is a set of conditions, not a life-plan. Reason requires favouring the basic goods. And he calls Rawls's "thin theory" of the good arbitrary: denying intrinsic value to truth, play, art and friendship is itself a view of the good.

Close: even the possible kind of neutrality is moral, which Rawls concedes and Finnis makes his starting point.

Section 2: "as possible"

No legislative stance leaves a moral question unanswered (block E4.1). Moving a 1969 bill on the termination of pregnancy, its mover said learned people could not agree on whether early life is human life, and then declared that it was not: permission settled the question, as TSH points out. Her hunter, unsure what moves behind a bush, should hold back: uncertainty is a reason for care, not a licence to stand aside. Repeal is no escape either: calling a repeal the right thing to do is a moral verdict. Every society takes a public stand (Finnis), and even Rawls concedes there may be no way to avoid implying that a doctrine is untrue. Add the last line of the neutrality kit's example: in 2007 there was no "Switzerland of ambivalence".

Close: so "as possible" cannot mean taking no side. The live question is how much of a side, on what reasons, and with how much coercion. That is the "matter of degree" point from E3.

Section 3: what replaces neutrality

What the statement wants is restraint, and restraint has its own tests. First the ladder (E1.2): enforcing can mean punishing, regulating or promoting, and the justification needed rises with the coercion used. Then the third option (E4.2, from Devlin to Finnis): Devlin's law-maker asks what twelve people at random would accept after discussion; Hart's tests every opinion by critical morality; Rawls's votes on political values others might reasonably accept; Finnis's reasons from goods accessible to all, whatever their religion.

Close: Rawls's own replacement for neutrality is a set of virtues, tolerance and readiness to meet others halfway, which he lets outweigh competing values for the sake of social cooperation. That ranking is itself a stand. So grade the coercion to the reasons, and give reasons that are not the legislator's taste.

The soundness limb

If asked to assess the theories, test each one three ways (E18.2): does it hold together, does it answer the hard case, does it beat its rivals on the job? Then rank by job (E10.6). Hart wins against punishing private immorality as such. Devlin lets common feeling, not reason, fix what the law enforces. Rawls sets the right discipline for public argument, but deciding who counts as reasonable already takes a moral side. Finnis gives firm limits and says who counts, but his goods are accessible, not accepted. So: Hart for limiting punishment, Rawls for disciplining reasons, Finnis for who counts.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is liberty and humility (E1.6). Political power belongs to free and equal citizens together, and we enter society only by birth and leave only by death, so imposing one faction's view of the good treats dissenters as subjects, not partners. That shows the state must not make one doctrine official. It does not show the state can take no side.

My weak point: either E1.7 (my answer leans on Finnis, whose goods are accessible but not accepted; reasonable disagreement limits what the state may coerce, even where he is right) or E2.6 (demanding reasons beyond majority feeling is itself a contested moral position, which confirms that every answer takes a stand).

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right in aim and wrong in name. No theorist here delivers neutrality: Hart's liberty is a critical morality, Rawls's public reason is political yet moral, Finnis is liberal in means and not in ends, and Devlin does not try. Repaired: “Legislators should treat all citizens, not all opinions, as equal, and coerce only on reasons every citizen can examine.” For the legislator, this means giving reasons, not claiming neutrality, and grading coercion to them.

Watch: this essay is the neutrality kit run deep, so never put it in the same script as E1, E3 or E4.

Using it in the hypo

A ban that "names no religion" is neutral in form, not in effect: it falls on one rite while factory farming and fishing go free. Cruelty is a real ground of harm (for Hart, suffering counts), so the state may regulate slaughter methods. But the exemptions show that the line was drawn by the majority's habits rather than by the harm. Disgust is not a reason either, since even Devlin's own test asks for more. If the bill targets a faith, use E45.11.

Memory line

Neutral in outcome: impossible. Neutral in reasons: possible, but moral. Even-handed between citizens, not opinions.
