Even restraint ranks values.
E13

No actor should act on moral truth

The question 2013-14

"Limited government is rooted in the denial of moral truth, or the suspension of moral judgement. In a constitutional democracy this yields each person's right to decide their own destiny, including bearing a child, intimacy and marriage. Justice is best served, and citizens best protected from totalitarianism, when legislators, judges and lawyers refrain from acting on moral truth." Is this sound and workable?

The hypo Hypo 2

A bill lets a doctor end the life of a terminally ill adult on request, after two opinions and 14 days. Ground three: "the bill takes no side on whether euthanasia is moral, since objectors need not take part". It passes. A son asks you, his father's lawyer, to complete the request quickly; you believe euthanasia is wrong and suspect the father is being pressured. What do you do?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

The statement makes three moves: a premise about where limited government comes from, a right that follows in a constitutional state, and a rule for every legal actor, with a pay-off. "Sound" asks whether the premise holds. "Workable" asks whether each actor could follow the rule. The flaw is self-refutation: bracketing moral truth, and the right to define one's own life, are themselves moral commitments.

The answer in one breath

The statement is right that a limited state must not make one comprehensive doctrine official. By moral truth it means right answers to moral questions; by refraining from acting on it, deciding as if there were none. But bracketing moral truth and the right to shape one's own life are themselves moral commitments, so the premise is unsound as stated. And the rule is unworkable: legislators, judges and lawyers can discipline their moral judgement, but they cannot refrain from it.

Section 1: "rooted in denial or suspension"

Sound means the premise holds; workable means each actor could follow the rule. The premise splits in two. As denial of moral truth, it defeats itself: the demand to limit government is a moral demand. As suspension, it is Rawls's bracketing. That is coherent, but it is confined to constitutional essentials, and it rests on moral values: reciprocity, and citizens as free and equal. And limited government can rest on moral truth: Finnis's state keeps only peace and justice.

Section 2: "constitutional supremacy"

Not running citizens' lives is common ground: no theorist here wants the state to impose a life-plan, so the dispute is over what grounds the limit. Use the right-to-decide kit (E1.1) for Finnis and Hart. Finnis: appealing to subjective preference and then grounding it in the value of self-governance is "outright self-contradiction". Hart: a strong presumption, not an absolute right.

Then: "as individuals define themselves" grounds the right in an ideal of self-definition, which Rawls counts as comprehensive, not political. And a supreme Constitution is no sign of suspended judgement: Singapore's now takes a stand on marriage, and even its secular school curriculum reflects that posture.

Section 3: legislators, judges and lawyers

Each actor's work requires moral judgement of a different kind, and "refrain" is no escape. Refusing to act on a moral judgement can amount to acting as if its opposite were true (TSH), so a legislator who withholds judgement on whether an act is wrong has permitted it. Take each actor in a paragraph.

  • The legislator: permitting decides too (E4.1). The 1969 Abortion Bill answered the very question its mover, Chua, said no one could answer.
  • The judge: from the judging kit (E20.1), TSH's point. A supreme Constitution relocates the debate. Article 4 is a master rule only because it is accepted, and Part 4's "law", "life", "liberty" and "equal" are contested moral terms.
  • The lawyer: from the lawyering kit (E20.3), Pepper's own limits. Even Pepper's lawyer keeps the choice of client, moral dialogue and conscientious objection, "rarely exercised". Spotting the extreme case means weighing morality throughout.

Close: the legislator judges as a trustee, the judge reads by fit and refuses only intolerable injustice, and the lawyer works above a floor; none can refrain. Even whether to take a client, or how hard to press a half-truth, is a moral choice the law leaves open.

Section 4: "justice" and "totalitarianism"

"Justice is best served" is itself a judgement about moral truth. The fear behind "totalitarianism" is that officials who act on moral truth will impose it and end argument. The card's reply to "realism is intolerant" answers that: imposition is no monopoly of realists; a realist, knowing the truth does not depend on his belief, should hold his judgements provisionally and deliberate more; and calling a view "subjective" also ends the debate.

History runs the other way too. Radbruch said positivism left jurists and people defenceless against a gravely unjust regime. Voiding Ordinance 11, the Nazi decree that stripped citizenship on racist criteria, took a moral judgement. And positivism, TSH says, leaves no check on whoever holds power. Concede Hart's candour: keeping law and morality apart keeps criticism clear.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is liberty and humility (E1.6): political power belongs to free and equal citizens together, so imposing one faction's view of the good treats dissenters as subjects. It shows that no doctrine may be made official; it does not show that officials can act on no morality.

My weak point (E1.7): my answer leans on Finnis's goods, which are accessible but not accepted. Reasonable disagreement limits what the state may coerce even where Finnis is right.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is neither sound nor workable as stated. Rawls, Finnis and Hart agree that a limited state needs moral limits. They split on the source: bracketing on essentials (Rawls), the common good's conditions (Finnis), or a presumption against coercion (Hart). Repaired: “Legislators should treat all citizens, not all opinions, as equal, and coerce only on reasons every citizen can examine; judges and lawyers should discipline their moral judgement by their roles, not abandon it.” For the citizen the statement means to protect, the safeguard against tyranny is reasons she can examine, not officials who claim to have no morality.

Using it in the hypo

"Takes no side" fails, because permitting decides too. The lawyer cannot stay out either: whether the client's consent is genuine is itself the moral issue, and Hart warns about clouded judgement. So begin with dialogue, refuse to speed up a request you believe is coerced, and use the three-scenario test (E20.3) to decide how far to go.

Memory line

Bracketing truth is a moral choice; no actor can refrain, only discipline their judgement.

Sheet blocks to copy from

E1.1E4.1E20.1E20.3E20.5E9.2E20.12E1.6E1.7

  1. The theorists are tools, not the tests.
  2. These are not the essay's tests.
  3. Narrower than what the task asks.
  4. Correct. The statement fails both, for different reasons.
Premise, then practice.
key ideaHow does the premise "denial or suspension of moral truth" split?
  1. The reverse, and Hart does not deny moral truth.
  2. Correct. Denying that there is moral truth is itself a claim to moral truth, so it defeats itself. Suspending it is Rawls's bracketing, which is limited to political questions and is itself a moral position.
  3. Denial is not sound: it defeats itself.

theoristsWhat does Radbruch add in section 4 (justice and totalitarianism)?

  4. He held this before the war and then recanted.

  2. Rawls binds judges most strictly, but each role's judgment differs in kind.
  3. That is the abdication section 3 rejects; each role needs its own kind of judgment.
  4. Correct: none can refrain from moral judgment, and each judges in a different way.
Three roles, three kinds of judgment.

How these essays fit together

E8 is about the legislator alone, and about neutrality. E13 is about every actor, and about moral truth. Both turn on "permitting decides too" (E4.1) and on Rawls's narrow, moral neutrality. E8 leads with the two neutralities (E8.1); E13 leads with the premise's two forms (denial and suspension) and then walks through legislator, judge and lawyer. If a paper offers both kinds of statement, E13's section on judges and lawyers belongs with the judges-and-lawyers answer. The shared idea: no actor can take no side, so each must give reasons and grade the force used to them.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • E8: neutral has two senses. Outcome: impossible. Justification: possible, but moral.
  • Rawls's neutrality is of aim, not of effect. Finnis is non-neutral in ends, liberal in means.
  • "As possible" means how much of a side, on what reasons, with how much coercion.
  • Rawls's replacement for neutrality, tolerance and meeting others halfway, is itself a stand.
  • E13: the premise as denial defeats itself; as suspension it is Rawls's bracketing, which is moral.
  • Refusing to act on a moral judgement is acting as if its opposite were true.
  • Legislator, judge and lawyer each judge morally, in a disciplined way.
  • Repaired: treat all citizens, not all opinions, as equal; discipline judgement, do not abandon it.

Video 5Majority will, and Singapore's 2007 and 2022 decisionsLaw-making

E10

Law as majority will, and the legislator's job

▲ likely

The question 2019-20 Q1

"Law is simply an expression of majoritarian will. The sole concern of legislators, then, is to express that will as accurately as possible."

The hypo Hypo 4, MP limb

After bombings by a group claiming to act for a religious minority, a bill lets the Minister detain anyone at his sole discretion without review, and strips citizenship from members of that faith who do not renounce it within 30 days; a two-thirds amendment shields it from Part 4. Grounds include an 82% poll for "strong measures" and that once passed "the measures will simply be valid law, which no one has any business questioning". How would you vote?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

Sentence 1 says what law is. Sentence 2 draws from it, with "then", what the legislator should do. That slide from "is" to "ought" is the crux. Also watch "simply" and "sole": both are absolutes.

The answer in one breath

By majoritarian will I mean what most citizens want at a given time. By the legislator's sole concern I mean the only reason on which he may vote. The statement slides from what law is to what legislators should do, and "then" carries the whole weight. Majority will settles who may make law, within rules that bind it. That fact settles neither what the law should say nor whose opinion should decide it. So legislators must give reasons and cannot merely record what a society wants. And "as accurately as possible" assumes a settled will that no poll can read.

Section 1: "majoritarian will"

Will makes law only through rules that bind it. Use the kit on law as will (E37.2), with Hart and Fuller, in about 250 words.

  • Hart rejects the claim. Law is more than commands obeyed from habit. Its rules carry an internal "ought", a critical reflective attitude, though not necessarily a moral one. Secondary rules of recognition, change and adjudication cure the uncertainty, stasis and inefficiency of a society with primary rules alone. They say whose will counts and how, so they bind the will that makes law.
  • Fuller rejects it too. The command view confuses fidelity to law with deference to authority. Tracking a shifting majority breaks constancy, like the imaginary King Rex who amended his rules daily, and legal form binds rulers too: Rex's subjects were faithful to him, not to his law, for he made none.

Close: law is more than what the most want. In Singapore, Article 4 voids statutes inconsistent with the Constitution, so the majority's will is law only within the Constitution's rules. And even that supremacy rests on our accepting the Constitution as the master rule, which is a social fact about practice, not a vote. That last point is from the lectures.

The bridge

One sentence: "then" slides from one fact to another. Law's sources are what officials accept; public opinion is what people think right; and neither tells us what the law should be.

Section 2: "sole concern … as accurately as possible"

This is the longest section. Even the theorist most willing to follow popular morality does not count heads. "As accurately as possible" assumes a settled majority will waiting to be transcribed, and there is none.

Use the majority kit (E2.3), Devlin and Rawls, then Finnis.

  • Devlin's test is deliberated, not counted: what twelve people at random would, after discussion, accept unanimously. A majority's dislike is "not nearly enough". And he wants the law to lag behind opinion, not track it: the limits of tolerance shift, so in a new matter of morals the law should be slow to act.
  • Rawls: legitimacy needs more than a majority and less than unanimity. He asks citizens to vote as if they were legislators, asking which statutes, on reasons that meet reciprocity, it is most reasonable to enact.
  • Finnis attacks any principle that leaves the gravest questions to sheer numbers or influence.

Then TSH's dilemma (from E2.4): if there is a right to the conduct, following the majority is oppression; if not, it is democracy. So the moral question returns either way. In 2007 the 70% who "frowned on" homosexuality were never asked whether it should be a crime. Majority opinion may indicate objective morality, but majorities have been badly wrong before.

Close: Hri Kumar drew the lesson in the same 2007 debate: laws should be made not because the majority wants them, but because they make sense for Singapore as a whole. The legislator judges as a trustee, not a stenographer.

The soundness limb

If asked whether the theories are sound, rank the four law-making theories by job (E10.6): Hart for limiting punishment, Rawls for disciplining reasons, Finnis for who counts and what consent cannot cure; Devlin shows what follows when feeling sets the limit. For Hart and Fuller in section 1, use their lines from the soundness card (E22.3). Hart: if obeying from fear counted as acceptance he would come close to Austin, but what counts is using the rule as a standard. Fuller: is legality morality or mere efficacy? It respects responsible agents and rests on reciprocity, but cannot make laws just.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is democratic (E2.5): majority rule treats citizens as equals amid disagreement, the legislature is the proper forum, and TSH fears rule by judges. It shows the legislature may decide moral questions. It does not show that majority feeling makes its decision right.

My weak point (E10.5): I use consensus as evidence of moral truth, and consensus can mislead. I concede it. I treat consensus as a gauge, not a ground: it can slow the legislator, but it never settles the question.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right about who makes law and wrong about what follows. Hart, Fuller, Devlin and Rawls agree that law is made through rules and reasons, not raw will. They split on what disciplines the will: critical morality (Hart), legality (Fuller), deliberated feeling (Devlin) or public reason (Rawls). Repaired: “Majority will settles who may make law, within rules that bind it; it does not settle what the law should say.” For the legislator, this means giving reasons the minority can examine.

Using it in the hypo

"Strong measures" does not mean "these measures": the mandate supports action, not every possible law. "Simply valid law" confuses validity with justice, and Hart says validity never settles whether a law should be obeyed. Part (b) targets a faith, so use E45.11 and the deliberate betrayal of equality in E43.6. Vote against both parts.

Memory line

Will makes law only through rules; "then" slides from is to ought; trustee, not stenographer.

E12

The 2007 compromise and the 2022 package

The question exam-style

"Singapore's decision in 2007 to keep s 377A but not enforce it, and its 2022 decision to repeal it while amending the Constitution to protect marriage, show that majority feeling decides moral questions here." (Variants: the harm principle won; the state is now neutral; Parliament, not the courts, should decide. Or: assess the 2022 package by the theorists.)

The hypo Hypo 3, judge limb, and Hypo 7

Ruritania passes the adultery offence with an announcement that police will not proactively enforce it. Three years later the Public Prosecutor charges a man after his wife complains. As the judge, would you convict? Then Parliament repeals the offence and passes Hypo 7's marriage-promotion bill: what does the pair of decisions show?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

The statement reads Singapore's two decisions as a victory for one theory. Neither was. The marker wants the facts used precisely and each theorist applied to them, then the lesson drawn: coercion graded to reasons, not neutrality.

The answer in one breath

In 2007 majority feeling prevailed, tempered by Devlin's own safeguards of slowness and privacy. In 2022 the criminal law withdrew on a ground close to Wolfenden's, and marriage was promoted by constitutional amendment. So the package shows the coercion ladder at work, not neutrality: punishment withdrawn, promotion kept, and the question left with Parliament.

Section 1: 2007

The 2007 result followed majority feeling; its reasons did not. Repeal was argued from rights (private acts harming no one, discrimination against a minority, a ground open only to religion) and from the nature of law (unclear, inconsistent with leaving adultery alone, hard to enforce). Retention answered that harm is not the only ground, that rights claimed for the private sphere would spill into marriage and adoption, and that majority opinion can be an indication of objective morality.

Use the 2007 block (E12.1). Proponents of retention largely avoided Devlin's treason analogy and his disintegration thesis. So "majority feeling decides" describes the 2007 result, not its reasons. The debate had moved from toleration and privacy to rights, and whether retention deferred to the majority or protected what was right was, for TSH, a "point of equivocation". The Government called its approach pragmatic, preserving social cohesion. Yet pragmatism is itself a theory, and TSH finds a second equivocation: majority opinion may have been invoked from pragmatism, to avoid talk of right and wrong, or from the democratic principle of representation. Writing on the Constitution, she adds that the pragmatist's view, that law is whatever a court says, "glosses over" the principles that should guide it. Both camps invoked social cohesion, yet neither argument was really about it: each turned on whether the acts are wrong, a question that cannot be shunned.

Add the facts (E2.7, first point): feedback was "emotional, divided", with a majority for retention, and the police would not proactively enforce the offence.

Close: retention was also defended for what it signals: "The effectiveness of s377A is seen in what it prevents beyond the act criminalized" (de Souza). That turns Hart's point on its head: Hart warned that a law constrains even those it never punishes, and here that was the aim. Non-enforcement had costs of its own. It breached Fuller's congruence between rules as announced and as applied, and left "formidable discriminatory powers" with police and prosecutors (Hart).

Section 2: 2022

Repeal was a moral verdict, not an abstention. Parliament moved because the Law Minister saw a "significant legal risk" that the courts would strike s 377A down, and a court's ruling would be "binary, zero sum", made without weighing what follows for marriage.

Then the facts (E1.9). s 377A was repealed, in force on 3 January 2023. The Government said a crime generally needs a public order or public interest issue, "broadly similar" to Wolfenden; non-consensual acts, acts involving the young and public indecency stay crimes. Article 156 shields the man–woman definition of marriage from challenge under Part 4, which TSH calls a pre-emptive strike against judicial activism. Yet the definition itself is not entrenched, so a simple majority may change it. TSH: Article 156 blocks the inference that repeal made public morality neutral.

Then the theorists, one by one (card E12.2, which applies them; that is the lectures' and my own application):

  • Mill: arguably not enough, since a safeguard was added because the majority was not ready.
  • Hart: his lines survive, and only private consensual adult conduct is freed. He says little on promotion.
  • Devlin: by 2022 tolerance had shifted, which on his own test removes the case for the offence; Article 156 protects the institution he thought built into the house we live in.
  • Rawls: on balance approves. The consultation was the background culture; consensus reached "not criminal", not full equality; and sending a divisive question to Parliament, not unelected judges, is a public reason. Against: his own model of public reason is a supreme court, and appeals to monogamy "as such" reflect comprehensive doctrines.
  • Finnis: likely approves, since Article 156 promotes marriage and the common good.

Close: so the package is no one theory's victory. Those who saw nothing wrong in the acts, those who thought them wrong but not the state's business, and those who feared only the slope, which Article 156 met, could all accept "not criminal". Only those who wanted the acts punished for their immorality were left out.

Section 3: what it shows

The package grades coercion to reasons. Use the ladder (E1.2): punishment came down, promotion stayed. Then "repeal is no escape" (from E4.1): calling the repeal the right thing to do is a moral verdict.

Then the question after repeal (E12.3): what follows from privacy? If intimacy is a substantive right, do other rights follow: recognition, marriage, adoption? This is the principled slope, the one reading of Devlin's disintegration that bites. Article 156 answers it in advance: Part 4 cannot void laws or executive acts based on the man–woman definition of marriage, which stays in statute. So the shield covers laws based on that definition, not every law touching marriage. Repeal was for risk, not because the offence was unconstitutional, so laws that differentiate are not incoherent in principle (TSH). Her threshold agrees: opposite-sex marriage laws are not intolerably unjust by broad historical consensus, though she admits the "catch" that a worldwide trend could one day be imposed.

Close: the package moved the question to Parliament; it did not remove it. If asked about the courts, add Lim Meng Suang (2014): the question is "quintessentially" for Parliament, and judges are not a "mini-legislature"; and Tan Seng Kee (2022): the 2007 non-enforcement promise gained legal force.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is democratic (E2.5): the legislature is the proper forum, as Lim Meng Suang said. It shows Parliament may decide; it does not show majority feeling made the decision right.

My weak point (E10.5): I treat consensus as evidence, and consensus can mislead. I treat it as a gauge, not a ground.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is too simple. No answer, repeal included, is neutral, and the Hart and Devlin debate is a moral quarrel, not a positivist one. Hart, Devlin, Rawls and Finnis can each approve part of the package for a different reason, and agreement in result is not agreement in reasons (G1.1: same result, different reasons). Repaired: “The majority decides, but must decide on reasons it can defend to the minority.” For the legislator, this means grading coercion to reasons: punishing only what public reasons support, and promoting openly what it values.

Using it in the hypo

Tan Seng Kee matters here: an announced policy of non-enforcement can gain legal force, so a prosecution brought without clear notice is open to challenge. Keeping a law while promising not to enforce it also strains Fuller's congruence between the rule and official action (E44.6). On the pair of bills, follow the 2022 model: withdraw punishment and keep promotion.

Memory line

2007: majority feeling with Devlin's brakes. 2022: punishment down, promotion up, the question left with Parliament.

  3. There was no referendum; Masagos said MPs must decide.
  4. No court struck it down; Tan Seng Kee left it unenforceable until the AG gave notice.
Risk, not a ruling.

How these essays fit together

E10 is the theory: majority will makes law only through rules, and the legislator is a trustee. E12 is the Singapore case study that tests it. In 2007 the majority's feeling won the result but not the argument; in 2022 Parliament acted against legal risk, on reasons close to Hart's, and promoted marriage. Both use the majority kit (E2.3) and TSH's dilemma. If both are on one paper, give E10 the theory (Hart and Fuller on will) and E12 the facts and the theorist-by-theorist card, and write the trustee line once.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • E10: "then" slides from is to ought. Will makes law only through rules that bind it.
  • Article 4: the majority's will is law only within the Constitution.
  • "As accurately as possible" assumes a settled will no poll can read. Devlin wants the law slow, not tracking opinion.
  • Rank the four theories by job.
  • E12: 2007 was majority feeling with Devlin's slowness and privacy, but argued from rights; a "point of equivocation".
  • Non-enforcement breached Fuller's congruence and left discretionary powers with police (Hart).
  • 2022: repeal for legal risk; a public order or public interest test; Article 156 against the slope.
  • Same result, different reasons. Repaired: the majority decides, on reasons it can defend to the minority.

Video 6Faith in law-making, and the Ruritania billLaw-making

E11

Religious arguments in law-making

▲ likely

The question exam-style

"In a multi-religious society, religious arguments have no place in law-making; laws should rest on secular reasons alone." (Or the reverse: "religious arguments are as good as any other".)

The hypo Hypo 22

On a free vote to lift a ban on embryo research: MP Tan says "my scripture forbids this, and that is the only reason I need"; MP Devi says it treats a human life merely as a means, a view held because of her faith but not citing it; an archbishop tells the faithful to vote out MPs who support it; a judge quotes scripture as one reason. The Minister's Public Discourse Bill would restrain religious leaders who "mix religion and politics", unreviewable, and make MPs certify "secular reasons only". Which offend public reason? How would you vote on the bill?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

The statement treats religious argument as a kind of argument that can be sorted out and set aside. The flaw is the absolute: "no place". The marker wants you to show that no theorist sorts reasons that way, and then say what a faith can and cannot do in law-making.

The answer in one breath

By a religious argument I mean a reason whose force depends on accepting a faith's doctrine, not any reason a believer happens to hold. No theorist here sorts reasons by religion. Rawls admits religious reasons if public ones follow. Finnis and Tan Seow Hon test every argument by its merit. Even Devlin asks only that the law not rest on doctrines citizens may disbelieve. So a faith may give a legislator a reason to speak; it cannot alone justify coercing those outside it.

Section 1: "no place"

Rawls's public reason is the statement's best support, and it binds only those who wield or seek coercive power, on constitutional essentials. Explain who it binds (block E16.2). It binds those who exercise coercive power in the public forum: judges most strictly, then officials, above all legislators, then candidates, and citizens when they vote as if they were legislators. It covers constitutional essentials and matters of basic justice. It does not bind churches, universities, other associations or the media: that is the "background culture", which is unrestricted. A doctrine of no salvation outside the church may be true; it is unreasonable only if it would use the public's political power to enforce that. Civility is a moral duty to justify, not a legal one, since a legal duty would curb free speech. No argument is barred for being religious: the Good Samaritan may be cited if public reasons follow "in due course". Secular comprehensive doctrines are limited in the same way, so public reason is not secular reason.

Then the Singapore point on sin and crime (E2.8): in 2005 Yaacob Ibrahim observed that drinking and other activities Muslims consider vices are not banned, and in 2022 Pritam Singh said that in a secular society sin and crime are separate.

Close: the statement asks for more exclusion than the theory most hospitable to it.

Section 2: "secular reasons only"

Sorting reasons by motive goes further than Rawls does. Use the sorting block (E11.1). Rawls sorts reasons by kind, Chua, moving the 1969 Abortion Bill, sorted them by motive, and Finnis and TSH sort them by merit.

  • Rawls treats secular comprehensive arguments "the same way" as religious ones, so a Kantian's absolute ban on lying is barred too, yet he admits Catholic common-good views when they are put in political values. His own example is whether to criminalise private conduct between consenting adults: the question is not sin, nor a sound philosophical view of the human good, but whether the statute infringes the civil rights of free and equal citizens. A bad reason is not the same as a non-public one, and a motive is not the justification.
  • TSH sees a Catch-22. Religious reasons would fail Rawls's test, while Chua dismissed even medical arguments as a facade for religion, a test more far-reaching than Rawls's. A motive test is also unfair: the non-religious are as moved by a worldview, and a worldview is often built from one's stands rather than causing them. So each argument must be taken on its merits. A Catholic MP opposed that bill as bad for the nation, and warned that writing off opposition as religious saves the trouble of answering it.
  • Concede Rawls's reply: sorting by merit rests on disputed premises. TSH answers that process rests on substance too. Merit can show an argument true; coercing those outside the faith still needs a reason they can examine.

Close: a secular comprehensive reason is no better than a religious one; what matters is whether others can examine it. Finnis denies that belief in personhood from conception is religious at all: it comes from the same close attention that yields good science.

Section 3: what is left for religion

Both Finnis and Rawls protect religion, neither neutrally (block E11.2). For Finnis "religion" is a basic good, open to agnostic or even negative answers; he values the inquiry whatever its outcome. For Rawls it is equal liberty of conscience, and denying a basic liberty "normally" breaks reciprocity; he takes religion's truths off the political agenda. TSH warns that this may leave religious perspectives "nugatory", of no practical effect, wherever an issue counts as metaphysical. The metaphysical question is then settled anyway, by reasoning opposed to religion. Whether an issue is "political" is itself contested: plural marriage is her example. So "ban it because a faith condemns it" fails, and so does "religion is a private matter with no claim on the law".

Close: the risk is the opposite one, that "political values" leave religious perspectives with little practical effect.

Singapore

Singapore is a secular state, as a 1966 Commission put it, with no clause forbidding an established religion. The Maintenance of Religious Harmony Act allows restraining orders against religious leaders who stir enmity between faiths or mix religion and politics, and a 1989 White Paper asks believers to stress common moral values. In 2022 Masagos said secular, universal values decide. Such an order goes beyond Rawls twice: it makes a moral duty legal, and it reaches the background culture he leaves free. Lifting the party whip for the 1969 abortion vote arguably endorsed weighing religious views (TSH), though the Minister then dismissed them as minority dogma.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case for exclusion is liberty and humility (E1.6): political power belongs to free and equal citizens together, so imposing one faction's view of the good treats dissenters as subjects. Against exclusion, use the first and last sentences of E4.1: no legislative stance leaves a moral question unanswered, so each stand must rest on reasons every citizen can examine.

My weak point: my test, whether those outside the faith can examine a reason, may look like Rawls's filter under another name, and it may favour secular reasons. I concede that it is a contested moral position; TSH asks what grounds Rawls's "reasonable", and the same question meets mine. But it bars no argument from being offered, and it asks the same of the Kantian as of the believer.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right that a faith cannot alone justify coercion, and wrong that religious argument must be excluded. Rawls, Finnis, TSH and even Devlin agree that a faith alone cannot justify coercing those outside it; they split on how to sort reasons: by kind (Rawls) or by merit (Finnis and TSH). Repaired: “Any argument may be offered, religious or not; a coercive law needs at least one reason that those outside the faith can examine.” For the legislator, this means giving that reason in public and answering for it. If the statement says religious arguments are "as good as any other": it is right that a faith may give a legislator a reason to speak, and wrong that it can alone justify coercing others.

Using it in the hypo

Tan offends against public reason: his only reason is religious, and he is an MP speaking on a law. Devi does not, because she gives a public reason. The archbishop speaks in the background culture, which Rawls leaves free. A judge is bound most strictly of all. The bill itself turns a moral duty of civility into a legal one and reaches into the background culture, so vote against it.

Memory line

Speak from faith, coerce on reasons others can examine; sort by merit or kind, never by motive.

  4. They are bound, but judges most strictly.

key ideaWhat are the three ways of sorting reasons in E11.1?
  1. Each label is attached to the wrong person. Rawls sorts by kind, Chua by motive, Finnis and TSH by merit.
  2. Correct. Rawls asks what kind of reason it is (public or comprehensive), Chua asked about the motive behind it, and Finnis and TSH ask whether it is a good reason.
  3. Each label is attached to the wrong person. Rawls sorts by kind, Chua by motive, Finnis and TSH by merit.
  4. Chua sorted by motive, and Finnis and TSH by merit.
Kind, motive, merit.
key ideaWhat is TSH's Catch-22?
  1. TSH does not claim that. Her point is that believers' reasons were rejected whichever kind they gave.
  2. TSH does not claim that. Her Catch-22 is about believers whose secular reasons were dismissed as religious.
  3. Correct. Religious reasons fail Rawls's test, yet when believers gave medical reasons in 1969, Chua dismissed them as a facade for religion, so they could not win either way.
  4. Rawls says public reason is not secular reason.
Damned either way.
hypoAmong the public-forum actors in Hypo 22, who keeps to public reason?
  1. That makes a moral duty legal and goes beyond Rawls.
  2. A free vote does not lift the duty on essentials.
  3. Correct. Her motive may be religious; her reason is public.
  4. Judges are bound most strictly.
Motive may be religious; the reason must be public.
E45

Ruritania: vote as MP, rule as judge (Chief hypo)

★ very highly likely

The question Sample 2 Q2, the Chief hypo

Ruritania's MP proposes banning the sale and consumption of alcohol and tobacco, with long prison terms and no exception. Grounds: a faith followed by a large minority condemns any mood-altering substance; studies show the products tend to be physically harmful; 85% at least "somewhat" support it. How would you vote as an MP? As a judge, would you enforce it if passed?

The hypo Hypo 5: same method, another bill

A bill makes it an offence (up to five years) to publish anything "offensive to the religious feelings of any group", prosecutions needing the Minister's consent with no guidelines, and lets the Minister order corrections of statements he thinks false. Grounds: harmony is fragile; believers are distressed even by private mockery; 88% say religion "should be respected". Vote; then, as judge, would you convict a comedian whose video mocking a festival went only to 20 friends?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

Two hats, two questions. The MP asks what the law should be. The judge asks what fidelity to the law requires. Each of the bill's three grounds maps onto a kit, and the penalty, long prison terms for consumption without exception, sits on the top rung of the coercion ladder. Plan about 870 words for the MP and 440 for the judge.

The answer in one breath

The bill rests on three grounds, a faith, a health study and a poll, and takes the gravest step on the coercion ladder. As an MP I would vote against it and move narrower measures, because none of its grounds justifies that coercion. As a judge I would still enforce it if validly passed, because it is harsh but not intolerable. The answers differ because the two roles ask different moral questions.

Section 1: the faith

A faith may give a citizen reasons; it cannot alone justify coercing others, and an MP speaks in the public forum, where public reason binds. Use the legitimacy kit for Finnis and Rawls (E1.4): Finnis's state may favour the basic goods, but within peace and justice, on reasons accessible to all whatever their religion; Rawls bars enforcing a comprehensive doctrine as such. Add Rawls on equal validity (E2.1): no doctrine is privileged in advance. Add Devlin's secular limit (E2.8): the law cannot rely on doctrines citizens may disbelieve. And Yaacob Ibrahim's 2005 observation that drinking and other activities Muslims consider vices are not banned.

Close: a large minority's view is not Devlin's shared morality, and for Rawls it is one comprehensive doctrine among many. So the faith is a voice, not a ground. And a ban without exception would catch sacramental or temple use; denying worship "normally" breaks reciprocity (Rawls).

Section 2: health

Harm to the drinker is paternalism, which Hart accepts only where consent is defective (kit E1.3). Concede that addiction is the inner compulsion Hart counts, so he would support controls. But his own example controls supply, drugs only on prescription, and jailing the user defeats the protection paternalism exists to give.

The best case for this ground is harm to others: passive smoking and drink-driving. That supports bans on smoking in public and drink-driving laws, not prison for private use. And "tend to be" harmful concedes that moderate use may do no harm. The public-offence ground reaches public drinking at most, not distress at the bare knowledge that others drink, and choosing which offence counts is itself a moral judgement (TSH, after Harris, on teetotallers).

On my application, health, unlike the faith, is a public reason for Rawls, and life is a basic good for Finnis, so both admit this ground. Their objection, like Hart's, is to the coercion, not the aim. Addiction subverts practical reasonableness, whose inner peace must not be due to drugs, and pleasure is only a "secondary and conditionally valuable" good (Finnis). Yet his state keeps peace and justice and does not set out to make people good.

Close: that supports licensing and restricting sale, not jailing the user it protects.

Section 3: the poll

Devlin is the bill's natural ally in principle. His own example is drunkenness: if a quarter or half the population were drunk every night, he sees no theoretical limit to legislating against it. But his practical tests then sink the bill: toleration up to the limits of tolerance, a real feeling of reprobation, and what twelve people at random would accept after discussion. "Somewhat support" meets none of them. Use either the majority kit (E2.3), Devlin and Rawls, or the line from E2.4 on the 70% who were never asked about criminalising, not both. Add the reasonable man (E15.1): his judgement may be largely feeling, so the test cannot tell settled judgement from prejudice; read charitably, Devlin asks for feeling after discussion.

Close: the poll is evidence to weigh, not a mandate.

The vote

Use the first three sentences of the ladder (E1.2): enforcing can mean punishing, regulating or promoting, and the justification needed rises with the coercion. On that ladder, regulation and promotion are justified; punishing consumption is not. I vote against the bill and move narrower measures: licensing, limits on sale, education.

The judge

Now the judge's hat (block E45.1), almost word for word. The judge asks not whether the law is wise, but what it is and what fidelity to it requires. Climb a ladder. 1. Validity: the law was validly passed and is consistent with Article 4. 2. Interpretation by principle and purpose, and discretion within the statute. But "without exception" cannot be read down against clear words. 3. Article 15, religious freedom: an as-applied challenge is weak, since Article 15(4), read literally, lacks the international covenant's "necessary" test (TSH), and Colin Chan reads the Constitution within its own four walls. 4. Refusal only at the Radbruch threshold, shown by broad historical consensus (TSH). The law is harsh, not intolerable: no consensus condemns prohibition as it condemns genocide and slavery.

Kennedy's five courses are open in principle, but candour bars his covert last two. In a basically just system I would go along, denouncing the law if I think it unjust. Withdrawing would only leave the work to another judge, and open refusal waits for the threshold. So "I would enforce it" is a moral conclusion, reached through a theory of the judicial role. If a temple or church claims Article 15, it runs here, with the sin-and-crime material.

Your own weak point

Two sentences (E2.6): demanding reasons beyond majority feeling is itself a contested moral position. That confirms that every answer takes a stand. There is no full steelman in a problem answer: give one best-case sentence per ground instead. And there is no repaired statement.

Conclusion

As an MP I would vote against the bill; as a judge I would enforce it if validly passed. That is not inconsistency. The MP asks what the law should be, and the judge what fidelity to it requires. On the MP's question the four theorists reach my vote by different roads: Hart by paternalism's limits, Devlin by his practical tests, Rawls by public reasons and Finnis by the state's limited aim.

Watch: two hats cannot keep to five theorists, so add no new names.

Other bills, other hats

If the exam's bill is different, the sheet has a router (E45.2). Each ground goes to a kit: a religious ground to the legitimacy kit with sin and crime; health or harm to the harm kit; a poll to the majority kit; autonomy or consent to the right-to-decide kit; a claim of neutrality to the neutrality kit with "permitting decides too". Each hat goes to a block: the lawyer to the lawyering kit (the three scenarios, Pepper's own limits); the citizen to the duty-to-obey blocks; the judge to E45.1; an official or junior to "stay within your role". An intolerable law goes to the unjust-system essay (E24), never E45.1.

Then the topics table (E45.3), grouped by kind of bill: morals (gambling, adultery, paid sex, plural marriage, promoting marriage, religious offence, a faith burdened); life and body (assisted dying, abortion, surrogacy, organ sale, sentencing); powers (public health rules, emergency powers, a rule-of-law package, a fallen regime); and courts (a new judge, a hard case, realists and crits, a lawyer's week). Find the row, copy its lead blocks.

A few pivots for special bills:

  • Where harm to others is plainly made out, such as trafficking in persons or contagion, support the offence and argue about the penalty, the exemption or the Minister's power (E45.6). Devlin then has nothing to say, because his quarrel with Hart is about punishing immorality as such.
  • Severity: Hart separates whether to punish from how severely, so a harsh penalty needs its own case, and a mandatory term buys certainty, not justice (E45.7).
  • Wolfenden's test and paid sex (E45.8): the Report would drive it off the streets, not ban it in private.
  • Consent to bodily harm (E45.9): consent is no defence to the gravest offences against the person; Devlin says that shows the law enforces morality, Hart says it is paternalism.
  • Plural marriage (E45.10): Rawls decides nothing until equality of women is shown; Devlin's "house" presupposes one house.
  • A faith as the target (E45.11), rule-making in a crisis (E45.12, Fuller's requirements all at once) and whose morality governs private law (E45.13).

Using it in the hypo

Distress at "the bare knowledge" that others mock a faith in private is not harm, as Hart argues (E10.1). Unpublished guidelines and the Minister's discretion fail Fuller's requirements of promulgation and clarity (E44.3). That 88% "respect" religion does not mean they want offenders imprisoned. As a judge, note that a private chat group of 20 people sits at the edge of what "publish" means: read the word narrowly first, then work down the judge's ladder.

Memory line

Faith a voice, health a reason for controls, poll a gauge: vote no, move narrower measures; as judge, enforce unless intolerable.

How these essays fit together

E11 is the theory behind the first ground of E45: a faith may speak but cannot alone coerce. E45 applies it, with the harm kit for the second ground and the majority kit for the third. If a paper has both a religion statement and a problem question, keep the full sorting material (E11.1) in E11 and give E45 only the short version: Devlin's secular limit, the 2005 observation, and Rawls's "normally breaks reciprocity" for worship.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • E11: no theorist sorts reasons by religion. Rawls by kind, Finnis and TSH by merit, a motive test is unfair.
  • Public reason binds the public forum on essentials; the background culture is free; public reason is not secular reason.
  • Religion is a basic good for Finnis and a basic liberty for Rawls; TSH fears it becomes "nugatory".
  • Repaired: any argument may be offered; coercion needs a reason those outside the faith can examine.
  • E45, MP: faith a voice, health supports controls not prison, the poll is evidence. Vote against; move narrower measures.
  • E45, judge: validity, interpretation, Article 15, the Radbruch threshold. Harsh, not intolerable, so enforce.
  • Two hats, two questions; no repaired statement in a problem.
  • Other bills: route each ground and hat through E45.2 and the topics table.

Video 7Hart and Devlin under testLaw-making

E14

Hart's criminal law theory under test

The question 2022-23 Q2

"Applying Hart's theory of what the criminal law may prohibit requires moral judgement. But that is not fatal to it. Hart's theory remains the best way of keeping the law to the minimum."

The hypo Hypo 20, MP limb

A bill lets any adult sell one kidney to a state agency for $60,000 after two opinions and 30 days; surgeons must operate when rostered. Grounds: my body is my own and consent makes all the difference; hundreds of lives saved; people already take dangerous jobs for money. 61% are "uncomfortable". How would you vote, and is Hart's or Finnis's approach more convincing?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

Each sentence does a different job. Sentence 1 concedes an objection. Sentence 2 denies that the objection is fatal. Sentence 3 makes the real claim: Hart's theory is the best. So you agree, agree, and then qualify. The flaw is in a word with two readings: "the minimum".

The answer in one breath

By moral judgement I mean deciding what counts as harm, defective consent and serious offence. By fatal I mean showing that the theory cannot do the job it sets itself. I accept the first two claims. Hart's grounds need moral judgement at every step, and that is not fatal, because he never promised otherwise. The third overstates: his theory is the best way of limiting punishment, not a complete account of what law may do.

A trap: "the minimum" is Devlin's phrase. The law sets the minimum of morality, not the maximum. It is not Hart's "minimum content of natural law", the rules on violence, theft and deceit that every society must have, which is a floor under law's content, not a ceiling on punishment.

Section 1: "requires moral judgement"

The concession is right. State Hart's test in one sentence (card E10.1): what justifies this coercion, apart from immorality as such? Harm to others (but not the distress people feel at the bare knowledge of what others do in private); paternalism, where consent is defective; public decency, where offence is serious and likely, weighed against the freedom lost; and moralism alone, never.

Then the objection and reply from the card. TSH presses that paternalism is ultimately "based on morals", because morals pick its targets: the law treats dangerous sports differently from a person who wants a healthy limb removed. And "harm" is indeterminate. The reply: Hart can grant that applying his grounds takes moral judgement. He denies only that immorality as such is a reason to punish.

Test case (from E45.9): consent is no defence to the gravest offences against the person. Mill cannot explain that. Devlin says there is only one explanation, that the law enforces a moral principle. Hart replies that paternalism explains it: the law may protect people against themselves, since passing desires, clouded judgement and subtle pressure weaken consent.

Close: what counts as harm, defective consent and serious offence are moral questions.

Section 2: "not fatal"

Needing moral judgement is fatal only to a theory that promised to avoid it, and Hart never did (block E14.1). His case against Devlin is itself a critical morality, a quarrel between "divergent critical principles", not between morality and its absence. What he denies is narrower: that immorality as such is a reason to punish.

Then Hart's grey sentence from the legitimacy kit (E1.4): his objections are moral and prudential, not sceptical. Coercion is "prima facie objectionable", and Devlin's claim that society would disintegrate, with no evidence behind it, deserves no more respect than the Emperor Justinian's old statement about earthquakes. Whether a society may preserve itself depends on what sort of society it is and on the steps it takes.

Close: Hart even lets moral gravity set how severely offences are punished, treating morally similar offences alike, yet denies that this makes immorality a ground for punishing at all. What may be punished and how severely are distinct questions. And applying any standard, legal or moral, needs judgement, as Hart's own penumbra shows.

Section 3: "the best way … to the minimum"

This is the longest section. "Best" needs a rival, a job and a cost (block E14.2, which already holds the rivals; copy none of their cells beside it).

On the job of limiting punishment, Hart wins. Devlin sets no theoretical limit and can punish what meets none of Hart's grounds. Devlin's own example, cruelty to animals, proves less than he thinks: Hart grounds it in the animals' suffering, not in immorality. Rawls tests kinds of reason, not conduct, so a majority acting on public reasons may still criminalise. Finnis may prohibit acts that intend damage to a basic good, even senseless ones, though only as a permission used inside a state that keeps only peace and justice. Hart alone excludes moralism as a ground.

Yet Hart's minimum is not the smallest. Mill forbids paternalism outright, so he would punish less, and Hart goes beyond Mill. So "the best way" must mean the narrowest defensible minimum. Mill's is narrower but cannot explain why consent is no defence to the gravest offences. Devlin's is set by feeling, not reasons. Hart's is the narrowest that survives the hard cases.

But Hart says little about regulation and promotion, which is where most law-making on morals now happens. Singapore's Article 156, which lets Parliament promote marriage, is an example.

Close: Hart also counts costs Devlin leaves out: the pain to the offender, the restraint on everyone else, and, where laws are kept but not enforced, "formidable discriminatory powers" for police and prosecutors. On punishment Hart wins; on regulation and promotion he says little.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is Hart's own (E14.3). He asks the right question: whether the bare fact that positive morality condemns conduct is any reason to punish it. He finds no evidence for disintegration. And Singapore's position after 2022 is, in the Government's words, "broadly similar" to Wolfenden's, with Hart's lines (non-consent, the young, public indecency) still crimes. This shows Hart's grounds limit punishment tightly and workably. It does not show that they need no moral judgement, or that they say what law may promote.

My weak point: I rank Hart first. I concede he loses on regulation and promotion, where Rawls's public reasons and Finnis's common good say more. I claim only that on the job the statement names, keeping punishment to a minimum, he beats them.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right in its first two sentences and overstated in its third. Devlin, Rawls and Finnis need moral judgement as much as Hart; they part from him on the ground of punishment: society's feeling (Devlin), public reasons (Rawls) or damage to a basic good (Finnis). Repaired: “Hart's theory needs moral judgement, which is no objection to it, and it limits punishment more tightly than Devlin, Finnis or Rawls; it is not a complete account of what law may do.” For the legislator, this means using Hart's grounds to decide what may be punished, and looking elsewhere for what may be promoted.

If the statement is about another theorist in the same three-sentence shape, use the same plan: concede with that theorist's test card (Devlin, Rawls or Finnis), then "fatal only if it promised to avoid it", then compare with rivals on the job named. The repaired line becomes: "This theory sets a sound discipline on reasons, or on coercion, but its contested premise means it cannot settle the issue alone." For Fuller, use his soundness line and lead with his cell on the law–morality link.

Using it in the hypo

This bill permits rather than prohibits, so Hart's question is reversed: may the law keep banning the sale? Look at consent: money pressure and "transitory desires" can make consent defective, which is where Hart allows paternalism. Finnis adds that injuring yourself for money damages a basic good as a means (E17.2, E45.9), and consent cannot cure an intended harm. Then rank the theories by the job they do.

Memory line

Fatal only to a theory that promised to avoid moral judgement, and Hart never did. Best at limiting punishment, silent on promotion.

  2. Correct. Hart treats morally similar offences alike without making immorality a ground for punishing at all.
  3. Hart rejects positive morality as a reason to punish; severity is a separate question.
  4. The distinction is between two questions, not between two institutions.
Two questions: whether, and how much.
E15

Devlin under test

The question exam-style

"Society may use the criminal law to enforce its shared morality, just as it punishes treason, because without a shared morality it would disintegrate." Or: is the reasonable man's feeling a sound guide to what the law should punish?

The hypo Hypo 19, MP limb

Selling sex in private is lawful; soliciting in public is an offence. A bill makes paying for sex anywhere an offence (up to three years) and repeals the soliciting offence; police will not enforce in two tolerated districts. Grounds: "every decent person knows" it degrades buyer and seller; many sellers were trafficked or in debt; residents complain and 68% say the trade "should not be seen". How would you vote?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

The statement gives Devlin's premise and leaves out his safeguards. The marker wants you to read Devlin fairly, then find where he really fails: the disintegration claim, and the use of feeling instead of reasons. "Disintegrate" has several readings, so this is an equivocation.

The answer in one breath

Devlin is right that no law on morals is neutral and that a society may judge conduct. He is wrong that the survival of society supplies the reason to punish, because that claim is either trivial or unproven, and his own test lets common feeling, not reason, fix what the law enforces. Read fairly, he licenses far less than the statement claims.

Section 1: "society may judge and enforce"

Devlin asks three questions: may society pass judgement on morals, may it use the law to enforce that judgement, and on what principles? He answers yes to the first two. His case is not that any religion's morality is true. Monogamy "remains there because it is built into the house in which we live", and whoever wants to live in the house "must accept it as built". So any society may enforce its own morality: that is an argument from cultural relativism, not moral realism. He adds one secular limit: the law cannot rely on doctrines citizens may disbelieve. And consent is no defence to the gravest offences, he says, because the offence is against society.

Close: Hart answers the consent rule with paternalism, not moralism: Devlin's "only one explanation" is "simply not true". So the weight falls on the treason analogy. An established morality is "as necessary as good government", and "the suppression of vice is as much the law's business as the suppression of subversive activities". That treats private immorality as an attack on society, which is exactly what has to be shown.

Section 2: how the morality is found

Devlin finds the shared morality in the reasonable man, not in a poll. A majority's dislike is "not nearly enough", and every citizen's assent would be too much, so he asks what twelve people at random might, after discussion, be expected to accept unanimously. Picture a jury.

Then the block on the reasonable man (E15.1). He is not "the rational man": he need not reason, and his judgement may be largely feeling. But why call him reasonable? He looks like the average citizen, shaped by his society, so the test cannot tell settled judgement from prejudice. That is Hart's point: disgust is not yet a reason to punish. Read charitably, Devlin asks for feeling after discussion, deeply felt and not manufactured. Add Devlin's relativism in a clause: he treats a society's morality as honest belief, "right or wrong", not truth.

Close: disgust is only "a good indication" that the limits of tolerance are near. Before a practice goes beyond them there must be "a deliberate judgement" that it injures society, reached "calmly and dispassionately". So the test is deliberated feeling: better than a head-count, but unable to tell settled judgement from prejudice. TSH allows that his "common sense" may mean intuitive moral judgement, but realists fear that judgement by feeling comes close to non-cognitivism. Hart's example of a prejudiced society shows the risk: a consensus can be tested and found wanting.

Section 3: disintegration

Here is the decisive section. "Society would disintegrate" has four readings (from the lectures). 1. Society simply is its morality. Then any moral change destroys it, which Hart calls "absurd". He grants that some shared morality is essential to any society, but moral change is more like a peaceful change of constitution than an overthrow. 2. Society will actually collapse. But there is no evidence. Devlin appeals to "history"; Hart replies that "no reputable historian" has held it, and that it deserves no more respect than Justinian's statement about earthquakes. Nor is there evidence that morality is a "seamless web", so that deviation in one area spreads to all conduct. Where European countries relaxed such laws, Hart finds that conventional morality did not even move in a permissive direction. 3. The bonds between people weaken. That is Robert George's version: without a shared morality people merely live "in proximity to one another in peace". But TSH notes that those who reject a shared life may not regret its loss. 4. A principled slope: once private intimacy is a right, other rights may be claimed to follow, such as recognition, marriage and adoption. This is the reading that bites hardest, and Singapore's Article 156 answers it in advance for marriage.

Devlin's reply is that immorality only can threaten society. But if it only can, why harsh sanctions now? And every reading presumes a consensus to lose: the thesis fits a society in broad and deep moral agreement, like 1950s England, far better than a plural one like Singapore.

Close: for private conduct the treason analogy fails. A threat to society must be shown, not presumed.

Soundness

Devlin is consistent on his relativist footing, but that footing gives every society's morality the same claim to enforcement, a persecuting one included (Hart). It also undercuts his alarm: if every morality has an equal claim, a new one replacing the old is no loss, so calling change "disintegration" needs a reason to value the old code, which he never gives. On the hard case his practical tests often save him: the maximum freedom consistent with society's integrity, toleration up to the limits of tolerance, slowness and privacy. He eventually backed decriminalisation. But against Hart he loses on the job of limiting punishment, since he sets no theoretical limit.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is Devlin's candour. Every society takes a public stand, and even Wolfenden presupposed a public morality: it would protect the young from "corruption" and punish living on "immoral" earnings, which makes no sense unless society condemns the conduct. Devlin says openly what the law already does. His restraints are real: the law is concerned "with the minimum and not with the maximum", and the man in the jury box is "an active reality", so morals laws he will not accept do not, in the long run, work. This shows that law may protect a shared morality. It does not show that private deviation threatens society, or that feeling can fix what the law punishes.

My weak point (E10.5): I use consensus as evidence, and consensus can mislead. Majorities have been badly wrong before. So I treat it as a gauge, not a ground.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right that a society may judge and protect its morality, and wrong that disintegration supplies the reason to punish. Read fairly, Devlin licenses far less than the statement claims, but his test still lets common feeling, not reason, fix what the law enforces. Devlin and Hart agree that some shared morality is essential to a society; they split on whether its bare existence is a reason to punish. Repaired: “A society may protect its shared morality by law only where reasons, not feeling alone, show that the conduct injures others or the society, with coercion graded to those reasons.” For the legislator, this means treating deliberated feeling as evidence, never as the ground. For the reasonable-man form: "His feeling is evidence of what a society will tolerate, not a reason to punish."

Using it in the hypo

"Every decent person knows" is Devlin's right-minded man speaking, so ask whether twelve people chosen at random would agree after discussion. Wolfenden kept selling sex lawful and drove soliciting off the streets (E45.8); the bill reverses both halves. Trafficking is a real harm, but it supports offences aimed at coercion, not at every buyer. Non-enforcement in two districts breaches congruence (E44.6).

Memory line

Devlin is slower and more careful than the people who quote him; only the principled slope bites.

theoristsWhat makes a morality binding for Devlin?

  2. That is Rawls's idea. Devlin binds society to its actual morality, right or wrong.

  4. That is the trap: he is no natural lawyer.
Built into the house we live in.
hypoIn Hypo 19, "every decent person knows that sex for money degrades" is closest to…
  1. Degradation is a comprehensive claim.
  2. Correct. "Every decent person knows" is Devlin's right-minded man speaking. Test it his way: would twelve people agree after discussion, and is the feeling real and deeply held?
  3. The MP is appealing to what decent people feel, not to Finnis's reasoning about the basic goods.
  4. Hart's decency ground supports the street offence, not this.
The decent person.
key ideaWhy does E15's soundness line say Devlin's relativism undercuts his own alarm about disintegration?
  1. Devlin refuses to count heads; this is not his test.
  2. Devlin's relativism concerns which morality binds, not whether moral claims can be held.
  3. Devlin happily enforces a society's actual morality; the problem is calling change a loss.
  4. Correct. He never gives one; and his footing gives a persecuting society the same claim (Hart).
If all codes are equal, why mourn the old one?

How these essays fit together

E14 and E15 are the two halves of Hart against Devlin. Hart wins on limiting punishment, because he alone excludes moralism; Devlin is right that no law is neutral and that society may judge. Both essays use the point that consent is no defence to the gravest offences: Devlin uses it to show the law enforces morality, Hart to show paternalism. Write it once per script. Both also use Justinian's earthquakes. If a paper asks you to test a different theorist, Finnis has his own essay (E17), Rawls has E16, Dworkin E31 and Radbruch E43.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • E14: agree, agree, qualify. Hart needs moral judgement; that is fatal only to a theory that promised to avoid it.
  • "The minimum" is Devlin's phrase, not Hart's minimum content of natural law.
  • Hart's checklist: harm to others, paternalism, public decency, never moralism alone.
  • "Best" needs a rival, a job and a cost. Hart wins on limiting punishment, says little on promotion.
  • E15: Devlin is a cultural relativist; monogamy is built into the house.
  • The reasonable man: twelve people after discussion; deliberated feeling, not a head-count.
  • Disintegration: absurd, unproven, not regrettable, or a principled slope, which Article 156 blocks.
  • Repaired: protect shared morality only on reasons, not feeling alone.

Video 8Finnis and Rawls under testLaw-making

E17

Finnis under test

The question exam-style

"Finnis's natural law either dictates how citizens must live, or it gives the law-maker no guidance at all." (Or: it rests on taste.) Or: explain the basic goods and practical reasonableness, and how they bear on law-making.

The hypo Hypo 15, MP limb

Trafficking already carries up to 30 years. A bill makes death mandatory for trafficking over 15 grams of "Velvet", applies it to 40 prisoners already serving terms, and lets a court substitute life only if the Prosecutor certifies the offender a helpful courier, the certificate unquestionable. Grounds: those who deal in death deserve to die; each kilogram ruins about a hundred lives and death deters; 83% support it for "drug lords". How would you vote on each part?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

The statement assumes a theory of the good must either dictate a way of life or offer no guidance at all. That is a false dilemma. Finnis shows a third path: firm limits, a direction, and a wide range of reasonable choices within them.

The answer in one breath

By natural law I mean Finnis's account of the basic goods and the requirements of practical reasonableness, not a code of divine commands. Seven basic goods and nine requirements of practical reasonableness yield a few absolute limits, such as never taking life directly as a means, and a common good that is a framework, not a life-plan. Within those limits legislators choose prudently, and reasonable people may differ.

Section 1: the basic goods

Finnis names seven basic goods: life, knowledge, play, aesthetic experience, sociability (friendship), practical reasonableness, and "religion", which is open to agnostic or even negative answers. They are self-evident and grasped by reflection, not inferred from facts or from urges. They have no objective hierarchy, so people may rank them differently. A scholar may put knowledge first for his whole life, but the ranking lies in his chosen plan, not in the goods. Self-evident does not mean obvious to everyone: dissent may be "inculpably erroneous", an honest mistake.

Pleasure is not an eighth good. Nozick's experience machine shows why: a sensible person would not plug into a machine that gave them any experience they liked, because they want to do things and be someone, not merely to have the experiences. So the goods fix what is worth pursuing, not one way of life.

Section 2: practical reasonableness

Practical reasonableness is both a good and the method that orders the others. Three of its nine requirements bear most on law: no arbitrary preference among persons (the third), respect for every basic good in every act (the seventh), and the common good (the eighth).

The seventh yields a few absolutes, such as never taking life directly as a means. Acts are individuated by intention, so harm foreseen as a side effect is judged by fairness, using the golden rule and prior commitments, not barred outright (block E17.1). The method runs in steps: a good, a requirement, and circumstances in which only some acts respect them, so those acts ought or ought not be done. Life is a good, and the seventh requirement bars acting against it, so we ought not kill. Natural law is not just its strict negatives: everything reasonably required is required by natural law.

If the statement names an act, use the permissibility card (E17.2). Which good is pursued? Is a basic good damaged as an end or a means? If so, the act is wrong. Side effects must pass the golden rule: would I think the act reasonable had I been the person harmed? Barred: the hostage case, sacrificing one to free ten, even a volunteer; self-injury for insurance; euthanasia. Permitted or arguable: switching a runaway trolley, triage, a dangerous mining job.

Close: practical reasonableness yields some absolute limits and leaves a wide range of reasonable choices. The hostage case is barred because it takes life directly as a means and arbitrarily prefers some persons to another.

Section 3: the law-maker

For the law-maker the theory gives limits and a direction, not a blueprint. Draw on Finnis's cells in three kits (neutrality, legitimacy, harm). Every society takes a public stand, and the state may favour the basic goods, but it keeps only peace and justice, and its common good is a set of conditions, not a life-plan. It may prohibit acts that intend damage to a basic good, which is a permission, not a programme. It must reason from principles accessible to all, whatever their religion. And prudence may counsel restraint, as on hate speech or fake news, where legislators weigh chilling effects in particular places. Public authorities may not themselves intend damage to a good.

Finnis denies that there is one reasonable life-plan the state should press on its citizens, yet holds the common good definite enough to exclude many kinds of law. He calls it unreasonable for a statesman to make each subject conform to the excellences he has chosen for himself.

Close: "dictates how citizens must live" fails, since Finnis is non-neutral in ends but liberal in means. And "gives no guidance" fails, since the absolutes and the common good rule some laws out.

Soundness

Finnis holds together better than his critics allow, since self-evident means grasped, not obvious. He concedes there is no magic in the number seven, so disputing one good leaves the method standing. His weak point is access: self-evidence arguably rests on intuitions shaped by upbringing, and the line between intended and foreseen harm is arguably artificial, though he answers that intention is fixed by the plan adopted, which cannot be redescribed at will. On the hard case he gives a clear answer, against euthanasia, which is a strength in a theory and a cost in a plural society. Against Rawls, he is stronger on who counts and on what consent cannot cure, and weaker on reasons others can accept. If the statement says "rests on taste", this is the longest section.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case depends on the statement's side.

  • For "dictates": the best case is Rawls's. A state that favours the basic goods acts on one comprehensive doctrine, and Finnis calls Rawls's "thin theory" arbitrary only by asserting a fuller one. His absolutes bind even a competent adult who chooses death, since consent cannot cure intended harm. This shows Finnis constrains more than a liberal would accept. It does not show he dictates how citizens must live: his common good is a set of conditions, his state keeps only peace and justice, and his ninth requirement honours even a mistaken conscience.
  • For "no guidance": the best case is the theory's own structure. The goods are incommensurable and unranked, so on most bills Finnis leaves the legislator where he started. That shows the theory decides few statutes, not that it gives no guidance: the absolutes and the aim of peace and justice rule some laws out. Where a case turns on judgement, Finnis asks for the practical wisdom good people have, and expects no more precision than the subject allows.

My weak point (E1.7): Finnis's goods are accessible but not accepted. My view would impose his conception only if his goods alone could justify coercion, and they cannot: reasonable disagreement limits what the state may coerce, even where he is right.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is wrong on both horns. Finnis neither dictates how citizens must live nor leaves the law-maker without guidance: his absolutes and his common good set limits, and within them legislators choose prudently and reasonable people may differ. A theory is not refuted by failing to dictate an answer. Finnis and Rawls agree that the state may not impose a whole way of life; they split on whether it may favour the goods (Finnis) or only political values (Rawls). Repaired: “Finnis's natural law gives the law-maker firm limits and a direction, not a blueprint; within the limits reasonable people may differ, and coercion needs reasons all can examine.” For the legislator, this means respecting the absolutes, favouring the goods by securing conditions rather than commanding lives, and giving reasons accessible to all. For "rests on taste": the goods are grasped by reflection, not chosen, so dissent is error, not taste; the weak point is access, not arbitrariness.

If the statement is about law's nature rather than law-making, use Finnis's cell on the law–morality link: an unjust law is no nullity to disregard; it lacks moral force to bind in conscience and falls short of law's central case.

Using it in the hypo

Start with Finnis's absolute: a law authorising an act that intends death is itself wrong (E45.7, E17.2), whatever the deterrence figures, because life is not a cost to net off against other costs (E17.3). Below that absolute, prudence decides, so long prison terms remain open. Part (b) punishes retroactively (E44.4), and part (c)'s unreviewable certificate fails Fuller.

Memory line

Seven goods, nine requirements, a few absolutes: limits and a direction, not a blueprint.
