E16

Rawls under test

The question exam-style

"Political power is rightfully used only on grounds that every citizen can share." Or: political liberalism is neutral; "freestanding yet overriding" is a contradiction; it is a modus vivendi in philosophical dress.

The hypo Hypo 14

On commercial surrogacy, commissioner E says: "We should legislate only what all Ruritania's faiths agree on. That is an overlapping consensus." F says: "Only reasons every citizen can share count. Faith-based objections must be ignored." H says: "Law should make people better; ban it because it degrades motherhood." Evaluate E, F and H, and recommend using Rawls and Finnis.

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

Each form tests a different part of Rawls. Always start with when power is legitimate, then take only the one further section that matches the form set, and drop the other two. The flaw is often an equivocation: "share" can mean "in fact accept" or "may reasonably be expected to endorse".

The answer in one breath

Rawls rightly requires reasons each citizen could accept, but his neutrality is of justification, not outcome: permitting something still takes a moral side. By grounds every citizen can share, Rawls means political values all may reasonably be expected to endorse, not reasons all in fact accept, and those values come from a family of liberal political conceptions, not from his own alone. I argue that the test should be accessibility and soundness, not acceptance: a reason can be open to all and still be rejected, which is Finnis's difficulty as well as Rawls's. And process rests on substance.

For a modus vivendi statement, open instead: the statement mistakes an overlapping consensus for a truce; it is affirmed on moral grounds, not bargained. For freestanding yet overriding: the statement sees a contradiction in two claims that fit.

Section 1: when power is legitimate

Rawls asks more than a majority and less than unanimity (block E16.1): power is legitimate only under constitutional essentials that all citizens may reasonably be expected to endorse. Reciprocity asks for reasons others might reasonably accept, not merely understand. Servetus understood perfectly well why Calvin persecuted him; that was not enough.

Finnis poses a dilemma. Read as a prediction, the test gives a gross veto to anyone who refuses. Read as a standard, it judges merits, as calling some believers "mistaken" does. He calls the principle illegitimate, unreasonable and uncivil: it restricts deliberation where being right matters most, on who holds fundamental rights, and leaves the gravest questions to sheer numbers or influence. TSH adds that Rawls cannot equate public reason with majority views, so he must show the votes on one side are not unreasonable.

Close: the test is moral though political, so deciding who is reasonable already takes a side.

Section 2: who must give public reasons (for "grounds every citizen can share")

Public reason is narrower than its critics suppose (block E16.2). It binds those who wield or seek coercive power in the public forum: judges most strictly, officials (above all legislators), candidates, and citizens when they vote as if they were legislators. It covers constitutional essentials and basic justice. It does not bind churches, universities or the media: the background culture is unrestricted. No argument is barred for being religious, if public reasons follow "in due course", and secular comprehensive doctrines are limited in the same way, so public reason is not secular reason. Civility is a moral duty, not a legal one, since a legal duty would curb free speech.

Close: Singapore goes further. The Maintenance of Religious Harmony Act allows restraining orders against religious leaders who stir enmity between faiths or mix religion and politics, which makes legal the duty Rawls keeps moral.

Section 3: overlapping consensus (for "a modus vivendi")

An overlapping consensus is affirmed, not bargained (block E16.3). It is a consensus of reasonable doctrines on a political conception of justice. Its object is a moral conception, and each doctrine affirms it on its own moral grounds, so it survives shifts in political power, as a mere truce cannot. A modus vivendi is a treaty kept while it pays, like sixteenth-century toleration between Catholics and Protestants, each of whom held it the ruler's duty to suppress heresy. Rawls concedes that liberal principles often begin that way, accepted reluctantly as the only alternative to civil strife, and only later come to be affirmed. The consensus is on principles, not reasons: the Golden Rule is held as divine teaching by some and as reciprocity by others, and secular doctrines such as Kant's and Mill's join too. Tolerance works outside the overlap: I may be right, and yet legislating my view would be unfair. And "legislating what the faiths share" is Devlin's shared morality, not Rawls's consensus.

Close: it is no modus vivendi, though "reasonable" decides who belongs.

Section 4: "freestanding yet overriding"

Freestanding means derived from no comprehensive doctrine, not value-free (block E16.4). The conception is political yet moral, and each citizen settles, as a matter of conscience, how it fits the rest of their doctrine. On essentials, political values normally override because they are "very great values", governing the basic framework of social life, and the cooperative virtues are "political capital". Rawls need not call them intrinsically weightier: where reasonable doctrines already support them, severe conflicts are rare. So there is no contradiction for the reasonable, who may hold their doctrine true and still think it unreasonable to enforce it. For dissenters, though, the label "reasonable" does the overriding, and TSH asks what standard grounds it. Rawls concedes that against a believer who thinks salvation justifies civil strife, he may have to imply that the doctrine is false.

Close: freestanding holds for the reasonable, and for the rest only so far as possible.

For "neutral"

Use section 1 and the two neutralities (E8.1): neutral in outcome is impossible; Rawls's neutrality is of justification, of aim and not of effect, and even that is moral.

Soundness, rival, best case and weak point

Test Rawls three ways (E18.2). Does he hold together, given that "reasonable" carries a moral load? Does he answer the hard case, given his retreat on the abortion question? Does he beat his rivals? Then the reply from Rawls's card (E10.3): the standard is moral though political, not a head-count; "freestanding" means derived from no comprehensive doctrine, not value-free; and Finnis grants that reciprocity seems broadly reasonable. The rival is Finnis: non-neutral in ends, liberal in means.

The best case is liberty and humility (E1.6): political power belongs to free and equal citizens together, so imposing one faction's view of the good treats dissenters as subjects. My weak point (E2.6): demanding public reasons is itself a contested moral position, which confirms that every answer takes a stand.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is partly right. Rawls rightly demands coercion justifiable to every citizen, but neutrality cannot deliver it: any law on the issue takes a moral side, as Rawls's own retreat on the abortion question, and Finnis's attack on it, show. Rawls and Finnis agree that coercion must be justified to those it binds. They split on whether that needs reasons all may reasonably accept (Rawls) or reasons open to all and sound (Finnis); for Finnis, some questions of basic rights have correct answers accessible to all, and dissent there is honest error, not reasonable disagreement. Repaired: “Political power is rightfully used only on reasons every citizen can examine, sound and prudently fitted to circumstances, even where some citizens reject them.” For the legislator, this means giving reasons open to all, not reasons all accept. For "neutral": Rawls's neutrality is of aim, not effect, and even its starting point is not neutral, since it treats a conception of the good as one chosen among many.

Using it in the hypo

E confuses Devlin's shared morality with Rawls's overlapping consensus. F overstates public reason: faith-based arguments may be offered so long as public reasons follow in due course, and the background culture is free. H misreads Finnis, whose state does not set out to make people integrally good, though "degrades" may hide a real point about who counts, the child.

Memory line

Reasons all may reasonably accept, from those who wield power, on essentials; a consensus affirmed, not a truce.

  3. The consensus is on a political conception only.
  4. That describes a modus vivendi.

theoristsWhat is Finnis's dilemma for Rawls?

  2. Not Finnis's objection to Rawls.
  3. Correct (E16.1). If "what others could accept" means predicting what they will accept, any group gets a veto; if it means what they should accept, the test is judging merits after all.
  4. Rawls binds judges most strictly.
Prediction or judgement?
hypoHypo 14, commissioner E: "Legislate only what all the faiths agree on: that is an overlapping consensus." Reply?
  1. Correct. Legislating only what all faiths agree on is Devlin's shared morality. Rawls's overlapping consensus is agreement on a political conception, each doctrine accepting it for its own reasons.
  2. Finnis does not legislate by agreement among faiths.
  3. The problem is the concept, not the odds.
  4. Rawls's consensus is on principles, affirmed on each doctrine's grounds.
Shared morality versus overlapping consensus.
key ideaE16's intro replaces Rawls's test of acceptance with what?
  1. Correct. That is Finnis's difficulty as well as Rawls's.
  2. That is a stricter acceptance test, not a replacement for it.
  3. Finnis's goods are accessible, not accepted; the test is soundness too.
  4. A majority is not enough for legitimacy on Rawls's own terms either.
Open to all is not accepted by all.

How these essays fit together

E17 and E16 are mirror images. Finnis is strongest on who counts and what consent cannot cure, weakest on reasons others can accept. Rawls is strongest on disciplining public reasons, weakest on who counts as reasonable. Both reject imposing a whole way of life. If a paper tests both, give Finnis the goods, the absolutes and the permission to prohibit; give Rawls the legitimacy principle and the one section that fits the form. Each essay names the other as its rival in a single sentence. For the beginning-of-life question as the test case, use E19.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • E17: a false dilemma. Finnis neither dictates nor leaves no guidance.
  • Seven goods, self-evident, grasped, unranked; pleasure is not a good (the experience machine).
  • Nine requirements; the seventh gives absolutes; intention, not foresight; side effects judged by fairness.
  • The state keeps peace and justice; may prohibit acts against a good, a permission not a programme.
  • Weak point: access, not arbitrariness.
  • E16: legitimacy needs reasons all may reasonably accept; Servetus understood Calvin, which was not enough.
  • Public reason binds the public forum on essentials; the background culture is free.
  • Overlapping consensus is affirmed on moral grounds, not a truce; freestanding is not value-free.
  • Repaired: reasons every citizen can examine, sound and prudent, even if some reject them.

Video 9Rawls and Finnis on the beginning and end of lifeLaw-making

E18

Autonomy and euthanasia through Rawls and Finnis

▲ likely

The question 2021-22 Q1

"Autonomy best furthers the common good. This therefore demands the legalisation of voluntary euthanasia, the ending of life to relieve pain and suffering. Individuals should be free to choose how and when to end their lives." Discuss with reference to Rawls and Finnis, assessing the soundness of each.

The hypo Hypo 2, MP limb

A bill lets a doctor end the life of a terminally ill adult on request, after two opinions and 14 days. Grounds: the right to the most fundamental decisions about one's life; patients suffer greatly and end-of-life care is costly; the bill "takes no side". How would you vote, using Rawls and Finnis?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

The statement moves from a value, to a statute, to a right. "Therefore demands" carries the weight: it is a gap between the premise and the conclusion. And the third sentence claims more than the second can support.

The answer in one breath

By autonomy I mean self-determination, choosing the shape of one's own life. By "demands" I mean that the law must permit the practice, not merely that it may. Autonomy deserves great weight, but as the primary value it is a contested moral claim that neither Rawls nor Finnis accepts. Neither theory supports "demands": Rawls leaves the outcome to public reasons, and Finnis opposes the practice, each sound where the other is weak.

Section 1: "autonomy … the common good"

Both theorists value autonomy; neither makes it primary. Use Finnis from the neutrality kit (non-neutral in ends, liberal in means: the state keeps only peace and justice), and Rawls from the right-to-decide kit: he protects basic liberties, but moral autonomy as the highest value is a comprehensive ideal, not a political value.

Close: for Finnis, self-determination belongs to practical reasonableness, one of seven basic goods with no objective hierarchy, so making any one primary contradicts the structure of the goods, and life is another of the seven. Rawls separates two autonomies. Political autonomy, citizens' legal independence and equal share in political power, is a political value, but it concerns citizens' standing in political life, not the choice of death. Moral autonomy, Mill's ideal of examining one's deepest ends, fails reciprocity, since many citizens reject it. The statement needs the second.

Section 2: "therefore demands"

A framework needs inputs, so it rarely demands one statute. Use the card on Rawls and Finnis (E18.1), Rawls first.

  • Rawls: if this is a constitutional essential, the reasons must be political values, and autonomy as the highest value is not one, so "autonomy demands" is not a public reason. Public reasons run both ways: liberty over one's own death, and protection of citizens whose consent may be clouded. So the outcome is left to public reason and a vote. On abortion he first called denying the right unreasonable, and then retreated.
  • Finnis: life is a basic good. Intending death, one's own or another's, chooses directly against it, and consent cannot cure intended harm, so euthanasia is barred and the state may prohibit it. Pain relief with death foreseen but not intended may be a side effect. And grounding a subjective choice in the intrinsic value of self-governance is "outright self-contradiction".

Then the last sentence of E45.5: say what the theory rules out, what it requires, and what it leaves open.

Close: Rawls leaves the outcome to public reason; Finnis's framework decides it, but against the statement.

Section 3: "a free choice of how and when"

"How and when" reaches beyond the practice the statement defines, ending life to relieve pain and suffering. A right to choose how and when would cover the healthy and the despairing, which relief of suffering cannot justify. Nor is the choice purely self-regarding: another person, usually a doctor, does the act, and since consent is no defence to the gravest offences against the person, ending a life at the person's own request is still treated as the gravest offence. Devlin reads that rule as the law enforcing a moral principle, and lists euthanasia first among the offences Wolfenden's principle would remove; Hart keeps the rule but explains it as paternalism.

A free choice also presupposes free consent. Rawls's fair terms must be acceptable to citizens as free and equal, not as dominated or manipulated, so a scheme must answer to those whose consent is least free. Add Finnis's closing point from the right-to-decide kit: the statement's argument fails on its own terms, since appealing to subjective preference and then grounding it in the value of self-governance contradicts itself.

Close: clouded judgement and subtle pressure are why the vulnerable need protection (Hart).

Soundness

Test each theory three ways (E18.2). Does it hold together: is its premise consistent with its method (Finnis's self-evidence, Rawls's "reasonable")? Does it answer the hard case? Does it beat its rival on this job?

  • Rawls: both sides can argue in political values, so public reason narrows the reasons but leaves the outcome open. Sound as a discipline, incomplete as a way to decide.
  • Finnis: critics call the line between pain relief that hastens death and intended killing artificial; he replies that intention is fixed by the plan adopted, which cannot be redescribed at will.

Each can be sound where the other is weak. Rawls is strongest on reasons in a plural society and weakest on who counts. Finnis is strongest on who counts and on what consent cannot cure, and weakest on reasons others can accept. So end with a ranking by job, not a winner.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is liberty's own. For Mill the individual is sovereign over body and mind; for Hart free choice may be valuable in itself; and a competent adult facing a painful death is the clearest case of a choice about one's own life. This shows that autonomy deserves great weight in the common good. It does not show that it is the primary value, or that it demands a particular law: the statute must also protect those whose consent is least free.

My weak point: saying that neither theory supports "demands" risks sitting on the fence. So I give a verdict: Parliament may legalise a narrowly drawn scheme, for competent adults facing death, with safeguards against pressure; but nothing demands it, and protecting the least free comes first.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right that autonomy matters to the common good, and wrong that it demands this statute. Rawls and Finnis agree that autonomy matters and is not primary; they split on what decides the statute: public reasons and a vote (Rawls), or the basic good of life (Finnis). Repaired: “Autonomy deserves great weight in the common good, but it demands no particular law.” For the legislator, this means deciding on reasons those who disagree can examine, and protecting those whose consent is least free.

Using it in the hypo

Ground 1 treats autonomy as the highest value, which is a comprehensive view, not a public reason. Ground 2's point about cost treats life as a cost to be weighed, which Finnis rejects (E17.3). Ground 3 claims neutrality, but permitting decides too. A narrowly drawn scheme might pass Rawls's test; Finnis would bar it.

Memory line

Autonomy weighs heavily but is not primary; a framework needs inputs, so nothing "demands" this law.

Sheet blocks to copy from

E19

Abortion through Rawls and Finnis

The question exam-style

"Since nobody can say when life begins, the law should take no side and leave the choice to each woman." Or: "Public reason settles the question in favour of choice." Discuss with reference to Rawls and Finnis.

The hypo Hypo 6, MP limb

A bill cuts the period for termination for any reason from 24 to 12 weeks, save to save the woman's life. Grounds: the MP's faith teaches life begins at conception; by 12 weeks there is a heartbeat and human form; 65% support "some reduction". A fellow MP says nobody can say when life begins, so Parliament should take no side. Whips are lifted. How would you vote?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

The statement treats a permissive law as the one that takes no side. That is self-refuting: permission also answers the question of who counts. The marker wants Rawls's method and Finnis's reply set against each other, and an honest ranking.

The answer in one breath

By taking no side I mean a law that implies no answer to whether the unborn counts. A permissive law takes a side all the same: permission settles whether the unborn counts. Rawls offers a way to decide without settling deep questions, and Finnis's reply is that this question cannot be bracketed, because it decides who is owed fair terms. Parliament cannot stand aside, and should argue for its limits from reasons all can examine.

Section 1: "nobody can say, so stand aside"

Uncertainty about when life begins is a reason for care, not a licence to stand aside: TSH's hunter, unsure what moves behind a bush, should not fire. Use "permitting decides too" (E4.1): moving the 1969 Abortion Bill, Chua said learned men could not agree whether the foetus is human life, and then declared that it was not, so permission settled the question (TSH). Add the facts (E1.10, first point): the 1969 Act passed 32 to 10 on a free vote, and later law allows termination up to 24 weeks. A free vote serves conscience, not necessarily public reason. And a clause from Finnis: facts such as embryology show only how the basic goods apply; they do not supply the value.

Close: truly private judgement would leave those who think the practice ends a person's life free to act on that view outside the law, as they would to protect a newborn (Finnis). A law that permits termination but forbids abandoning a newborn already rates the unborn below the newborn (TSH). Permission is neutral only if the unborn is no person, or if, though it is, the mother may have it removed, as in Thomson's argument. So "take no side" cannot be met: a permissive law answers the question too, in the woman's favour.

Section 2: public reason

Rawls offers a way to decide without settling the metaphysical question. If this is a constitutional essential or a matter of basic justice, as Rawls treats it, citizens and officials must reason from political values others might reasonably accept, and these run both ways: due respect for human life, the ordered reproduction of society over time, and the equality of women as equal citizens. Public reason also takes in the conclusions of science where they are not controversial, so embryology may enter it; what is disputed is the moral weight of those facts.

Rawls first called denying a first-trimester "qualified right" unreasonable, then accepted arguments against it framed in political values, such as public peace and the protection of human rights. So "public reason settles it for choice" overstates his own later view: public reason narrows the reasons and leaves the outcome to argument and a vote.

Section 3: Finnis's reply

Finnis gives an answer of his own. Life is a basic good, the unborn is a human being, and intending death chooses directly against that good, so direct termination is barred and the state may prohibit it. Medicine supports the premise: doctors treating the unborn speak of two patients, and the unborn and the newborn differ only in where they are. Treating a serious illness of the mother, where the loss of the pregnancy is a side effect, is a different act (the double-effect block, E17.1).

Then Finnis from the right-to-decide kit: where a choice affects another's basic good, he asks why Rawls draws reciprocity's boundary at birth. And from the abortion block: telling believers they "need not exercise" a right they think wrong is, for Finnis, no answer at all.

Close: Rawls's view that equal rights start at birth is no less comprehensive, and no more public, than one that starts them before birth, so the pro-choice side imposes a doctrine too. And any line drawn at self-awareness would exclude newborns as well. So who counts cannot be bracketed; it can only be argued, with reasons others can examine.

Soundness

Rawls is sound as a discipline on reasons and incomplete as a way to decide: his retreat suggests he could not give a defensible answer on the hard case. Finnis gives an answer, on premises many citizens reject. Each is sound where the other is weak (E18.2).

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is that the woman bears the pregnancy, which disrupts her body, education, work and family life. Where reasonable citizens cannot agree whether the unborn counts, a ban sets a definite right against an unproven one and imposes a contested answer on the one person whose body is at stake. Rawls's first view had this shape, and Thomson's argument, which he cites, puts it in public terms: severe limits on liberty may not rest on considerations those constrained may reasonably reject. This shows the woman's choice deserves great weight. It does not show that permission takes no side: it settles who counts, in her favour, and Finnis replies that rejecting the unborn's right to life is unreasonable without some relevant difference from the newborn.

My weak point (E1.10, third point): consensus on human worth does not yet extend to who counts as human, so I argue for circumspection, not victory.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right that nobody can prove when life begins, and wrong that the law can therefore stand aside. Permitting, punishing and promoting all take a stand, and permission answers who counts: here, TSH says, the sphere of the merely political is largely illusory. Rawls narrows the reasons but leaves the outcome open; Finnis answers the question, on premises many reject. They agree that the question is public and must be argued; they split on whether it can be settled without deciding who counts. Repaired: “No law on this question is neutral, so Parliament must decide who counts on reasons all can examine, and argue for its limits with circumspection, not as a victory.” For the legislator, this means that a free vote serves conscience, but the reasons must still be given in public, and a middle ground, such as twenty-four weeks with mandatory counselling, must be defended as a judgement about who counts, not as neutrality.

Using it in the hypo

The fellow MP repeats Chua's error of claiming no one can know, then answering the question anyway. The MP's faith is a voice in the debate, but not a public reason on its own. Embryology fixes the stakes once a moral premise is agreed (E37.6). The 65% who want "some reduction" give evidence, not a mandate. On a free vote, each MP should still give public reasons.

Memory line

Permission is not neutral: it answers who counts. Rawls narrows the reasons; Finnis answers the question.

How these essays fit together

E18 and E19 run the same method on the two ends of life. In both, Rawls leaves the outcome to public reasons and a vote, and Finnis gives a clear answer from the basic good of life and the bar on intending death. Both use the soundness block (E18.2) and end with a ranking by job. A shared line: Rawls and Finnis both reject force against a permissive law, for opposite reasons (for Rawls it is legitimate law; for Finnis, only because the conflict could not be won). Use it once per script. E18 leads with autonomy and consent; E19 leads with "permitting decides too" and who counts.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • E18: value, statute, right. "Therefore demands" is the gap.
  • Neither Rawls nor Finnis makes autonomy primary. Rawls: political autonomy is not moral autonomy.
  • A framework needs inputs: Rawls leaves the outcome open; Finnis decides it, against the statement.
  • "How and when" goes beyond relief of suffering; consent must be free; protect the least free.
  • Verdict: Parliament may legalise a narrow scheme; nothing demands it.
  • E19: permission takes a side; it answers who counts.
  • Rawls's political values run both ways, and he retreated from his first view.
  • Finnis: the unborn is a human being; who counts cannot be bracketed.
  • Repaired: decide who counts on reasons all can examine, with circumspection, not victory.

Video 10Law is a floor, not a ceilingJudges and lawyers

E20

No need for theory or morality (Sample 2 Q1)

★ very highly likely

The question Sample 2 Q1 · 2025-26 Q2

"In a society with a supreme written Constitution setting out the tests of legal validity and citizens' rights, judges and lawyers have no practical need for legal philosophy or personal morality. Complying with the law always gives them sufficient moral guidance."

The hypo Hypo 13

At dinner a Minister tells new Justice Siti: "Our Constitution is supreme and spells out everyone's rights, so you need no philosophy, and certainly not your personal morality." Her first cases include a request to find a natural-law right to privacy in Art 9 against an offence on private consensual conduct, which the Court of Appeal upheld ten years ago. Respond to the Minister.

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

The statement has three parts. A premise about the system: the Constitution sets validity and rights. An inference: in such a society judges and lawyers need neither philosophy nor personal morality. And what the inference rests on: compliance always gives sufficient guidance. The flaw is that the statement is itself a legal philosophy, so it takes the stand it forbids, and "always" is an absolute.

The answer in one breath

The statement is itself a legal philosophy, too moralised for the positivist and too amoral for the natural lawyer, and its natural ally, Hart, rejects its conclusion: law is not morality and must not supplant it. I accept the premise: the Constitution sets the tests of validity and the rights. By legal philosophy I mean a view of what law is and how to read it. By personal morality I mean a person's own view of what is right, as distinct from the morality the law itself adopts. But a constitution that states its own tests moves the need for legal philosophy into the reading of its open terms, and the need for a disciplined morality into the choice between lawful courses. So compliance is necessary but never always sufficient. Open each section by naming the link it tests.

Section 1: "legal philosophy"

The Constitution settles who decides and by what tests, not what its open terms mean. Either the Constitution's moral terms are filled by the positive law, and applying it already draws on the moral and philosophical ideas embedded in it; or they are not, and the law cannot always guide. Either way "no practical need" fails. To say the law is whatever the courts say glosses over the principles that should guide them. The purpose of theory, TSH says, is to guide practice and to give a standard by which practice may be judged, and no declaration of supremacy settles that.

Then TSH's cell from the judging kit (E20.1): a supreme Constitution relocates the debate. The rights chapter's words "law", "life", "liberty" and "equal" are contested moral terms. Reading Article 9's "law" as whatever is validly passed is circular, because whether an Act is valid depends on whether it breaches Article 9, the very question (block E35.4). There are four readings of "law" there: validly passed, natural justice, not absurd or arbitrary, and international law, and the courts have moved between them. Add E35.5: reading the Constitution "within its own four walls", the positivist approach of Colin Chan, need not exclude universal standards. Where the text points to a moral concept, a positivist judge may follow it outward.

Close: Article 4 is supreme only because it is accepted, and the rights chapter's "law" and "equal" force a choice of theory. Concede that higher law cuts both ways, since a judge with a very different view of dignity could use it too, so defend only a narrow trigger.

Section 2: "personal morality"

Keeping personal morality out assumes it is merely subjective. But disagreement shows at most that someone is wrong, and what cannot be demonstrated is not thereby unreal. That point is from the lectures.

Then E20.8, from "fit and public reasons": morality in judging is disciplined, not private. Fit and public reasons are tested on appeal, and a rights consensus and broad historical consensus check personal ideology. That is Alexy's answer to Hoerster's objection, a judge acting on a gravely unjust personal morality, and it is TSH's test.

Then TSH from the law-and-justice kit (E20.4): in a stable democracy the Radbruch formula is a narrow check, not an unelected veto. Legislation stands unless it is intolerably unjust by broad historical consensus, which checks personal ideology.

Close: morality in judging is disciplined, not private, and refusing manifest injustice is fidelity to law, not mere conscience (TSH).

Section 3: "no practical need … always sufficient"

Concede that extremes are rare (E20.5). Most cases are routine, and in practice the master-rule point may not lead to much. But the law still leaves several lawful paths, above all for lawyers, and cannot choose between them: whether to plead a time bar against a just debt, how to advise on enforcement risk, whether to take the client, how hard to press a half-truth. Legality settles what may be done, not who answers for it, so even a junior following lawful instructions shares responsibility: Thunder's "lazy lawyer" takes for granted the standards of peers and superiors. The need for a disciplined morality is practical because it is daily, not dramatic.

Then E20.6 from "For lawyers": as Devlin says of the criminal law, law sets the minimum, not the maximum: "no man is worth much who regulates his conduct with the sole object of escaping punishment". Pepper's own premise implies the same: what is not yet unlawful is not thereby good. And Article 5(2) lets a special two-thirds majority amend most provisions, with no express core like the German Basic Law's art 79(3), so treating compliance as sufficient moral guidance hands judgement to whoever holds two-thirds.

Then the lawyering kit's verdict (E20.3): the lawyer's role is itself moral, and law sets its floor, not its ceiling. Above the floor, prudence, dialogue and rare objection decide hard cases.

Close: the law leaves several lawful paths and cannot choose between them. It is a floor, not a ceiling, and Pepper's own limits and Thunder's prudence both work above it. The realists add that the materials often leave the outcome open, as in Cheng San, where "outside" and "at or near" could be read either way, and Kennedy that how a judge works the materials depends on where he wants to come out. So the law alone cannot always be what guides.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is institutional restraint (E20.9). Rules have a core of meaning where morality sits idle (Hart), and deciding by personal morality would make each judge a law unto himself. Singapore has already decided: Article 4 makes the Constitution supreme; in Rajeevan the legislature rightfully determines rights absent specific guarantees; and in 2022 change came through Parliament. This shows that judges must not decide by private conviction and should presume validity. It does not show that law can be applied without theory or morality, or that intolerable injustice must stand.

My weak point (E20.12): my threshold relies on TSH's broad historical consensus. TSH admits a "catch": consensus can go "dreadfully wrong", decades on it could impose a worldwide trend, and among courts it may reflect only a few judges. I concede these and adopt her reply: the alternatives are worse, judges voiding whatever they think unjust, or positivism, which leaves no check on an autocrat, a few oligarchs or a brute majority; and the test binds courts only.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right that the Constitution settles who decides, and wrong that it settles how to decide. The theorists share the view that compliance is necessary; they split on what must be added: a theory for the open terms (TSH, Dworkin), a disciplined morality above the floor (Thunder, and Pepper's own limits), or candour about the choice (Hart). Repaired: “The Constitution settles who decides and by what tests; judges and lawyers still need a theory to read its open terms and a disciplined morality to choose between lawful courses.” For the judge, this means fit first and refusal only at intolerable injustice; for the lawyer, the law as a floor.

Variants. If the statement is about legality, certainty and justice, lead with the law-and-justice kit (Fuller first), then the judging and lawyering kits. If it says theory is idle in practice, use the theory kit (E20.2) and E20.7, with two examples. The Jaffa Cakes tribunal (United Biscuits) reasoned about what makes a cake a cake like philosophers, without citing one. And in Pepper's effluent case, where a limit of .050 g/l was in practice ignored up to .075, realism empties Pepper's own limit, since nothing presses the client to obey the law as written. If it says theory or morality is always in play, use essay E22. Watch: retelling positivism against natural law, or Pepper against Thunder, is marked down. Argue whether this regime makes the debates practically idle.

Using it in the hypo

The Minister's speech is the statement itself. On the privacy case, start with precedent and Lim Meng Suang's restraint. Reading "law" in Article 9 needs a theory whichever way you read it. A judge should refuse to apply a law only where it is intolerably unjust by broad historical consensus, and this is not such a case (Yong Vui Kong in 2015 refused to make rights from "whole cloth", E20.10).

Memory line

The Constitution settles who decides, not how; compliance is necessary, never always sufficient.

Sheet blocks to copy from

E20.1E35.4E35.5E20.8E20.4E20.5E20.6E20.3E20.9E20.12E20.10

  1. It treats judges and lawyers together through one inference.
  2. That is not the structure. The statement is a premise, an inference and a claim that compliance is always enough.
  3. Correct. Name the link each section tests.
  4. "Always" matters, but it sits in the last link.
Premise, inference, ground.
key ideaWhy is reading Art 9(1)'s "law" as "whatever is validly passed" circular?
  1. Disagreement is not circularity.
  2. That is not the circularity. The problem is that the reading assumes the answer to the question it should decide.
  3. Art 4 makes the Constitution supreme.
  4. Correct (TSH). If "law" in Article 9 meant whatever is validly passed, the test would assume its own answer, because whether an Act is valid depends on whether it breaches Article 9.
Validity depends on Art 9.
singaporeWhy does Art 5(2) matter to E20?
  1. Basic features were rejected in Teo Soh Lung.
  2. Correct (E20.6). A special two-thirds majority can amend most provisions, so treating compliance as enough hands moral judgement to whoever holds two-thirds.
  3. That is Part 4 of the Constitution, not Article 5(2).
  4. Article 5(2) sets the two-thirds majority for amendment; it does not require a referendum.

  1. Correct. The natural ally turns against it.
  2. Positivism does not entail the amoral judge.

  1. Correct: E20.12. And the test binds courts only.
  2. A worldwide trend is part of the catch, not the cure.
  3. TSH's test binds the courts only, so this misstates her reply.
  4. That is not her reply; she compares two worse alternatives.
Compared with what?
E33

Good lawyer, good person

■ highly likely

The question 2019-20 Q2 form

"A good lawyer can be a good person only as far as the legal system allows. The lawyer is a hired gun; what lawyers do is simply their job." Or: "Why should lawyers care about theory? Just do your job."

The hypo Hypo 10

Your partner says: "Leave your conscience at the door. We are all realists now. If the conduct rules don't forbid it, we do it." This week: a factory asks "the real limit" when its licence caps effluent at 0.050 g/l but readings to 0.075 are ignored; plead limitation against a debt the client admits; answer only what is asked when selling a house with painted-over termite damage; a murder client tells you where two victims are hidden, one perhaps alive. What do you do?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

The statement seals the lawyer's role off from morality. The flaws are an absolute ("only as far as") and self-refutation: even the defence of the amoral role is a moral argument. The marker wants Pepper and Thunder used as tools, not retold.

The answer in one breath

By a good person I mean someone whose conduct is morally defensible all things considered; by a good lawyer, one who does the role well. So the statement asks whether the role can require what a good person would refuse. Yet even Pepper defends the role morally, citing autonomy and equality. I reject the sealing and keep the role: it is moral, bounded by law, and dialogue comes before refusal.

Section 1: "just a job" or "a hired gun"

Start with separate compartments (E33.6). "Lawyers are like soldiers" is the "dramatic bifurcation" Thunder rejects. Pepper fears the moralising lawyer turns policeman, judge or deceiver; for Thunder the lawyer stays accountable as a human being. Thunder accepts role morality, as a public servant owes courtesy and compassion. So "no one can say what a virtuous lawyer would do" overstates: roles are not wholly idiosyncratic, and prudence applies the virtues.

Then Thunder from the lawyering kit (E20.3): a lawyer's role is justified only by its contribution to justice and flourishing. Merely pointing to the role does not explain it, and amoral lawyers become slaves of their clients' consciences. Prudence and tact decide cases above the floor, though Thunder concedes that virtue under-determines particular cases.

Close: Thunder's view is natural-teleological. Law is a means to ends naturally good for us, such as justice and public order, so lawyering is inherently normative. Its function is to further justice and the rule of law, not to follow the client's impulses or the letter of the law. Pepper's own image is a technician, arguably a particular kind of tool, one that lets clients exercise their own moral autonomy. A role is justified by what it is for, so it is no sealed-off world. Thunder's librarian cannot plead official policy, and a lawyer cannot plead the client's instructions alone.

Section 2: "as far as the system allows"

Pepper's defence of the amoral role is itself a moral argument (E20.3). Law is a public good; autonomy is "a moral good"; access to law needs lawyers, so screening clients by the lawyer's morality would mean rule by an oligarchy of lawyers and "second-class citizenship" for those refused. Conduct bad enough should be made explicitly unlawful; otherwise why impose "the happenstance" of one lawyer's conscience?

But Pepper's own limits undercut him (E33.1). His lawyer keeps four zones of autonomy: whether to practise at all, choice of client, moral dialogue with the client, and conscientious objection, an option always present. Together they form "a meaningful field". Morality comes through the door as part of the client, and the lawyer can be good, though uncomfortable. Spotting the "extreme cases" for objection means weighing morality throughout, as Radbruch's judge does. And positivism does not entail Pepper: it only identifies the law and leaves the moral decision.

Close: his own limits leave the lawyer a conscience the role cannot silence. Thunder adds that Pepper's view of autonomy is "implausibly demanding": a right of access to the law is not access "on whatever terms one pleases", and our legal projects may be "hampered" by others' ethical judgements, as our everyday ones are. And Pepper's limit, the law binding the lawyer, already includes the enforced conduct rules. Singapore makes the duty to the court overriding, though how far practice puts it above the client is debatable. So "as far as the system allows" already includes a duty beyond the client.

Section 3: "a good person"

The legal system sets what a lawyer may do, not how good the lawyer may be. Use the three scenarios from the lectures (E20.3) as a test, not a side. Ask in order: is the system by and large just? If so, is this rule intolerably unjust? If not, is this outcome unjust? Calling an unjust result the institution's wrong only moves the question. Then E20.6's sentence: law sets the minimum, not the maximum.

Close: Thunder's principles answer "a good person" directly. A good human life comes before the role's demands; roles are to be questioned rather than adopted uncritically; integrating them takes courage and moderation; and professional and personal excellence are interdependent, everyday virtue being "a sort of training ground". Above the floor, prudence, dialogue and rare objection decide. The hidden-bodies case shows the gap: where a client's confession reveals a victim who may still be alive, a Thunder lawyer may disclose it as part of the role, and a Pepper lawyer only as an extreme-case objection.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case depends on the statement's side.

  • Pepper's: access to law makes the lawyer "a good person", and the law, not the lawyer's morality, should limit client autonomy. Setting morality aside also shields unpopular clients from a society's prevailing morality. This shows lawful help is morally defensible. It does not show the role is amoral: his lawyer keeps four zones of autonomy.
  • Thunder's, if the statement attacks the role: Pepper's role is morally impotent for the sake of a moral principle, and its lawyer loses the right and duty to take some responsibility for what the work serves. That shows the amoral role has a moral cost. It does not show the lawyer must refuse: dialogue comes before refusal.

My weak point: my answer relies on Thunder, who admits disagreement on particular cases and that integrity is rarely if ever fully achieved. I concede that he guides character more than cases. So I send hard cases to dialogue first, and refuse only at the extremes.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is overstated. Pepper and Thunder are closer than their labels: Pepper keeps objection, and Thunder accepts role morality and that no legal system is perfect. They differ in mindset more than outcome, and the more robust the conduct rules, the narrower the gap. The live question is how often conscience may override the role: rarely and openly, after dialogue, where serving the client would subvert the law's point. Repaired: “The legal system sets what a lawyer may do, not how good the lawyer may be; within it, choice of client, dialogue and objection remain.” For the lawyer, this means answering for the choice of client and cause, not sheltering behind the role.

If the statement names something narrower

The sheet has a pivot for each: training and outlook (E33.2: "leave your conscience at the door" describes an outlook lawyers are trained into; it does not justify it); rules only (E33.3: conduct rules presuppose a purpose, and a norm exploited against justice is subverted); inequality (E33.4: the amoral role serves equality, but the rich already buy more law); advising on enforcement risk (E33.5: if law is only what officials will do, Pepper's limit is emptied); the guilty client (E33.7: defending the accused is the role working); and conduct rules, dialogue and leaving (E33.8: the lawyer as "moral educator"; leaving practice is a weak answer).

Using it in the hypo

In the effluent case, tell the client both figures, the limit and the level actually enforced, but do not encourage a breach (E33.5). On the time bar, pleading it is lawful, but talk with the client about paying the just debt (E20.5). On the termites, half-truths in a sale may be misrepresentation (E33.8). With the hidden victims, a Thunder lawyer may disclose as part of the role, and a Pepper lawyer only as an extreme-case objection. Calling yourself a "realist" is itself an unexamined theory (E33.2).

Memory line

The system sets what a lawyer may do, not how good the lawyer may be; dialogue before refusal.

How these essays fit together

E20 covers judges and lawyers together, against the claim that the Constitution makes theory and morality unnecessary. E33 is about lawyers alone. They share the lawyering kit (E20.3) and the floor-not-ceiling pivot (E20.6). If both are on one paper, give E20 the judge material (Article 9's readings, TSH's threshold, the restraint best case) and a single sentence on lawyers, and give E33 the full Pepper and Thunder material and the three scenarios. Write Devlin's "no man is worth much" line once.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • E20: the statement is itself a legal philosophy; Hart rejects its conclusion.
  • The Constitution settles who decides, not what its open terms mean; Article 9's "law" has four readings.
  • Morality in judging is disciplined, not private: fit, public reasons, broad historical consensus.
  • Extremes are rare, but lawful choices are daily: law is a floor, not a ceiling.
  • Repaired: judges and lawyers still need a theory for the open terms and a disciplined morality above the floor.
  • E33: the role is moral, and even Pepper's defence is a moral argument.
  • Pepper's four zones; Thunder: the role is justified by what it is for.
  • Three scenarios: just system? intolerable rule? unjust outcome?
  • Repaired: the system sets what a lawyer may do, not how good the lawyer may be.

Video 11Only the law, or philosophy everywhereJudges and lawyers

E21

The judge who only applies the law

■ highly likely

The question 2018-19 Q2

"Judges do not, and should not, bring any substantive moral judgement to bear; they are concerned only with what the law is. In a constitutional democracy this is desirable."

The hypo Hypo 23(c)

A 1950 by-law bans "vehicles" from parks. One man is fined for riding an electric unicycle; another for placing a decommissioned army truck as a war memorial. Justice Koh says: "I simply find out what the people who made the rule meant, and apply it. Anything else is the judge's politics." How should the appeals be decided, and is Justice Koh right?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

The statement first describes, then praises. The description fails at the word "any"; the praise fails because the judge it imagines is not available. "Any" and "only" are absolutes.

The answer in one breath

By moral judgement I mean a judgement of what is right or just, as distinct from what the sources say. By desirable I mean better for a constitutional democracy than the alternatives. Judges bring moral judgement to bear always as a presupposition and, in hard cases, as a ground. So a constitutional democracy should want a judge whose morality is disciplined by fit, candour and restraint, not a judge without one.

Section 1: "any moral judgement"

Even Hart's judge must decide penumbral cases by what the law ought to be. Use the judging kit (E20.1). Hart: rules have a core of settled meaning where morality sits idle, but in the penumbra the judge may have to reach beyond the law. Dworkin: law is interpretive, so morality enters every proposition of law. The realists: canons and precedents read narrowly or widely leave hard cases open, and even a clear rule's outcome depends on which facts the court accepts (fact scepticism). But a judge must justify, not merely predict.

Then two sentences from E20.7. Harris reports Llewellyn: a lawyer steeped in an appellate court's ways could predict outcomes "eight times out of ten". And Dworkin concedes that fit often yields no unique reading, but a judge who argues openly for the soundest principle is not pretending.

Close: easy cases are easy because judges share a theory; hard cases are where it is argued.

Section 2: "only with what the law is"

Deciding what the law is already takes a theory of law, and even a positivist must make moral judgements where the law adopts moral standards, as the rights chapter of the Constitution does. One sentence of positivism: it identifies law by its sources, and leaves the moral decision open.

Then TSH from the judging kit: a supreme Constitution relocates the debate. Article 4 is a master rule only because it is accepted, and the rights chapter's "law", "life", "liberty" and "equal" are contested moral terms. The kit's example: Lim Meng Suang's restraint is itself a theory of judging. And a trap: positivism only identifies law and leaves the moral decision, so it does not entail an amoral judge.

Close: in Singapore the Constitution's moral terms make that theory unavoidable (TSH), and Lim Meng Suang's restraint is itself one: the Court of Appeal defended it by citing Dworkin's theory of adjudication. And in Riggs v Palmer, Elmer, who had poisoned his grandfather so that he would not change the will, sought to inherit under it. Earl and Gray agreed that the statute bound them, and split over what it required.

Section 3: "desirable"

The right ideal is a judge whose morality is presupposed always and operative rarely (block E21.3). Morality is always presupposed: in legality, in the reasons to defer to clear words, and in every claim that a ruling is correct. It is operative, a ground of decision, only in hard cases and at intolerable injustice. For Hart it enters at most where rules run out; for Fuller, in fidelity joined to the judge's responsibility for making law what it ought to be; for Dworkin, in every proposition of law.

The democratic objection, that unelected judges are no "mini-legislature", is a reason for restraint, not for a judge without a morality. Concede its core: in 2022 change on s 377A came through Parliament, not the courts. In McLoughlin, a claim for psychiatric harm by a mother who came on the aftermath of an accident to her family, Lord Scarman said the court's function is "to adjudicate according to principle, leaving policy curtailment to the judgment of Parliament". So even deference rests on a moral view of the court's job.

Then Hart on law and justice (E20.4): validity leaves open the final moral question of obedience. Then the twin vices (from E21.1): every role can fail twice, by doing others' work or by refusing its own. Then TSH: in a stable democracy the Radbruch formula is a narrow check, not an unelected veto; legislation stands unless intolerably unjust by broad historical consensus.

Close: a judge who only applies the law fails just where a constitution is needed. Constitutionalism protects citizens from unreasonable legislatures, and the Court of Appeal's view that an extreme provision "would probably not be enacted by a reasonable Parliament" is a prediction, not a reason (TSH). Restraint without candour abdicates, and candour without restraint usurps: TSH's twin vices.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is restraint (E20.9). Rules have a core of meaning where morality sits idle (Hart), and deciding by personal morality would make each judge a law unto himself. Singapore has decided: Article 4 makes the Constitution supreme; in Rajeevan the legislature rightfully determines rights absent specific guarantees; and in 2022 change came through Parliament. This shows judges must not decide by private conviction and should presume validity. It does not show law can be applied without theory or morality.

My weak point (E21.4): my answer relies on Dworkin. A judge's best reading rests on beliefs other judges need not share, and fit will often yield no unique interpretation: whose political morality decides? I concede that no answer is demonstrable. But the choice is political, not personal: it must be justified in public reasons, it is tested on appeal, and it starts from the text, not the goal.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right about the ideal of restraint and wrong about the judge who could meet it. Hart, Dworkin, the realists and TSH agree that a judge's morality enters somewhere. They split on where: the penumbra (Hart), every proposition of law (Dworkin), the reading of facts and canons (the realists), or the Constitution's moral terms (TSH). Repaired: “Judges should not decide by personal conviction; they bring political morality to bear through fit and candour, and override the law only at intolerable injustice.” For the judge, this means fit first, candour always, and refusal only at intolerable injustice.

If the statement sets "according to the law" against justice, use essay E27. Watch: Hart appears in every section once the steelman is counted, so give him one sentence in section 1. There is no lawyer limb.

Using it in the hypo

Purpose may settle whether a unicycle is a "vehicle". The memorial truck is harder: it needs a judgement about the rule's point, which is more than the drafters' intention. Koh's "simply apply the law" hides a theory of interpretation (E22.2). The answer is fit and candour, not politics.

Memory line

Presupposed always, operative rarely: disciplined, not absent, morality.

  4. The description fails first, at "any".

  3. That is Dworkin's strong view, which E21.3 qualifies.

  2. Part 4 adopts moral standards, so the judge must judge morally.
  3. The praised judge is not available: theory and morality are presupposed.
  4. Lim Meng Suang showed restraint, and the Court defended it by citing Dworkin's theory of adjudication.
Restraint, not amorality.
E22

All legal argument turns on philosophy

▲ likely

The question 2016-17 Q1

"All legal argument and judgement turns on legal philosophy, moral judgement and justice, even where precedent is heavy." To what extent, in what sense, and with what implications for lawyers and for adjudication?

The hypo Hypo 8, Moonbites

A tax tribunal must decide whether "Moonbites" are cakes (zero-rated) or biscuits: soft when fresh, hard when stale, sold in the biscuit aisle. A columnist writes: "Legal theory is a luxury: a good dictionary decides it." Evaluate.

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

"All" has two readings: theory as a presupposition, or morality as the ground of every decision. The statement is true in the first sense and false in the second. The three limbs set your three sections.

The answer in one breath

The statement is right in a sense it does not choose. By legal argument I mean any argument about what the law requires, in court or in advice. By philosophy I mean a theory of what law is and what the legal roles permit, with the moral views those theories carry. "All" is true of theory as a presupposition and false of morality as the ground of every decision. Every legal argument presupposes a theory of law and a moral view of the role, but philosophy and morality become operative only in hard cases and at intolerable injustice. The implication is disciplined roles, not a licence.

Section 1: "to what extent"

Every legal argument turns on philosophy as a presupposition, and only some turn on it as a ground. Run the judging kit in reverse: Dworkin first (law is interpretive, so morality enters every proposition of law), then Hart (only in the penumbra), then TSH (the Constitution's moral terms). Then "presupposed, not always operative" (E21.3).

Close: philosophy is operative only at the margins, in hard cases and at intolerable injustice. Riggs shows both: every judge accepted the statute's authority, and rival theories of law decided the case.

Section 2: "in what sense"

In the sense of a theory of what counts as law, not a philosophy cited in court. Run the theory kit (E20.2) in reverse. Dworkin: easy cases are easy because lawyers share a theory; hard cases turn on rival ones. Positivism: identifying law involves an evaluation, since a lawyer must accept or reject the separation of law and morality before saying what the law requires. Fish: marshalling rules is purposive; without a sense of the general project, rehearsing them is "an empty gesture", though Rawls cited in court will likely seem "window dressing". Then E20.7: Llewellyn's "eight times out of ten".

Close: easy cases are easy because lawyers share that theory. The Jaffa Cakes tribunal, deciding for tax purposes whether they are cakes or biscuits, reasoned about cakeness like philosophers without citing one: it held them cakes, since stale cakes harden and biscuits soften. Even to call an argument weak takes a view of what it is for, since you cannot know that a thing is not being done well until you know what is being done.

Section 3: implications

For lawyers, the law is a floor and dialogue comes before refusal. Use the lawyering kit (E20.3): Pepper or Thunder, then the three scenarios (is the system by and large just; if so, is this rule intolerably unjust; if not, is this outcome unjust?). Whether to plead a time bar against a just debt is lawful either way; the choice is a moral one the law leaves open.

For adjudication, use "stay within your role" (E21.1): the twin vices of usurpation and abdication, then George and TSH or Fuller in a clause. George, as TSH reports him: at least in a basically just system, officials must respect the limits of their authority. TSH asks who decides whether those limits were prudently drawn. Fuller: fidelity is owed to law, not to rank.

Close: for judges, it means fit and candour, and a guard against both usurpation and abdication. Dworkin's chain novelist shows why: each judge both interprets and creates, and his duty is to interpret the legal history he finds, not to invent a better one.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

Here the steelman is the case for the statement (E22.1): indeterminacy and the Constitution's moral terms. Rules are picked or construed to fit results reached on other grounds, as in Cheng San ("outside" against "at or near"), and each principle meets a counterprinciple (Unger). The rights chapter's terms are moral: equality is empty without a higher moral law, and a natural-law judge refusing manifest injustice is faithful to law (TSH). This shows law does not decide every case mechanically. It does not show that law decides none, that fidelity is a pretence, or that natural law wins: a positivist may follow moral terms outward.

My weak point (the own-side line written in E28): my line between presupposed and operative may look like having it both ways. I concede that it blurs in hard cases, where a judge's view of law is what decides. But it marks a real difference: in an easy case a judge with another theory would reach the same result; in a hard case he might not, and that is where morality becomes a ground.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right that no legal argument is philosophy-free, and wrong that philosophy decides every case. Dworkin, Hart, Fish, Pepper and Thunder agree that practice presupposes some theory; they split on how often it decides: always (Dworkin), at the margins (Hart), or above the floor of the role (Pepper and Thunder). Repaired: “Every legal argument presupposes a theory of law and a view of the role; moral judgement decides only where the sources run out or the law is intolerably unjust.” For lawyers and judges alike, this means disciplined roles, not a licence.

Variants. If the statement names justice and says "even when" precedent is heavy, keep the three limbs, add the law-and-justice kit to section 1, and "it depends on the source" (E22.2) to section 2, since precedent still needs a test of likeness: at common law only a principle says which differences matter. If the statement is about judges deciding on common law and statutes, use E28. If it says theory guides practice, including the justice of the case, with a soundness limb, use E29 and the soundness card (E22.3). Watch: five names at most.

Using it in the hypo

Moonbites is the Jaffa Cakes case: the tribunal looked at core attributes, made comparisons, ran thought experiments and in effect built a philosophy of "cakeness". A dictionary lists the factors but cannot weigh them. So theory is presupposed in ordinary legal work, not a luxury.

Memory line

All argument presupposes theory; only hard cases make it decide.

Sheet blocks to copy from

E20.1E21.3E20.2E20.7E22.2E20.4E20.3E20.5E21.1E22.1E22.3

  2. Correct. Theory is always presupposed in legal argument, but morality decides only at the margins, in hard cases and at intolerable injustice.

  3. Common law needs a principle of likeness.
  4. Treating like cases alike needs a test of likeness.

How these essays fit together

E21 and E22 are the same distinction seen from opposite ends. E21 denies that a judge can avoid moral judgement; E22 denies that moral judgement decides everything. Both rest on "presupposed always, operative rarely" (E21.3) and on the judging kit (E20.1). If both are on one paper, give E21 the judge alone (Hart's penumbra, TSH, the twin vices) and give E22 the theory kit, Fish, the Jaffa Cakes, and the lawyer limb. Riggs goes to one of them only.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • E21: describes, then praises. "Any" fails; the judge it praises is not available.
  • Hart's penumbra; Dworkin's interpretation; the realists' fact scepticism; but judges justify, not predict.
  • Even restraint is a theory: Lim Meng Suang cited Dworkin.
  • Presupposed always, operative rarely; twin vices: usurp or abdicate.
  • E22: true of theory as presupposition, false of morality as the ground of every decision.
  • The Jaffa Cakes: philosophy of cakeness, no philosopher cited.
  • Implications: for lawyers a floor; for judges fit, candour and the chain novel.
  • Repaired: theory always presupposed; morality decides only where sources run out or injustice is intolerable.

Video 12When judges disagree, and Dworkin's right answerJudges and lawyers

E30

Disagreement about what the law is

The question exam-style

"Judges and lawyers never really disagree about what the law is, only about what it ought to be, or whether to follow it. Where the words are plain, there is no law left to argue about."

The hypo Hypo 8, Anil

The Succession Act gives an intestate estate "in equal shares to the deceased's children". Anil helped his terminally ill father die at his request, a crime for which he was fined, and claims the estate. Justice Lim: the words are plain; adding exceptions is for Parliament. Justice Rahman: no one may profit from his own wrong, and that principle is law. Justice Tan: precedent has run out, so the court must make law on policy. A columnist says the judges disagreed only about whether to obey the law. Whose approach would you adopt, and is the columnist right?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

"Never" and "only" are absolutes, and the statement offers a false choice: either agreement about law, or disagreement about morals or obedience. It leaves out a third kind of disagreement: about the grounds of law themselves.

The answer in one breath

By what the law is I mean the grounds of law, the tests that make a proposition of law true, not only whether those tests are met. Positivism's two theses, law as social fact and no necessary link with morality, are coherent. But positivism must still choose whose recognition counts, and in hard cases judges who agree that the law binds them split over what it is. So the statement is only partly right: lawyers usually agree, and where they do not, the disagreement is about law's grounds, not about what the law ought to be or whether to obey it.

Section 1: "only about what it ought to be"

The statement mistakes a disagreement about law's grounds for one about its merits. Dworkin separates three issues in a lawsuit: fact, law, and the twinned issues of political morality and fidelity. The statement folds the second into the third.

Use the block on theoretical disagreement (E30.1). Empirical disagreement asks whether agreed grounds of law are met. Theoretical disagreement disputes the grounds themselves. In Riggs all the judges accepted the law's authority but split over what it required; in McLoughlin the Lords agreed on the result but split over whether policy may limit liability. The disagreement gets glossed over in three ways: by the "plain-fact" view, which recasts it as a dispute about what the law should be; by the public debate over whether judges "discover" or "invent" law; and by theories that assume all lawyers share the same criteria for "law". For Dworkin, positivism must paint these judges as "well-meaning liars", or as simpletons arguing over borderline cases, yet the cases were "pivotal". Add Dworkin's first sentence from the judging kit: law is interpretive, so judges read the legal record by fit and political value.

Close: if lawyers used different criteria for "law", Earl and Gray would be talking past each other, like two people arguing about banks, one meaning savings banks and the other riverbanks, and even their agreements would be fake. Which arguments count as law is the point in dispute.

Section 2: "plain words"

In Riggs nobody doubted the words; they doubted what statute the words had made. Then one row from the card (E30.2), the two readings of Riggs, from the lectures: either both sides argued about law, the majority's argument including common-law principle, so Dworkin overstates; or the dispute was whether law is always subject to universal moral principles, as Earl's "universal law" suggests.

Close: Dworkin shows that neither reading was mechanical. Gray's protected testators' certainty and the principle that penalties are fixed in advance. Earl's asked what the legislators would have rejected had they foreseen the case, and read the statute against general principles, since law should be coherent in principle. I side with Earl. The maxim was already part of the common law, so even a positivist can call it law, and reading the statute without it would turn the statute against its own purpose. Nor did it add a penalty: it took "from him no property", but held that he "shall not acquire property by his crime". Neither side kept to the statute's four corners.

Section 3: precedent

Precedent needs a test of which differences matter. At common law only a principle says which differences matter (E22.2): whether a mother reaching hospital two hours later is like one at the scene turns on foreseeability or physical risk.

Then policy or principle (E30.3). Saying "the judge legislates once precedent runs out" mistakes a theoretical disagreement about law's grounds for a gap. In McLoughlin all five Lords allowed the appeal, yet split: Scarman held line-drawing policy "not justiciable"; Lord Edmund-Davies called that novel and startling. Lord Wilberforce's policy heads were a flood of claims, damages out of proportion to fault with their burden on insurers, harder proof and longer litigation, and deference to the legislature. Scarman put such policy beyond the courtroom. Wilberforce lists disproportion as policy, but on my reading it is principle, and Lord Bridge answered it as a matter of "moral responsibility". So leaving floodgates to Parliament is Scarman's contested view, which I defend as a choice, not as the case's lesson; counsel should run both arguments.

Close: the Court of Appeal below had found the harm foreseeable but, seeing no law beyond the precedents, treated its task as repair and drew the line by policy: floodgates, fraud, insurance cost. Bridge answered that stopping at the furthest point the cases had reached would be "an unwarranted abdication". So a disagreement about precedent is a disagreement about law, not about what the law ought to be.

The judicial role

Add "presupposed, not always operative" (E21.3): morality is always presupposed in judging, and operative only in hard cases and at intolerable injustice.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is clarity (E37.7). Law lies in legislation, cases and custom, which anyone can check; lawyers must know it before reforming it; and positivism arose in states with no shared moral base. Austin's candour adds that law's existence is one thing and its merit another, so calling law a fact keeps criticism honest. This shows that sources usually identify law. It does not show that identifying it is value-free.

My weak point (E37.8): describing is not praising (Hart), and Dworkin is arguably unfair to positivism, which need not make every disagreement empirical; "soft" positivism lets the rule of recognition adopt moral tests. I concede it: the evaluation I mean is about what counts as law, not approval of it.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is partly right. Positivism rightly ties law to sources. Positivists, Dworkin and the realists agree that Riggs and McLoughlin were hard; they split on why: a gap for discretion, rival theories of law's grounds, or results reached first. But naming law is an evaluation, and rival theories split Riggs and the Lords' reasons in McLoughlin, so theory is not optional. Weak natural law earns its keep only at extreme injustice; Riggs needed only a choice of theory about law's grounds. Repaired: “Judges and lawyers usually agree on what the law is; where they do not, the disagreement is about law's grounds, not about what it ought to be or whether to follow it.” For the judge, this means defending a view of law's grounds openly, since the plain words will not do it for him.

Using it in the hypo

Lim plays Gray, who would apply the words; Rahman plays Earl, who reads them with the maxim; Tan shows the positivist's gap. The columnist holds the plain-fact view. All three judges accept the Act's authority, so they disagree about what the law is, not whether to follow it. Side with Rahman, and note that a mercy killing differs from Elmer's motive, which tests how far the maxim reaches.

Memory line

Not merits, not defiance: a dispute about law's grounds. Savings banks and riverbanks.
