E31

Dworkin under test: the right answer

The question exam-style

"In a hard case there is one right answer, which the judge declares rather than makes. Principles are part of the law; policies are not."

The hypo Hypo 23(b)

A mother watches, on a live video call, her daughter being struck by a negligent driver 200 km away, and suffers psychiatric illness. Precedent allows recovery at the scene or its immediate aftermath; no case concerns live video. Counsel for the driver says the court must not "legislate" and warns of a flood of claims. How should the appeal be decided?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

The statement offers a choice between finding the law and making it, and Dworkin refuses both. "One right answer" has two readings: an aim that disciplines the judge, or a result provable to all. Test Dworkin against his rivals, the realists, the crits and Kennedy.

The answer in one breath

By one right answer Dworkin means the reading that best fits and justifies the legal record, not a result provable to all. Interpretation neither finds law ready-made nor invents it, so one reading can be best, and the judge must justify his ruling by it. But fit often yields no unique reading, and then political morality decides. So "one right answer" holds as an aim that disciplines the judge, not as a result he can demonstrate.

Section 1: "declares, does not make"

Dworkin refuses the choice between finding law and making it. The declaratory theory, that judges only discover law already there, has long been out of favour, yet it holds some truth: a judge making new law always claims to draw it from a principle, maxim or precedent already there. Dworkin explains why. Propositions of law are neither "pieces of history", as positivists hold, nor statements of what the speaker wants. They are interpretations of legal history, at once descriptive and evaluative.

Then the chain novel (E31.1). The artist interprets while creating, and the critic creates while interpreting, yet the two remain different. Picture a novel written by a chain of authors, each writing one chapter. Each novelist after the first both interprets and creates, and after the second no single author's intentions decide. So the judge, a partner in a chain enterprise, must interpret the legal history he finds, not invent a better one. Fit has "no algorithm", and value is political, not aesthetic.

Close: the judge neither declares a ready-made answer nor writes a new one; he continues the story he inherits.

Section 2: "one right answer"

Where fit leaves several readings open, substance chooses between them. Dworkin's own example is an aunt who hears by telephone that her niece has been injured in an accident, and suffers shock. Two principles fit the earlier cases: liability only within the zone of physical risk, and liability for foreseeable damage. Foreseeability is "probably a better interpretation" only because it states a sounder principle of justice.

Then Dworkin's cell from the kit on adjudication as politics (E23.1): law is deeply political but never personal or partisan. Where several readings fit, political morality decides, and the judge must justify his ruling by the reading that best fits and justifies the record, owing a duty to the history he finds.

Close: Dworkin sees "no obvious reason" to doubt that one reading can be "best of all". Terence Siow, a prosecution appeal against probation for molestation on public transport, shows the method at work: the court did not ignore Boaz Koh but reconciled it, as one where the need for deterrence overrode a strong prospect of reform. But Dworkin concedes that fit often yields no unique answer, so "one right answer" is an aim that disciplines the judge, not a result he can demonstrate.

Section 3: "principle, not policy"

Dworkin keeps judges to principle and leaves policy to the legislature. By principle I mean a requirement of justice, fairness or some other dimension of morality; by policy, a social or economic goal of the community. This is his rights thesis, which Lim Meng Suang echoed in leaving extra-legal arguments to Parliament (TSH). Use the first part of E30.3: Scarman's view that line-drawing policy is "not justiciable". Yet the chain-novel essay itself asks for the best "principle or policy" a body of law serves, so the bar is less sharp in his text than in the statement.

Close: the line holds as a discipline, but where it falls is itself argued.

Rivals

The realists say reasons follow results; the crits, that each principle meets a counterprinciple; and Kennedy, that the judge works the legal field toward how he wants to come out. The crits call Dworkin's belief in an intelligible order within the legal materials "objectivism", and Kennedy answers "always a right answer" with cases where he gave the law its shape "as a matter of my free ethical or political choice". The reply, as Harris puts it, is that a judge must justify, not predict, and Kennedy's own judge sometimes meets a rule he "can't budge". Both Dworkin and Kennedy work inside inherited materials, but Dworkin starts from the text, Kennedy from the outcome. Neither picture can be proved, since no one sees inside another judge's mind; the fair test is which is more persuasive as an account of the work and more useful as a guide.

Soundness

From the soundness card (E22.3): fit leaves readings open, so value is the judge's politics. Dworkin concedes no unique answer, not that law is personal or partisan.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is Riggs: all the judges accepted the law's authority yet split over what it required, and the plain-fact view must call them "well-meaning liars". A judge who says he found the answer is describing what he does, not pretending. This shows that hard cases are argued as questions of law. It does not show that the answer can be demonstrated to those who disagree.

My weak point (E21.4, from its question): whose political morality decides? I concede that no answer is demonstrable. But the choice is political, not personal: it must be justified in public reasons, it is tested on appeal, and it starts from the text, not the goal.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right as an aim and wrong as a result. Dworkin rightly refuses the choice between finding and making law, and rightly treats hard cases as argued about law. Dworkin and his rivals agree that rules leave some cases open; they split on what closes them: principle (Dworkin), the judge's reading of facts (the realists), ideology (the crits) or the judge's project (Kennedy). Repaired: “In a hard case the judge must seek the reading that best fits and justifies the law, and defend it as the right answer; that reading is political, not personal, but not provably unique.” For the judge, this means fit first and candour always.

Using it in the hypo

Is a live video like being at the immediate aftermath? A principle decides which differences matter, foreseeability or physical proximity. The floodgates worry is policy, which Scarman would leave to Parliament, so run both arguments (E30.3). The judge is neither "legislating" nor deciding mechanically.

Memory line

Not found, not invented: continued, like a chain novel. One right answer as an aim, not a proof.

How these essays fit together

E30 is the diagnosis: judges disagree about law's grounds, and Riggs and McLoughlin prove it. E31 is Dworkin's cure: the chain novel, fit and value, and principle over policy. They share the policy-or-principle block (E30.3) and both cases. If both are on one paper, give E30 the cases in full and the three ways disagreement is glossed over; give E31 the chain novel, the aunt, and the rivals, and mention Riggs only as its best case.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • E30: judges disagree about law's grounds, not merely its merits or whether to obey.
  • Empirical versus theoretical disagreement; "well-meaning liars"; savings banks and riverbanks.
  • Riggs: nobody doubted the words, only what statute they made; I side with Earl.
  • McLoughlin: same result, different reasons; policy or principle; Bridge's "unwarranted abdication".
  • E31: neither found nor invented; the chain novel; fit has "no algorithm".
  • The aunt: foreseeability wins because it is the sounder principle.
  • Principle, not policy, but the line itself is argued.
  • Rivals: realists, crits, Kennedy; Dworkin starts from the text, Kennedy from the outcome.
  • Repaired: seek and defend the best reading; political, not personal; not provably unique.

Video 13Is judging just politicsJudges and lawyers

E23

Moral reasoning, the Constitution and judicial limits

▲ likely

The question 2021-22 Q2 · 2012-13 Q2

"Moral reasoning in judging is inevitable. Therefore, there is no reason in principle for judges to refuse to read a right to abortion into the Constitution, no matter what the text says, where that is crucial to the paramount interest of equality." (2012-13: "Adjudication is essentially politics; judges' claims of fidelity to law and justice are pretences.")

The hypo Hypo 6, judge limb

Parliament cuts the limit for termination for any reason from 24 to 12 weeks. A woman challenges it as depriving her of personal liberty otherwise than "in accordance with law" and denying equal protection. As a judge, would you strike it down?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

The first sentence is largely right. The second is a permission, not a duty, and "therefore" is the gap: that judges must reason morally does not tell them what to reason about. "No matter what the text says" is an absolute.

The answer in one breath

Concede the first sentence: judges cannot avoid moral reasoning. By moral reasoning I mean reasoning about what is right or just. By "no reason in principle" I mean that nothing in the judge's role itself counts against reading the right in. Everything turns on "therefore". There are reasons in principle against reading in a right whatever the text says. Fit binds the judge to the legal record he inherits; the role leaves contested moral questions to Parliament; and only intolerable injustice overrides the text. Nor does equality settle the question, since both sides claim it.

Section 1: "inevitable"

The premise is right. Even reading the text first is a moral choice about the judge's role. Concede it in one paragraph, from "presupposed, not always operative" (E21.3): morality is always presupposed in judging, in legality, in the reasons to defer to clear words, and in every claim that a ruling is correct; it is operative only in hard cases and at intolerable injustice.

Close: the premise is right about presupposition, not about licence.

Section 2: "therefore … whatever the text says"

Dworkin, the natural ally, binds the judge to the history he finds. From the kit on adjudication as politics (E23.1): law is deeply political but never personal or partisan; where several readings fit, political morality decides, but the judge owes a duty to the history he finds, and fit often yields no unique answer.

Then Singapore: in a 2015 case the Court of Appeal held that courts cannot make a natural-law right out of "whole cloth". Then TSH's threshold (E20.4): in a stable democracy the Radbruch formula is a narrow check, not an unelected veto; legislation stands unless intolerably unjust by broad historical consensus.

Close: the abortion limits Roe struck down in 1973 did not meet TSH's threshold. Even some supporters of permissive laws thought they should have been enacted by legislatures: the decision was seen as an encroachment on legislative mandate through "the creative reading of a constitutional provision" (TSH). The statement's licence is the alternative TSH rejects, striking down laws that are unjust but not manifestly so, which "lends to temerity" in judges without the people's mandate.

Section 3: "equality … paramount"

Equality cuts both ways: the woman's equal standing and the unborn's equal worth. Which prevails turns on who counts, a moral question Article 12's text leaves open. Article 9's "person" leaves it open too, which is why personhood is contested in the United States, as in Roe. A right read in would settle it by judicial decision, which is Finnis's charge against Rawls: leaving the choice to each woman has already answered the metaphysical question. From Finnis's cell in the right-to-decide kit: where a choice affects another's basic good, he asks why Rawls draws reciprocity's boundary at birth.

Then Lim Meng Suang: its restraint is itself a theory of judging. And the twin vices (E21.2): TSH names both for judges. In bad faith they would legislate judicially or rubber-stamp whatever Parliament passes. Lord Bridge called stopping at decided cases "an unwarranted abdication".

Close: Lim Meng Suang left such questions to Parliament; refusing them all is abdication, deciding them all is usurpation. In Singapore the right is not even "crucial": statute already allows termination to 24 weeks, so reading a right in would only entrench it against Parliament. Lim Meng Suang's own deference was defended by a theory of adjudication, so restraint is no escape from moral reasoning either. Even the formula TSH adopts, which calls equality "the core of justice", lets it defeat a statute only at an intolerable level or where it is "deliberately betrayed".

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is Dworkin's own. "Equal" states an abstract principle, and the framers' concrete beliefs about what it covers do not bind (E35.2): a drafter may intend equal treatment in the abstract and believe a practice consistent with it, and which description governs is a political question. So the text alone cannot bar the right. TSH goes further at the extreme: constitutional supremacy rests on equal moral worth, so even an amendment allowing racial genocide would fall, whatever its text. This shows that the text does not decide alone. It does not show that the legal record can be ignored: fit includes the statute allowing termination, Lim Meng Suang's deference and the 2015 refusal to make rights from "whole cloth". For an indeterminacy version, use E22.1 instead.

My weak point (E20.12): my threshold relies on TSH's broad historical consensus, which she admits can go "dreadfully wrong". I adopt her reply: the alternatives are worse, and the test binds courts only.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right about inevitability and wrong about licence. Dworkin, TSH and Finnis agree that judges reason morally; they split on what binds that reasoning: fit to the legal record (Dworkin), a high threshold (TSH), or the prior question of who counts (Finnis). Repaired: “Judges cannot avoid moral reasoning, but fit and role bind it: they may read the Constitution's moral terms in light of the legal record, and set the text aside only at intolerable injustice, a threshold these limits did not meet (TSH).” For the judge, this means reading text-first and refusing only at intolerable injustice.

If the statement simply says adjudication is politics, lead with the full politics kit (E23.1), then the law-and-justice kit. If it also calls fidelity and justice a pretence and ends on staying in role, use E26. Watch: the twin-vices block already contains the twin-vices cell, so never write both.

Using it in the hypo

Reading "law" in Article 9 as whatever is validly passed is circular, but the other readings (fair procedure, nothing arbitrary) are satisfied here (E35.4). Under Article 12, Lim Meng Suang asks only for an intelligible line rationally related to the law's object (E35.3). A 12-week limit is not intolerably unjust by broad historical consensus, so uphold it.

Memory line

Inevitable is not a licence: fit, role and a high threshold bind the moral reasoning judges cannot avoid.

  2. Hart's discretion is interstitial, and fit includes the Act and Lim Meng Suang.
  3. Yong Vui Kong refused to make natural-law rights from "whole cloth".
  4. Realism describes; it gives no reason to read in the right.
Abstract principle, concrete record.
E32

Realists and crits: coherence or contradiction

The question exam-style

Form 1: "Rules do not decide cases; judges converge only because they share the same backgrounds." Form 2: "Contract law is a coherent set of principles with only a few exceptions." Form 3: "Critical views of law are destructive and of no practical use."

The hypo Hypo 9

A study of 10,000 District Court decisions finds similar cases decided differently by different judges; a think-tank concludes "Law is just a prediction of what judges will do. To change the law, change the bench." Judge Wong wants to rule for strikers lying across a depot entrance though trespass plainly applies, and a colleague calls fairness doctrines "exceptions that prove the rule" of freedom of contract. Evaluate the think-tank, and say what Judge Wong may do.

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

Each form has its own intro and uses two sections, never all three. Form 1 uses sections 1 and 3; form 2 uses section 2 (coherent) and 3; form 3 uses section 2 (of no use) and 3. The flaw in each is an absolute: rules decide nothing, doctrine is coherent, critique is useless.

The answer in one breath

Form 1: by "rules do not decide" the realists mean that rules alone do not fix the outcome, not that rules play no part. Realists rightly show that canons and precedents leave some cases open, and crits that principles meet counterprinciples. But a judge cannot predict his own ruling; he must justify it (Harris), and the best justification is the reading that best fits and justifies the law (Dworkin). And judges share a background largely because they share the rules. So the statement is overstated: political morality, not preference, closes the open cases.

Form 2: by coherent I mean that a body of principles fits and justifies the rules, so that each exception is explained by a principle. The crits show that each principle meets a counterprinciple and that fit is conservative. But competing principles are not chaos: doctrine is coherent enough to argue from, not enough to decide every case.

Form 3: by critical views I mean the claim that doctrine hides political choices and legitimates hierarchy. They are of use: they show where a choice was made, and that fit favours the status quo. Their limit is that exposing a choice does not say how to make it. Useful as a diagnosis, not as a programme.

Section 1: "rules do not decide" (form 1)

The realists are right that rules leave some cases open, and wrong that they leave all. From the politics kit (E23.1): rules are construed different ways to fit results reached on other grounds, and reasons follow results. But Llewellyn called realism a method, nothing more, and a flimsy judgment is costly: reputation, reversal, time and credibility keep judges close to the rules. From the judging kit: even a clear rule's outcome depends on which facts the court accepts, but a judge must justify, not predict. And Llewellyn's report: a lawyer steeped in an appellate court's ways could predict outcomes "eight times out of ten", so easy cases are easy because lawyers share a theory.

Llewellyn warned against "paper rules", and Frank, the fact-sceptic, held that even a clear rule decides little at trial, since the tribunal can find the facts it needs; he admitted that his case was "deliberately overstated".

Close: Harris's tests cut deeper. If rules had no effect, a motorist driving at 60 would be no likelier to be convicted of speeding than one driving at 20, and the officials whose conduct realists study are themselves appointed by rules. Kennedy concedes as much about backgrounds: his "gut" is "an organ deeply conditioned by existence in our legalized universe". So judges converge through a shared practice of justification, not only shared backgrounds.

Section 2: "coherent, with a few exceptions" (form 2)

The crits are right that doctrine carries more exceptions than its defenders admit. From the kit: each principle meets a counterprinciple that the dominant view files as an anomaly (Unger). Unger finds two pairs in contract: freedom to contract against community, seen in promissory estoppel and in social arrangements that are not presumed binding; and freedom of contract against fairness, seen in undue influence, unconscionability and inequality of bargaining power. Whole fields, from family and labour law to fiduciary duties, sit outside the theory that claims them: "an empire whose claimed or perceived authority vastly outreaches its actual power". Nothing in the rules fixes which is principle and which counterprinciple.

Close: Pepper replies that the crits see "contradiction" where "there may be only complexity". So "coherent, with a few exceptions" overstates, and so does its denial: exceptions show that principles compete, not that nothing governs.

Section 2: "of no use" (form 3)

The crits' use is diagnostic: they show where doctrine hides a choice. They also show legitimation: arguing that employees' rights should be broadly construed only buttresses the assumption that the employer's control is the starting point (Harris). From the kit: fit is conservative, so fairness fixes such as duress stay "arbitrarily selective" exceptions. Harris replies that the crits must show more "incremental justice" than reformed legalism.

Close: their programme fares worse. The transformative project has mainly failed, and Harris doubts there could be a crit judge in the courts we now have. Harris's demand is fair, but it is a demand for more, not a proof that the critique is useless.

Section 3: what follows (every form)

What follows is not that law is politics in the partisan sense. Kennedy: law is a medium that constrains like bricks, relative to one's project; the judge works the field toward "how-I-want-to-come-out", paying in time and legitimacy. His activist judge has promised "some diffuse public" that he will "decide according to law", and earlier judges' opinions pull on him: "They are old; they are many." Then Dworkin: law is deeply political but never personal or partisan.

Close: even Kennedy's judge sometimes meets a rule he "can't budge" and must give reasons others can test, so what follows is candour about the choice, not the end of law.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

For form 1, the best case is indeterminacy and moral terms (E22.1): rules are picked or construed to fit results reached on other grounds, as in Cheng San ("outside" against "at or near"), and each principle meets a counterprinciple. For forms 2 and 3, the best case is Dworkin's integrity: judges read doctrine in its best light, so an exception such as duress can rest on a principle of its own, fairness, and need not be an anomaly. That shows doctrine can be argued from as a body of principle. It does not show that its principles never conflict, or that exposing their conflicts is of no use.

My weak point (E21.4): my answer relies on Dworkin, and fit often yields no unique reading. Whose political morality decides? I concede no answer is demonstrable; but the choice is political, not personal, tested on appeal, and starts from the text.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is overstated. The realists and crits rightly show that rules leave some cases open and that doctrine carries more exceptions than it admits, but a judge must justify, not predict, and political morality, not preference, closes the open cases. The realists and crits share with Dworkin the premise that rules leave some cases open; they part on whether a judge's choice there can be justified or only explained. Repaired: “Rules decide most cases and leave some open; there, judges choose between principles of political morality and must justify the choice in public.” For the judge, this means candour about the choice, not pretence that there was none.

For form 2, the first sentence becomes: the statement is right that doctrine can be argued from as a body of principle, and wrong that its exceptions are few or settled. For form 3: right that critical views need a standard of their own to condemn what they expose, and wrong that exposing hidden choices is of no use.

Using it in the hypo

The variance study shows that some cases at the margin are open, not that law is just prediction, and a judge cannot predict her own ruling. For Wong, set out Kennedy's five courses (E21.1); candour rules out the two covert ones; and the trespass rule may be one she "can't budge". The fairness doctrines are the crits' best point, so concede them.

Memory line

Rules leave some cases open, not all; justify, don't predict; critique diagnoses, it doesn't prescribe.

E23.1E20.1E20.7E22.1E21.4E21.1E31.1

key ideaWhat does Llewellyn's "eight times out of ten" show?
  1. Correct (E20.7). Lawyers can predict most outcomes because they share a way of justifying decisions; the tenth case is where that shared theory is argued.
  2. This is the reverse: on Llewellyn's figure, the shared practice settles eight cases in ten.
  3. Convergence comes from shared justification.
  4. That is the think-tank's error.
The tenth case.
theoristsWhat is the crits' best point?
  1. The crits say rules are indeterminate.
  2. That is not the crits' best point. Their strongest claim is about the conservatism of fit.
  3. That is not the crits' view; they deny that law is a neutral science.
  4. Correct. Because a judge must fit past decisions, fairness doctrines like duress stay as "arbitrarily selective" exceptions instead of reshaping contract law.
Exceptions that stay exceptions.
theoristsHow does Harris reply to the realists and crits?
  1. Correct. Harris says a judge's task is to justify a decision, not predict it, and the crits must show they would deliver more justice step by step.
  2. This goes too far. Harris answers the crits but does not say critique is useless.
  3. A judge cannot predict her own ruling; she decides it.
  4. Rules leave some cases open, as Harris accepts.
Justify, not predict.
hypoHypo 9(c): Judge Wong wants to rule for the strikers though trespass plainly applies. What does candour bar?
  1. Allowed, at the cost of legitimacy.
  2. Allowed, though it leaves the dirty work to another.
  3. Candour allows this, because the judge says openly what she thinks.
  4. Correct. Candour rules out the two hidden courses: an implausible legal argument presented as sound, or misstated facts.
The covert two.
theoristsWhat is Harris's speeding test against the strong realist claim that rules do not decide cases?
  1. The test compares conviction rates, not appeals.
  2. The test is about convictions, not about motorists' attitudes.
  3. Variance at the margin is consistent with rules deciding most cases.
  4. Correct. And the officials realists study are themselves appointed by rules.
60 against 20.

How these essays fit together

Both essays use the politics kit (E23.1). E23 takes Dworkin's cell, and TSH's threshold, to limit a judge's moral reasoning on one constitutional question. E32 takes the realists', crits' and Kennedy's cells to ask whether rules and doctrine constrain at all. If both are on one paper, give E23 Dworkin, TSH and Finnis, and give E32 the realists, the crits and Kennedy, with Dworkin only as the reply in its last section.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • E23: moral reasoning is inevitable, but that is about presupposition, not licence.
  • Fit binds the judge to the record; whole-cloth rights refused (2015); TSH's threshold not met.
  • Equality cuts both ways; who counts is the prior question (Finnis).
  • Twin vices: refusing all is abdication, deciding all is usurpation.
  • E32: three forms, two sections each.
  • Realists: rules leave some cases open, not all; Harris's speeding test; Kennedy's conditioned "gut".
  • Crits: principles and counterprinciples (Unger); complexity, not contradiction (Pepper).
  • Critique diagnoses; its programme fails. Kennedy's judge "can't budge" some rules.
  • Repaired: rules decide most cases; in the rest, justify the choice in public.

Video 14The Constitution: positivists, and who decidesJudges and lawyers

E35

A written Constitution makes us positivists

▲ likely

The question exam-style

"By declaring the Constitution supreme, Article 4 settles the debate between natural law and positivism. 'Law' in the Constitution means whatever is validly enacted, and every question of validity is answered by the text."

The hypo Hypo 13(a), (d)

Justice Siti hears an Art 12 challenge to a law punishing citizens' corrupt acts abroad even with no effect at home; and counsel urges her to read Art 9 consistently with a treaty banning inhuman punishment, while the Attorney-General says the Constitution must be read within its own four walls. How should she decide?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

"Therefore" hides a gap: from supremacy to a choice of legal theory. The statement assumes a supreme written text settles what law is. It does not, because a master rule exists only because it is accepted, and the rights chapter's terms are moral.

The answer in one breath

By positivists I mean those who identify law by its sources, with moral tests only where the sources adopt them. A supreme, written Constitution does not settle what law is. Article 4 is a master rule only because it is accepted, and the rights chapter's "law", "life", "liberty" and "equal" are contested moral terms. I qualify the statement: the Constitution relocates the debate into the reading of those terms, and courts should presume validity yet void intolerable injustice.

Section 1: "Article 4 settles it"

A master rule exists only because it is accepted (block E35.1). Article 4 makes the Constitution supreme, but that cannot be inferred from the text alone (TSH). Her argument works at two levels: the meaning of each contested term, such as "law", "life" and "personal liberty" in Article 9; and the import of the supremacy clause itself. She holds that positivism sheds no light on the rights chapter's morally loaded concepts. So "the Constitution sets the criteria of validity" holds only formally. Concede that in practice the master-rule point may not lead to much; what matters is how theory shapes judges' duty to law.

Close: the text cannot tell us why it binds, or which school reads it. Article 4 voids later inconsistent law and Article 162 reads earlier law to conform, yet neither says how to read "law" in Article 9. If judges ignored the Constitution and followed another directive, that directive would be the master rule. Nor does "law is what the courts say" end the debate: it glosses over the principles that should guide the courts, and theory exists to guide practice and to judge it (TSH).

Section 2: which school wins

Declaring supremacy neither adopts positivism nor, by itself, proves natural law (block E35.2). That the Constitution includes moral terms does not mean natural law wins: one may be a positivist happy that morality is now positive law. Nor does supremacy mean positivism was adopted: TSH's own thesis is that constitutionalism affirms a higher law. Each inference needs a further premise. If the drafters' intention is offered, why should it matter? Their will begs the question why it still binds us. Nor is an intention one thing: a drafter may intend equal treatment in the abstract and believe, concretely, that a practice is consistent with it, and which description governs is a political question (Dworkin).

Then "within its own four walls" (E35.5). Colin Chan's approach, reading the Constitution primarily within its own four walls, need not exclude universal standards: reading an earlier case charitably, TSH finds fairness universal, though its manifestation varies with local conditions. Positivism mandates little but is compatible with more: where the text points to a moral concept, a positivist judge may follow it outward, as commercial reasonableness imports trade practice. Concede that higher law cuts both ways: a judge with a very different view of dignity could use it too, so defend only a narrow trigger.

Close: a positivist may follow moral terms outward, and a natural lawyer need not strike down what he dislikes. Even courts that void manifest injustice through the text, rather than through natural law, rely on the text's incorporation of universal principles (TSH).

Section 3: "law" and "equal"

Reading "law" as whatever is validly passed begs the question (E35.4). Whether an Act is valid depends on whether it breaches Article 9, the very question (TSH). There are four readings of "law": validly passed (an early dictum, later confined); natural justice (procedural, the Court of Appeal held in 2015); not absurd or arbitrary (a 2010 suggestion, arguably a natural-law hook short of the Radbruch formula); and international law (statute beats custom).

Then Article 12 and equality (E35.3). For Lim Meng Suang, an intelligible line rationally related to the law's object usually suffices, since courts are not "mini-legislatures". That is a threshold test with no free-standing review of the law's legitimacy. Picture a double fee for left-handed drivers: the Court of Appeal asks whether the line rationally serves its aim; the High Court would also void an illegitimate object. This is not cleanly positivism against natural law: extreme illogicality fails a limb, and TSH calls the test natural law at work. For her the real question is which differences are morally significant, so moral arguments cannot be dismissed as "extra-legal"; without a higher moral law, laws treating everyone "equally badly" pass.

Close: Article 15(4) shows the same. Placing the line where religious liberty yields to "public order, public health or morality" needs a view of human nature, or the liberty is left to the will of the state or the majority (TSH). Stopping at fair-trial rules, or at an intelligible line, is a choice of theory, not of text.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is the text and the cases. Article 4 makes the Constitution supreme and voids inconsistent law; an early dictum read Article 9's "law" as whatever is validly passed; the Court of Appeal in 2015 refused to make rights from "whole cloth"; and Lim Meng Suang refused to act as a "mini-legislature". On that record the courts read the Constitution by its sources. This shows that Singapore's judges identify law as positivists would. It does not show that the text settles which school reads it: the early dictum was later confined, and the rights chapter's moral terms still force a choice. Against the statement, use indeterminacy and moral terms (E22.1).

My weak point (E20.12): my narrow trigger relies on TSH's broad historical consensus, which she admits can go "dreadfully wrong". I adopt her reply: the alternatives are worse, and the test binds courts only.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is overstated: Article 4 and the rights chapter relocate, not settle, the debate, so judges choose a theory whenever they read "law" or "equal". TSH, Dworkin and the positivist agree that Article 4 makes the Constitution supreme; they split on whether its moral terms import morality as a source or merely point to it. The Constitution binds the government as well as citizens, narrows the practical gap between natural-law and positivist judges, and makes Radbruch's formula rarely needed, but it leaves open the extreme case, our attitude to law, and who decides by what standard. Repaired: “A supreme, written Constitution relocates the debate between natural law and positivism into the reading of its moral terms, such as 'law' and 'equal'; it does not settle it. Judges should presume validity but void intolerable injustice, through the Constitution's own terms, Article 9's 'law' and Article 12, not as a free-standing formula, a power untested here.” No clause relieves anyone of conscience.

If the statement says supremacy adopts Radbruch's formula, use the case for it (E43.5: supremacy rests on equal moral worth), then the formula in a stable democracy (E43.4: untested against a sitting legislature, and an open question), then E35.2 (each inference needs a further premise). Watch: E20 answers the neighbouring statement, that theory and morality are not needed. Never write both in full.

Using it in the hypo

(a) Taw Cheng Kong upheld such a law under the presumption of constitutionality (E35.6), as a rational classification. (d) Reading the Constitution within its "four walls" need not exclude universal standards, but international law yields to statute (Nguyen). Each of these is a choice of theory, not something the text dictates.

Memory line

Supremacy relocates the debate into "law" and "equal"; it does not settle it.

E36

Who decides: courts or Parliament

▲ likely

The question exam-style

"Contested moral questions are for Parliament, not the courts. Unelected judges must never invalidate a law on grounds of justice; natural law defeats the separation of powers. The Constitution is legitimate because the people accepted it, so any amendment properly passed is valid."

The hypo Hypo 13(c), (e)

By two-thirds, Parliament amends the Constitution to let the Prime Minister postpone general elections indefinitely during an "emergency", unreviewable; it is challenged as destroying a basic feature. A litigant argues the Constitution binds no one alive, since none consented. The Minister says a judge who invalidates a law usurps Parliament. How should Justice Siti decide?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

"Never" is an absolute. The statement is right as a presumption and wrong as an absolute. The marker wants the forum question (who decides) kept apart from the merits, and validity kept apart from legitimacy.

The answer in one breath

By contested moral questions I mean those on which reasonable citizens disagree. By invalidating on grounds of justice I mean striking a law down because it is unjust, not because it breaches a clause. Contested rights are for Parliament: Lim Meng Suang said so, and in 2022 change came that way. But "never" proves too much. Rubber-stamping is a vice too, and a narrow threshold, intolerable injustice shown by broad historical consensus, binds courts without making them a second legislature.

Section 1: "for Parliament"

Accept that contested rights are for Parliament. Parliament can also weigh what a court cannot: a court must decide whether a law is constitutional, and holding back to avoid social disruption would be improper if there is a right legal answer. A court's answer is "binary, zero sum", while Parliament could repeal s 377A and shield marriage in Article 156 in one calibrated step. That is a reason for the forum in 2022, not a verdict on the merits. The Court of Appeal said the same from the bench in 2015: courts cannot make a right from "whole cloth".

Then the block on who decides in the courts (E36.1): accept it, but only until Radbruch's threshold of injustice is met (TSH). That threshold is met for genocide and slavery, not for the laws voided in Roe and Obergefell, nor for the Women's Charter's definition of marriage. Lim Meng Suang left Hart against Devlin to the legislature; for TSH that is right only if no constitutional right was at stake, and the court was "overly sanguine" about future Parliaments. Add Tan Seng Kee (2022): the 2007 non-enforcement promise gained legal force, so minority protection depends on legislators' reasons.

Close: but only until the threshold of injustice is met.

Section 2: "never invalidate"

In a stable democracy the Radbruch formula is a narrow check, not an unelected veto (E38.5 and TSH's cell in the law-and-justice kit). Legislation stands unless intolerably unjust by broad historical consensus, which checks personal ideology. Ordinary injustice is Parliament's to repeal. Deviating can be unjust too, since a judge's morality may be wrong (Hoerster), which is why the threshold is high; Radbruch himself warned of the formula's "frightful dangers".

Nor does natural law "defeat the separation of powers". It allocates authority as a prudent choice among acceptable options (George), so it supports one. Natural law and positivism answer what law is; the separation of powers answers how power is organised, so either camp may value it. And on the weak thesis, a natural-law judge applies unjust laws save at the extreme, so the separation largely stands. TSH agrees that where constitutionalism has been adopted, the legislative mandate should generally prevail over judicial opinion on contentious moral norms. Natural law's weak point is TSH's question of who decides, and the answer is a narrow threshold, not a general licence. Denying courts that power would also unsettle judicial review itself, since Article 12 is no clearer than "intolerable injustice".

Section 3: legitimacy and amendment

Acceptance cannot alone found legitimacy (from the legitimacy kit, E38.3: Finnis, TSH and Rawls). For a Constitution, the framers' will begs the question why it still binds us; treating non-revolt as acquiescence is artificial; and only co-equal dignity makes consent meaningful (TSH). Rawls's essentials, which all may reasonably endorse, are moral though political. The unproven is not the false.

Then Article 5(2): a special two-thirds majority of MPs may amend most provisions, and there is no express core like the German Basic Law's art 79(3).

Close: validity and legitimacy part here. An amendment properly passed is valid law, but its legitimacy rests on the reasons it can give, not on the people's past acceptance. For TSH supremacy rests on equal moral worth, which a majority vote, only a second best to unanimity, cannot legitimately violate, so even a properly passed amendment authorising genocide would fall. A "basic features" limit on amendment was rejected by the High Court in Teo Soh Lung and has been left open since, though arguably natural law justifies it more easily, since a good constitution has a purpose some amendments destroy. So the only sure limit is intolerable injustice, and even that is untested here.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is restraint (E20.9): rules have a core of meaning where morality sits idle; deciding by personal morality would make each judge a law unto himself; and Singapore has decided, through Article 4, Rajeevan and the 2022 change through Parliament. This shows that judges must not decide by private conviction and should presume validity. It does not show that intolerable injustice must stand.

My weak point (E20.12): TSH's consensus test can go "dreadfully wrong"; I adopt her reply that the alternatives are worse, and that the test binds courts only.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right as a presumption and wrong as an absolute. TSH, the Radbruch formula and Lim Meng Suang agree that Parliament is the normal forum; they split on whether any injustice is grave enough for a court to say otherwise. TSH concedes there is "no available formula" for that balance, so the threshold is a judgement, not a rule. Repaired: “Parliament decides contested moral questions, and courts presume its laws valid; only intolerable injustice, shown by broad historical consensus, defeats that presumption.” For the judge, this means deference without abdication.

Using it in the hypo

(c) A "basic features" limit on amendment was rejected in Teo Soh Lung and has been left open since (E20.10). Postponing elections indefinitely may be the rare case where the threshold argument is live, so say so openly. (e) Consent is not the ground of legitimacy: co-equal dignity and the reasons owed to citizens are (TSH, Rawls). The Minister names only one vice, usurpation, and ignores the other, abdication (E21.2).

Memory line

Parliament is the normal forum; never is too strong; valid is not the same as legitimate.

Write TSH's threshold in full once.

  • Neither school wins by declaration; each inference needs a further premise.
  • Article 9's "law" has four readings; "validly passed" is circular.
  • Article 12: an intelligible line; which differences matter is a moral question.
  • Repaired: the Constitution relocates the debate, it does not settle it.
  • E36: Parliament is the normal forum; a court's answer is "binary, zero sum".
  • The Radbruch threshold: a narrow check, not an unelected veto. Natural law supports a separation of powers.

Video 15Roles under pressure: refuse, stay or resignJudges and lawyers

E24

An immoral system: refuse, stay or resign

The question exam-style

"In an immoral legal system, the only right course for a judge or lawyer is to resign. Any other course either condones injustice or breaches fidelity to law." (Also: a lawyer leaving a firm, an official resigning.)

The hypo Hypo 24(b), (c)

In Borduria under the Council, Decree 14 strips the Ostran minority of citizenship and property. Advocate Brun is asked by the state to act in the confiscation cases, and by an Ostran family to defend them before Judge Ferro; he wonders whether to take either brief or leave the profession. Sergeant Voss is ordered to clear an Ostran village. What should each do?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

"Either … or" is a false dilemma, and "only" is an absolute. The statement offers two options, to condone injustice or to betray fidelity, and hides a third: refusal from within, which is fidelity, not its breach.

The answer in one breath

By fidelity to law I mean faithfulness to law as law, which Fuller distinguishes from deference to whoever holds power. If "immoral legal system" means a gravely unjust regime, refusing its intolerable laws is what fidelity requires. If it means a basically just system with some immoral laws, the ordinary duty holds and resignation is rarely in point. Resignation is sometimes right but never the only right course, and staying can protect the system's victims.

Section 1: "fidelity"

Fidelity is to law, not to whatever is enacted. From the block on refuse, stay or resign (E24.1): ask first what is immoral, the system, a rule or one outcome. Where a rule is intolerably unjust, refusing it is fidelity, not its breach. For Fuller, reciprocity ruptured completely leaves the duty no ground; for Radbruch and Alexy, the rule lacks legal character, so the judge who refuses applies the law. Hart's candour sets the condition: whoever stays and refuses must say so openly. From Hart's cell in the law-and-justice kit: validity leaves open the final moral question of obedience; the decree is law but too evil to obey, a condemnation everyone understands.

Close: refusing intolerable injustice is fidelity (Fuller, Radbruch, Alexy); Hart asks only that it be done openly. Alexy grants that for a judge in a rogue state it makes no essential difference whether he refuses on Hart's moral grounds or Radbruch's legal ones: either way he must reckon with the personal consequences. Fuller adds that a court which refuses to apply what it admits to be law shows "moral confusion" at its height, which is why the refusing judge says, with Radbruch, that the rule is not law. In a degenerate system no simple test settles fidelity: each must decide whether to stay, and, like a voter whose ballot may not be counted, cast it as a symbolic act of hope for a better day (Fuller, of citizens). So "the only right course" fails. Ordinance 11, the Nazi decree that stripped citizenship on racist criteria, shows the first case: a rule so unjust that refusing it was fidelity, as a German constitutional court later held.

Section 2: "condones", the judge

Staying is not condoning if the judge does the role's own work, though the role alone excuses no one: a judge who applies a gravely unjust law cannot answer by pointing to his office (Thunder). From E24.1: Kennedy gives a judge five courses short of resigning, among them going along while denouncing the law and withdrawing from the case, and excludes none in advance. Where the system is unjust but the office still does good, staying may shield its victims, while a judge's withdrawal only leaves "the dirty work" to another. From the role kit (E21.1), Alexy's risk effect: in a rogue state an office-holder who is "neither a saint nor a hero" may later be prosecuted. And Dworkin in a clause: the judge owes a duty to the history he finds.

Close: the statement is a stricter form of what Kennedy calls the "devil's compact": if you dislike the job, do not take it, and once in office do it or withdraw from the case. Kennedy finds unconvincing the claim that the rule of law means obeying that compact, and each of his courses carries its own hard question: going along makes the judge an instrument of injustice, and deciding openly against the law raises who authorised him to take the law into his own hands. Fuller likewise counts surrendering office, like a wooden and literal reading of precedent, among a dissenting judge's "melancholy escapes", not what fidelity demands. Kennedy's covert courses, an implausible legal argument or false facts, are barred by candour, so whoever stays must refuse openly. And a judge who resigns makes the regime's personnel change for it: a rogue regime breaks the lawyers' shared refusal by intimidation, personnel changes and rewards for conformity (Alexy).

Section 3: "condones", the lawyer

Pepper's justification lapses once law is no longer a public good. Thunder's survives, because defending the system's victims serves justice. Use the lawyering kit (E20.3): the three scenarios first (is the system by and large just; if so, is this rule intolerably unjust; if not, is this outcome unjust?), then Thunder, then Pepper's own limits (choice of client, moral dialogue, conscientious objection). Close the lawyer's part with the twin vices for lawyers (E21.2): screening clients usurps (Pepper), and simply focusing on the law abdicates (Thunder).

Close: leaving practice is a weak answer. At what cost, and to whom does the unpopular client then go? If conscientious lawyers leave, the system's victims get the least scrupulous lawyer or none. Pepper himself lists the choice whether to be a lawyer at all as only one of four outlets for conscience, so even for him resigning has no priority, and it can itself be abdication. Thunder expects no perfect legal system: the good lawyer works within imperfect conditions while trying to improve them, and must tell moderation and patience apart from acquiescence in avoidable injustice.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is candour. Hart insists that an iniquitous rule is law but may be too evil to obey. A judge who stays and applies it lends the office's authority to injustice; one who stays and refuses it pretends the law says what it does not, the insincerity Hart condemns. Resigning keeps faith with both. This shows that staying has a moral cost the role cannot cancel. It does not show that resigning is the only right course: withdrawal leaves the dirty work to another.

My weak point (the own-side line written in E33): my answer relies on Thunder, who admits disagreement on particular cases and that integrity is rarely if ever fully achieved. I concede he guides character more than cases, so I send hard cases to dialogue first and refuse only at the extremes.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right that an immoral system leaves no clean hands, and wrong that resignation is the only right course. The theorists agree that the role alone excuses no one; they split on what to do instead: refuse openly (Hart), refuse as the law itself requires (Radbruch and Alexy), or stay and protect (Thunder), while Kennedy will rule out no course in advance. Repaired: “Faced with an immoral system, a judge or lawyer may rightly refuse, stay or resign; which is right depends on what is immoral and on what staying can still protect.” For both, this means refusing the intolerable, staying to protect where they can, and resigning when staying protects no one.

Watch: never use the problem question's judge's-hat ladder here, because it assumes a basically just system. And the grudge informer (E24.2) belongs after a regime has fallen, not during it: keep it for E43.

Using it in the hypo

Brun should refuse the state's confiscation brief, since it serves an intolerably unjust law, but take the family's defence, which serves the law's victims. Voss owes fidelity to law, not to rank. Alexy's risk effect reaches him, so he should refuse openly.

Memory line

Condone or betray hides a third option: refuse from within, openly. Resigning is one course, never the only one.

key ideaWhat should you ask first (E24.1)?

  2. That is not the first question. First ask what exactly is immoral.

  4. Correct (E24.1). The right course depends on what is wrong: a whole unjust system, one intolerable rule, or one unjust outcome.
Locate the immorality.
theoristsWhat does Kennedy say about withdrawing from the case?
  1. Candour bars only the covert courses.
  2. That is not Kennedy's view; he warns that withdrawal leaves the dirty work to another.
  3. Withdrawal is one of five courses short of resigning, none excluded a priori.
  4. Correct. Kennedy points out that a judge who withdraws only passes the unjust work to someone else.
Someone else does it.
hypoHypo 24(b): what should Advocate Brun do?
  1. Victims then get the least scrupulous lawyer or none.
  2. Validity never settles what to do.
  3. Correct: staying can serve the system's victims.
  4. The confiscation brief applies an intolerable law.
Staying can protect victims.
theoristsWhat does Fuller say about a judge who surrenders office rather than apply an unjust law?
  1. Fuller counts it an escape, not a duty; refusing intolerable injustice can be fidelity.
  2. Hart asks for candour in refusing; Fuller calls resignation a melancholy escape.
  3. Correct. It is not what fidelity demands; a court refusing what it admits is law shows "moral confusion".
  4. Legality comes in degrees; partial failure does not oblige resignation.
An escape, not a duty.
E34

Stay in your role, for several actors

The question 2017-18 Q1

"Natural law requires legislators, judges, officials and lawyers to respect the limits of their own authority, and not to do what is rightfully the work of others." (Or justice, or the rule of law.)

The hypo Hypo 18(iii)

Under new outbreak rules, enforcement officer Tan is told by his superior to fine gatherings in the older housing estates and to "leave the better neighbourhoods alone". Must he comply? And should the Health Minister, given power to make "such rules as he thinks fit", leave the hard choices to Parliament?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

"Respect the limits" is an absolute that assumes each role's limits are settled. The marker wants you to take each actor in turn, and to name the vice the statement leaves out: refusing one's own work.

The answer in one breath

By the limits of their own authority I mean the boundaries a constitutional order draws between roles; by "rightfully others'", work another role is entitled to do. Roles do have limits, but they are "essentially contestable" (Thunder), so drawing them is a moral judgement. Refusing one's own work is as much a vice as taking another's. And refusing extreme injustice is fidelity, not usurpation.

Section 1: judges

Start with where the statement comes from (E34.1). It is almost George, as TSH reports him: at least in a basically just regime, natural law requires officials to respect the limits of their authority, allocated by a prudent choice among acceptable options. But the statement drops George's proviso, a basically just regime, and adds other actors. TSH's reply is the question George leaves open: who rightfully decides whether authority was prudently allocated? Natural law condemns vesting authority in a random group of bandits, but cannot say who decides. So "what is rightfully others'" assumes its answer: a role's limits come from a theory of the role.

Then the twin vices (E21.1): every role can fail twice, by doing others' work (usurpation) or by refusing its own (abdication). And TSH's threshold: legislation stands unless intolerably unjust by broad historical consensus.

Close: George's reason is that judicial usurpation, even in what judges take to be good causes, is itself unjust, and the Court of Appeal cited him in a 2015 case. Restraint without candour abdicates, and candour without restraint usurps.

Section 2: legislators

A legislature keeps to its role by doing its own work: deciding contested moral questions, not handing them to the executive, the courts or a referendum. The 2007 compromise arguably shows both vices at once: s 377A was kept but not proactively enforced, so Parliament abdicated and the executive quasi-legislated. When Poa sought a referendum in 2022, Masagos replied that MPs must decide. Frame it as refusing to abdicate.

Close: keeping a law while promising not to enforce it also strains Fuller's congruence between declared rule and official action. Article 156 shows the other side of the same duty: for TSH it was a pre-emptive strike against judicial activism, the legislature guarding its work from the courts. A legislator who is no natural lawyer may not think himself bound by higher standards, and may see himself as representing the interest groups that elected him (TSH), yet one who wants good laws, not merely valid ones, must still weigh morality. Positivism uses morality in one dimension, good law; natural law in two, good law and law at all.

Section 3: officials and juniors

Officials owe fidelity to law, not deference to rank. From the role kit: Fuller says respect for constituted authority must not be confused with fidelity to law; King Rex's subjects stayed faithful to him as king, not to his law, for he never made any. Alexy's risk effect reaches the office-holder who is "neither a saint nor a hero". Then E20.5: legality settles what may be done, not who answers for it, so even a junior following lawful instructions shares responsibility, and Thunder's "lazy lawyer" takes for granted the standards of peers and superiors.

Close: so raise the concern first, and refuse only where the instruction would work serious injustice. Thunder's union member who puts patients' lives in jeopardy cannot plead the role.

Section 4: lawyers

Screening clients usurps, and simply focusing on the law abdicates. From the lawyering kit's verdict: the lawyer's role is itself moral, and law sets its floor, not its ceiling; above the floor, prudence, dialogue and rare objection decide.

Close: in Singapore the duty to the court is overriding, so a lawyer who refuses to mislead the court, or discloses adverse authority, does the role's own work and takes nothing from the judge.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is restraint (E20.9): rules have a core of meaning, deciding by personal morality would make each actor a law unto himself, and Singapore's Constitution divides power. It shows each role must presume against doing another's work. It does not show that a role's limits are settled, or that refusing one's own work is no vice.

My weak point (E20.12): my refusal threshold rests on TSH's broad historical consensus, which she admits can go wrong; I adopt her reply that the alternatives are worse.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right only as a presumption: roles divide moral work, but no role ends moral duty. George, TSH, Fuller and Alexy agree that roles have limits; they split on who draws them, and on when refusing is fidelity rather than usurpation. Repaired: “Legal actors should respect the limits of their roles and do their roles' own moral work, refusing only intolerable injustice.” For each actor, this means doing the role's own work before policing another's.

Using it in the hypo

Enforcing the rule in some estates and not others fails generality and Article 12 (E44.3, Ramalingam). Tan owes fidelity to the law, not to his superior's rank: he should raise it first, then refuse the discriminatory part. The Minister's unbounded power is Parliament abdicating and the executive quasi-legislating (E21.2); see also E45.12.

Memory line

Every role can fail twice: usurp or abdicate. Do your own role's moral work first.

How these essays fit together

E24 is the extreme case: a whole system is immoral, so the question is refuse, stay or resign. E34 is the ordinary case: a basically just system, many actors, and the question of who does what. Both use the role kit (E21.1) and the twin vices (E21.2). If both are on one paper, give E24 Kennedy's courses, the candour condition and the lawyer who stays to protect; give E34 George's source, the four actors and the 2007 example. Write the twin vices once.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • E24: condone or betray is a false dilemma; refusing from within is fidelity.
  • Ask what is immoral: the system, a rule or one outcome.
  • Kennedy's five courses; candour bars the covert two; withdrawal leaves the dirty work to another.
  • Fuller's "melancholy escapes"; Alexy: resigning lets the regime change its personnel.
  • Lawyers: leaving practice is weak; Thunder's lawyer stays to protect.
  • E34: George's proviso dropped; TSH asks who decides.
  • Twin vices for each actor: judge, legislator (2007), official, lawyer.
  • Repaired: respect the limits of your role and do its own moral work; refuse only intolerable injustice.

Video 16What law is: social fact, and the two schoolsWhat law is

E37

Law as fact, will and opinion

The question 2022-23 Q1

"Laws are made by human beings. So in understanding law, and in debating what our laws should be, we are and should be concerned solely with social fact, that is, with the positions and views that people hold."

The hypo Hypo 24(d), (iv)

An Ostran refugee sues a Ruritanian bank for jewellery the Council confiscated under Decree 14 and deposited there. The Council's lawyers say a Ruritanian court has no business judging Borduria's law: "Decree 14 is law in Borduria, and that is a fact." Is the existence of Decree 14 as law simply a fact, and how should the judge decide?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

A truism, that people make law, is turned by "solely" into a theory: only fact counts, both in what law is and in debate about what it should be. Two flaws. "We are and should be" joins a description to a norm, and a claim about how we ought to argue cannot itself be a social fact, so the statement refutes itself. And "social fact, that is, the positions and views people hold" misdescribes positivism.

The answer in one breath

By debating laws the statement means arguing about what the law should be, which is a normative question, not a factual one. Positivism's social fact is what officials accept, not what people think. Law's sources are social facts, but treating sources as all that counts is itself an evaluation, and no fact about opinion can settle what the law should be.

Section 1: "understanding law", social fact

The truism is right about where law comes from. Use the social-fact kit (E37.1).

  • Hart agrees, more narrowly. Law is the union of primary and secondary rules that officials accept from the internal point of view, so its sources are facts about that acceptance. But acceptance is not approval: Hart allows it to come from self-interest, habit, tradition or even fear (Harris reports), though arguably officials cannot accept a rule purely from fear, and describing law is not praising it. Where only officials accept the rules, Hart warns, the sheep may end in the slaughter-house.
  • TSH: naming law is an evaluation. Both schools ask what law is; treating official recognition as conclusive is a choice, since once the highest court upholds a manifestly unjust rule its injustice is "treated as irrelevant". Preferring positivism for a plural state is itself a value choice.
  • Natural law, in a clause: human law is made, but law contrary to natural law is defective as law (Aquinas) or lacks prima facie power to bind in conscience (Finnis), who doubts law can be described from a "rubbish heap" of facts.
  • Dworkin, in a clause: which arguments count as law is the point in dispute.

Then the slide (E37.5): the statement slides from one fact to another. Law's sources are social facts about official practice: what officials accept as criteria of validity (Hart). Public opinion is a different fact, positive morality, what people think right. The first tells us what the law is; the second tells us what people want it to be; neither tells us what it should be.

Close: TSH adds that on Hart's account citizens may obey with no critical reflective attitude at all, so positivism is "not necessarily democratic" and may come close to law as whatever is effectively enforced. Deciding that sources are all that count is an evaluation, and what people accept cannot itself settle whether law is what they accept or what conforms to a higher law (TSH).

Section 2: "debating laws", what should be

Facts about opinion fix the stakes. Use the kit on facts about opinion (E37.4).

  • Rawls starts from a fact and ends with a norm. Reasonable pluralism is a fact of free institutions; legitimacy is a norm, requiring reasons all citizens may reasonably be expected to endorse. An overlapping consensus is affirmed on moral grounds, not counted, so no poll can supply it.
  • Finnis rejects outright any inference of value from fact. The basic goods are self-evident and grasped, and facts, from biology or economics, show only how they apply. Practical reasonableness, not the "power of numbers", decides what the common good requires.
  • Hart, as the bridge: whether the bare fact that positive morality condemns conduct is any reason to punish it is a question of critical morality, not of fact.
  • Devlin, in a clause: the nearest ally stops short, calling disgust only "a good indication" and wanting a deliberate judgement.

Then "what people made is not what they should make" (E37.6). Hume's gap holds: no ought follows from an is alone. The gap is only logical: agreed ought-premises plus facts yield oughts, so facts fix the stakes. But the ought-premise must be argued, not read off the statute book or the poll.

Close: facts about opinion cannot settle what the law should be. In 2007 the consultation feedback was "emotional, divided": it fixed what Parliament had to weigh, not what it should decide. And if we should attend to people's views, the reason is that each person whose view makes up the consensus matters and has moral dignity, a moral premise no social fact supplies. So "should be solely concerned" refutes itself. If law may have any content people give it, some people become the ultimate lawgivers over their fellows, and the meaning of liberty is left to the will of the state or the majority (TSH).

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is clarity (E37.7). Law lies in legislation, cases and custom, which anyone can check; lawyers must know it before reforming it; and positivism arose in states with no shared moral base. Austin's candour adds that law's existence is one thing and its merit another, so calling law a fact keeps criticism honest. This shows that sources usually identify law. It does not show that identifying it is value-free, or that what law is settles what it should be.

My weak point (E37.8): describing is not praising (Hart), and Dworkin is arguably unfair to positivism, which need not make every disagreement a factual one; "soft" positivism lets the rule of recognition adopt moral tests. I concede it: the evaluation is about what counts as law, not approval of it. That is enough to defeat "solely".

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right about where law comes from and wrong about what that settles. Hart, TSH, Rawls and Finnis agree that law's sources are facts; they split on whether naming law is itself an evaluation, and none lets fact decide what law should be. Repaired: “Law's sources are matters of social fact, but what law should be is not, and facts about opinion are evidence to weigh, never the answer.” For those debating a law, this means counting opinion as evidence and still arguing what is right.

Variants. If the statement adds will or power, add the will kit (E37.2) as a middle section: will makes law only through rules that bind the will (Austin's command theory, Fuller's King Rex, TSH on Article 4). The repaired line then reads: majority will settles who may make law, within rules that bind it; it does not settle what the law should say. If it denies any moral link, use the no-necessary-link kit (E37.3): the separation holds for ordinary law, and a narrow link survives at the extreme. If it ends on conscience, use E40.

Using it in the hypo

For an observer, Decree 14 was law in Borduria: Hart says identifying it as law is what lets us condemn it. For the Ruritanian judge, a participant in her own system, it is too evil to be given effect here (E43.1). "That is a fact" hides an evaluation of whose recognition counts.

Memory line

Sources are facts; naming law is a choice; opinion is evidence, never the answer.

  2. That is not the structure. It is a description followed by a norm.
  3. Correct: official acceptance is not "the views people hold".
  4. These are not the flaws. The statement joins a description to a norm and confuses positivism's social fact with opinion.
Fact to norm; opinion versus acceptance.
theoristsWhat is acceptance for Hart?

  2. That is Austin's habit, which Hart supersedes.
  3. Officials' acceptance is what matters.
  4. Correct. Acceptance is not approval.

key ideaWhat does E37.6 say about Hume's gap?

  2. From what people made we learn what they did.
  3. Overstated: facts fix the stakes.
  4. No fact alone yields an ought.
Only logical.

Recognition, change, adjudication.
E42

Positivism and natural law at cross purposes

The question exam-style

"Positivists describe what law is and natural lawyers say what it ought to be, so they debate at cross purposes. Positivism is neutral description; natural law is for the devout, and would license any judge to strike down whatever he dislikes."

The hypo Hypo 13, the Minister

The Minister tells Justice Siti: "Natural law is for the devout, and it would let any judge strike down whatever she dislikes. Our national philosophy is pragmatism." Respond, and say whether positivism would serve her better.

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

"Cross purposes" has two readings, and the statement uses the wrong one. Both schools ask what law is. The marker wants you to correct each caricature: positivism is not simply neutral, and natural law is neither religious nor a licence.

The answer in one breath

By natural law I mean the view that law's validity or authority depends in part on morality, strongly or only at the extreme. The statement treats identifying law as neutral and the debate as a division of labour. It is not: both schools ask what law is. Positivism's two theses, law as social fact and no necessary link with morality, are coherent, but treating official recognition as conclusive is a choice. And natural law, in its weak form, claims to say what law is at the extremes, without asking anyone's faith. So the debate is real, though narrower than its reputation.

Section 1: "cross purposes" and "neutral description"

Both schools ask what law is (block E42.1). On one view there is no debate, one school describing and the other prescribing. But treating official recognition as conclusive is a choice. To equate law with what people regard as law is to evaluate, not merely describe, and one may ask whether the claim that law is what accords with a master rule is itself a description. Finnis doubts that law can be described from a "rubbish heap" of facts without criteria of importance, and adds that natural law supports positive law rather than competing with it. A trap: "no necessary connection" still allows contingent links, so a legal rule with moral content troubles no positivist.

Close: positivism's description smuggles in an evaluation, so it is not simply neutral. Nor is it democratic by nature: Hart needs only officials to accept the rules, while citizens may merely obey, so law nears whatever is effectively enforced, and positivism is "not necessarily democratic" (TSH). Nazi law shows the stakes: positivism calls it law so that it can be condemned, and Radbruch denies the name to its worst statutes. Hart's case for positivism is that separation keeps a critical stance; Alexy replies that only a strong connection invites uncritical acceptance.

Section 2: "for the devout" and "a licence"

Natural law, unlike divine law, can be apprehended by reason (block E42.3). So the charge of an elite of "high priests" is mistaken, and the charge that it conflates law with morals attacks a straw man, since no natural lawyer wants every sin made a crime (TSH). Aquinas distinguishes four kinds of law, eternal, divine, natural and human, and Grotius made natural law secular. Nor does it license any judge to strike down what he dislikes: the Radbruch formula voids only intolerably unjust statutes, and for Finnis an unjust law is no nullity to disregard. So the statement hits only strong natural law; weak natural law strips legal status only at extreme divergence, such as a law to execute all redheads. Concede the hard question: TSH admits "no easy answer" on who pronounces natural law, so judges' authority below the threshold is doubtful.

Close: nor is a natural-law ruling mere fiat. Unlike a tyrant's whim it must cohere with a system of principles, can be tested by Socratic questioning, and stays open to revision (TSH).

Section 3: Fuller and Dworkin

The statement's two boxes leave out the theorists who fit neither (block E42.4). Fuller and Dworkin are natural law's allies, not natural lawyers in all but name: neither lets injustice as such defeat validity. Fuller's is a thin, procedural natural law: legality demands rules, whether fair or unfair. Dworkin's judge must interpret the legal history found, and Dworkin does not say what follows when no reading both fits and avoids intolerable injustice. Where the practice has adopted Radbruch's formula, his judge can depart at intolerable injustice and still fit.

Use the comparison card (E42.5) only to choose which cells to write, never to copy it: what law is, what law may enforce, and who decides, theorist by theorist.

Close: concede the statement's grain of truth. Hart and Fuller arguably talk across purposes, since one may agree with Fuller on Nazi Germany and with Hart or Radbruch on the grudge informer. Hart and Radbruch do not: what divides them, Fuller says, is how to state the problem. So the line between the schools runs through the extreme case, not between description and prescription.

If the statement defines law as command or habit

Add a middle section: Austin's command theory from the will kit, then the internal "ought" (E42.2). Hart's rules carry an internal "ought", a critical reflective attitude, but that "ought" need not be moral: officials may accept a rule from self-interest, habit, tradition or even fear, so long as they use it as a standard. Dworkin presses: many Nazi officials obeyed only from fear, so if that is acceptance Hart nears Austin, and if not there was no law there. The reply: most of its judges did use the rules as standards, so positivism calls that law law, and identifying it is what lets us condemn it.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is clarity (E37.7): sources usually identify law, and calling law a fact keeps criticism honest. It does not show that identifying law is value-free.

My weak point (E37.8, without its last sentence): describing is not praising, and Dworkin is arguably unfair to positivism; soft positivism lets the master rule adopt moral tests. I concede it: the evaluation is about what counts as law, not approval of it.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is wrong that the schools talk past each other, and partly right about each. Positivism rightly ties law to sources; natural law rightly denies that anything whatever can be law. Naming law is an evaluation, so the choice between them is live. Hart, Finnis, TSH, Fuller and Dworkin share the question what law is; they split on whether its answer ever depends on morality, and if so only at the extreme (weak natural law) or throughout (Dworkin). Repaired: “Law's sources are matters of social fact, but what law should be is not; extreme injustice is the narrow case where the two questions meet.” For a judge or citizen, this means that calling a rule law settles what it is, never whether to apply or obey it at the extreme.

Watch: E37 answers the statement that law is only social fact; E42 answers statements about the two schools as such. Never write both in full.

Using it in the hypo

"For the devout" is wrong: natural law is grasped by reason, not faith. "Strike down whatever she dislikes" describes only strong natural law; the real threshold is intolerable injustice. Pragmatism is itself a theory, which TSH says "glosses over" principle (E12.1). Positivism would not free the judge from choosing how to read Part 4.

Memory line

Same question, different answers at the extreme; naming law is a choice; natural law is reason, not faith, and a threshold, not a licence.

  2. This is the reverse. Both schools ask what law is.
  3. That is not accurate. Natural lawyers ask what law is, and their answer includes a moral test.
  4. Some do, but that is not the reply.

Reason, not faith.

How these essays fit together

E37 is about law as social fact and opinion; E42 is about the two schools as such. They share the clarity steelman (E37.7) and the own-side line (E37.8), and both rest on TSH's point that naming law is an evaluation. If both are on one paper, give E37 Hart's acceptance, the slide from official practice to opinion, and Hume's gap; give E42 the caricatures of each school, Fuller and Dworkin, and the internal "ought". Write the clarity steelman once.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • E37: "people make law" is a truism; "solely" turns it into a theory.
  • Positivism's social fact is what officials accept, not what people think.
  • Naming law is an evaluation (TSH); positivism is "not necessarily democratic".
  • Facts about opinion fix the stakes; Hume's gap is only logical; the dignity premise is moral.
  • E42: both schools ask what law is; positivism's description smuggles in an evaluation.
  • Natural law is reason, not faith; a threshold at the extreme, not a licence.
  • Fuller and Dworkin are allies, not natural lawyers in all but name.
  • Repaired: sources are facts; what law should be is not; extreme injustice is where the questions meet.
