Video 17Unjust law: the duty to obey and the Radbruch formulaWhat law is

E38

Law, morality and the duty to obey

The question 2020-21 Q1

"It is absurd to think that law is necessarily connected to morality. Such a view licenses people to disobey any law they consider unjust, which is a recipe for disaster."

The hypo Hypo 18(ii)

A law requires every adult to be vaccinated, with fines and, after a third refusal, six months' imprisonment, and no conscience exemption. Mdm Goh refuses: her conscience forbids it, she is a "sovereign citizen" whom Parliament's laws do not bind, and she will accept any punishment. Is she under any obligation to obey, and what would you say to her?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

Sentence 1 calls the link absurd; sentence 2 gives the reason, that it licenses private disobedience. "Absurd" is an absolute, and the reason is aimed at the wrong target.

The answer in one breath

The statement's natural ally, Hart, leaves the citizen the same question, stated honestly: validity never settles obedience. By a link between law and morality I mean the narrow claim that extreme injustice defeats legal character, not that every law must be just. A narrow link is a serious position, not an absurd one. And it is not what licenses disobedience, because obedience is a moral question on every theory, and every serious theory confines refusal to grave injustice.

Section 1: "absurd"

The link the statement attacks is narrower than it supposes. Use the kit on the law–morality link (E37.3).

  • Radbruch and Alexy reject the separation, narrowly. Positive law prevails even when unjust, unless the injustice reaches an intolerable degree; then, for the participant, it is not law. The weak connection keeps law open to critique; only the strong one risks uncritical legitimation. Positivism, Radbruch said, left jurist and people defenceless.
  • Finnis rejects "absurd" but keeps the distinction. An unjust law is no nullity to disregard; it lacks prima facie moral force, and falls short of law's central case, which treats subjects as partners in public reason.
  • Hart, in a clause: law as it is, is separate from law as it ought to be, and validity never settles whether to obey.

Close: Fuller turns the charge round. It is strict separation that gives no coherent meaning to the moral duty of fidelity to law, since it leaves no bridge between the duty to obey and our other moral duties. Only extreme injustice defeats legal character, so the link is serious, not absurd.

Section 2: "licenses disobedience"

"A recipe for disaster" is a reason to punish the disobedient, not a reason for her to obey; obedience is a moral question on every theory. Use the kit on valid law (E38.1).

  • Hart separates the questions. There is a legal duty to obey, but no moral duty follows from validity alone, and past a certain degree of iniquity there is a moral duty to disobey.
  • Fuller grounds obedience in reciprocity. The legal duty comes in degrees and vanishes on total failure; the moral duty lasts until reciprocity's final and complete rupture.

Then the three questions (E38.4). The statement merges three questions: is there a legal duty to obey, a moral duty to obey, and a moral duty to disobey? Hart: a legal duty; no moral duty from validity alone; a duty to disobey past a certain iniquity. Fuller: a legal duty by degree; a moral duty from reciprocity until its rupture. Radbruch and Alexy: no legal duty past intolerable injustice. Finnis: no prima facie moral duty from unjust law, though one can arise lest a largely just system weaken.

Close: there is a strong default of obedience, which grave injustice can defeat and mere disagreement cannot. The pigeon feeder, a lecture example, shows its ground. A woman feeds pigeons where it is forbidden: deterrence gives her no moral reason to obey; fair play does, since she gains from institutions others uphold, and a positivist has the same reason. History even runs the other way: post-war German courts denied a fallen regime's informer statutes legal character partly so that people would not take the law into their own hands while waiting for a statute (Fuller).

Section 3: "justifiably disobey"

The formula is a threshold, not a licence. Start with Hart from the disobedience kit (E38.2): he leaves the citizen the same question, stated honestly. Validity never settles obedience, so the positivist citizen too must ask whether a law is too evil to obey. Separating law from morality moves the question of disobedience; it does not remove it. This is the one place to make Hart's point in full.

Then E38.5: the statement's licence to disobey states the strong connection thesis, which no serious non-positivist defends (Alexy). The formula voids only intolerable injustice: in a stable democracy legislation stands unless that threshold is met, shown by broad historical consensus, which checks idiosyncratic ideologies (TSH). Ordinary injustice is Parliament's to repeal. Deviating can be unjust too, since the citizen's or judge's morality may be wrong (Hoerster), and Radbruch himself warned of the formula's "frightful dangers".

If the statement doubts that the moral ground can be shown, add the legitimacy kit (E38.3): unprovable is not arbitrary. If it names the citizen, add the citizen's question (E38.7): validity tells her the rule is law; whether to obey is a moral question; while the regime lasts she bears the consequences of refusal either way, so refusal must be open, rare and aimed at grave injustice.

Close: Alexy adds that the more extreme the injustice, the more certain the knowledge of it, so the threshold sits where a private mistake is least likely. The Federal Constitutional Court found Ordinance 11, the Nazi decree stripping citizenship on racist criteria, not only intolerable but "evident". The real problem is who decides, and that problem binds the positivist too.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is candour (E38.6). Denying evil law the name breeds romantic optimism that all values fit one system, and insincerity about a dilemma; Hart thought the formula useless while the regime lasted and unneeded after. And a moral test of validity seems to hand each citizen a licence to disobey whatever he thinks unjust. This shows that candour is a virtue and that obedience needs its own argument. It does not show that a narrow link is absurd, or that it licenses disobedience.

My weak point (E38.8): my answer relies on the Radbruch formula. Answering relativism needs a rational justification for some minimum moral requirements, and Hoerster's judge with a gravely unjust personal morality shows the risk. I concede the formula is only as sound as that core; broad historical consensus disciplines judges but is evidence, not proof. Radbruch himself preferred a retroactive statute for the informers.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right that private conscience cannot be a licence, and wrong to blame the moral link for it. Hart, Fuller, Radbruch, Alexy and Finnis all confine refusal to grave injustice; they split only on whether that injustice also defeats legal character. Repaired: “Citizens owe a strong moral duty to obey, which grave injustice can defeat and mere disagreement cannot.” For the citizen, this means obedience as the default and refusal only at grave injustice.

Watch: the actor is the citizen, so no judge's hat. The law-and-justice kit and the law–morality kit are near twins: never write both in full.

Using it in the hypo

"Not bound" is false: whether a law binds is a claim about what officials accept (E45.12, Hart). Whether she should obey is a moral question. Fair play and harm to others weigh heavily; conscience may justify open refusal while accepting the penalty, but this law is not gravely unjust. Advise obedience, or open and non-evasive refusal (E38.7).

Memory line

Validity never settles obedience on any theory; the link is narrow, and refusal is for grave injustice only.

E43

The Radbruch formula under test

The question exam-style

"Once the highest court has held an unjust rule to be law, it is law. To deny it the name is confused, useless, uncertain, undemocratic and dishonest." Or: are there good reasons, from the participant's point of view, to support Radbruch's thesis?

The hypo Hypo 11

After Borduria's Council falls: Mrs Kova, wanting to be rid of her husband, had reported his private remarks against the Council under Decree 9 (circulated only to officials); he was sentenced to death, sent to a labour camp instead, and survived. Judge Ferro applied Decree 14 in 300 cases: "Law is law. My conscience belongs to my private life." (i) Would you support a retrospective statute? (ii) Without one, would you convict Mrs Kova under the old Penal Code? (iii) Should Ferro be prosecuted?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

The statement runs two questions together: is the rule law, and must anyone apply or obey it? "Law" has two readings, the observer's and the participant's. The marker wants Alexy's replies to the objections, and Hart's candour faced squarely.

The answer in one breath

I agree only in part. For an observer the rule is law, and Hart is right that calling it so keeps criticism honest. For a participant who must decide, extreme injustice is the narrow case where the label matters, and Alexy answers the objections to denying it well enough. But the formula is only as sound as the minimum moral core it assumes.

Section 1: "it is law"

Whether it is law depends on who is asking (block E43.1). From the observer's standpoint there is no necessary link with morality: a value-neutral word is needed for norms that are authoritatively issued and socially effective, and Alexy grants that. A participant is in a different position. The court, the lawyer and the law's addressee join a procedure that claims correctness, and extreme injustice cannot meet that claim. So a judge applying Ordinance 11, which stripped citizenship on racist criteria, must say more than "it is law": refusing it on legal grounds, he calls it law at first sight, but in the end no law at all. A ruling of the highest court is law for observers and for positivists; for participants it too claims correctness, so at extreme injustice a later court may call it legally wrong. Hart wins on labels for observers and historians; a judge deciding today needs more than candour. A foreign judge is both: an observer of the regime's system and a participant in his own.

Add Radbruch and Alexy from the law-and-justice kit: positive law prevails even when unjust, unless its injustice reaches an intolerable degree; every judicial decision claims correctness, which extreme injustice cannot meet; and Alexy grants that candour is the strongest objection, not a fatal one.

Close: a judge who refuses the rule on legal grounds calls it law at first sight and, in the end, no law at all.

Section 2: the objections

The objections bite on the strong connection thesis, which no serious non-positivist defends. Take three or four of Alexy's eight replies (card E43.2), never all eight, and never candour here.

  • Clarity. Objection: speak plainly, and treat injustice as a matter of ethics, not legality (Hart). Reply: a concept must be adequate before it is simple; at the extreme the ethical problem is also a legal one.
  • Effectiveness. Objection: a definition cannot stop a tyrant, and linking law to morality breeds uncritical acceptance. Reply: largely conceded for the lone judge; but only the strong thesis legitimates, and the "risk effect" deters officials who are neither saints nor heroes, since they may later be prosecuted.
  • Legal certainty. Objection: judges will differ about justice. Reply: the more extreme the injustice, the more certain the knowledge of it, so the formula gives certainty precedence except at the extreme.
  • Relativism. Objection: no notion of justice can be rationally justified, and the judge's morality may itself be gravely unjust (Hoerster). Reply: the formula needs only a rudimentary core, and in a state steeped in rights, consensus would thwart that judge. The core is asserted here, not proved.
  • Democracy. Objection: the legislature, not judges, should void unjust laws. Reply: the formula covers only a core area, far less than ordinary rights review, so the objection would abolish judicial review.
  • Dispensability. Objection: a new legislature can pass a retroactive statute. Reply: in criminal law the rule against retroactive punishment may bar that; outside crime, a citizen should not have to suffer an unjust ruling to prompt the legislator.

Language is arguably the weakest objection, and effectiveness is speculative both ways. Alexy's verdict: the objections are answered well enough to tie the score; the risk effect, necessity after a regime collapses and fit with the rule against retroactive punishment break the tie.

Close: limited to intolerable injustice, the formula costs little certainty and no more democracy than judicial review does.

Section 3: candour

Candour is the strongest objection, not a fatal one (block E43.3). Whether an evil statute is "law" is a definitional quarrel, neither side less honest: Hart judges the effect of each usage, Alexy the concept. It is no mere quarrel about words, because the label decides who may lawfully refuse and who may shelter behind the statute, and even a quarrel about words matters where words have "a powerful effect on human attitudes" (Fuller). Hart's case is that candour is no minor virtue: where punishment needs retroactivity, choose openly "between two evils" by statute, since "the thing to do with a moral quandary is not to hide it".

Fuller has two replies. A retroactive statute must itself treat the regime's laws as void, so the question becomes only who should do the dirty work, the courts or the legislature; and positivism never gives the obligation of fidelity to law a coherent meaning. Alexy's reply is that where the injustice was evident at the time, the court ascertains the law then in force, so there is no hidden retroactivity. Two corrections keep the debate fair: Hart wrongly supposed that the post-war court voided a statute, as he later conceded; and Radbruch did not oppose a statute, since for the informers he too preferred a retroactive one.

Then the grudge informer case itself (E24.2). In 1944 a woman denounced her husband's anti-Hitler remarks out of a personal grudge; he was sentenced to death and sent to the front; in 1949 she was convicted under the 1871 Criminal Code. Whether the regime's statute was "law" decides who bears the cost of past injustice. Hart left two honest options: let her go, or punish under a frankly retrospective law. Fuller would void the regime's laws for informers, at least that statute. On Alexy's reading the court voided no statute, as Hart later conceded.

Close: where the injustice was evident at the time, the court finds what the law then was, so nothing is hidden.

In a stable democracy

The formula is a narrow check, not an unelected veto (E43.4). Drawing on common morality, it is arguably a transitional safeguard, a tool of resistance, and idle once morality has fully shifted. German courts applied it to a fallen regime's laws; against a sitting legislature it is untested. Whether striking a law down under the formula differs from striking it down as unconstitutional, and whether adopting a supreme Constitution adopts the formula, are open questions. TSH answers that the formula gives "meaningful constitutional judicial review with inbuilt restraints on judicial power", since broad historical consensus leaves local and current opinion to Parliament. Then E38.5 from its "deviating" sentence: deviating can be unjust too, since the judge's morality may be wrong.

If asked about Radbruch's own words, use his three tiers (E43.6). First, positive law prevails even when unjust. Second, at an "intolerable level", it is "flawed law" that must yield to justice. Third, where equality is deliberately betrayed, it lacks the very nature of law. So a statute aimed at one group by race fails on the third tier, and no role excuses applying it (Thunder).

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

If the statement attacks the formula, the best case is candour (E38.6), as in E38. If it takes Radbruch's side, the best case is equal moral worth (E43.5): constitutional supremacy rests most soundly on "equal moral worth", so the Constitution's articles may legitimately import a higher law, including Radbruch's formula, which he wrote, Alexy reports, to "arm jurists against the recurrence of a rogue state". This shows that extreme injustice can defeat validity. It does not show that ordinary injustice does.

My weak point (E38.8): the formula is only as sound as its minimum moral core; broad historical consensus is evidence, not proof.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is overstated: validity never settles what the actor should do. Hart, Radbruch and Alexy agree in condemning Ordinance 11; they split only on whether to call it law, which decides who may lawfully refuse. A law surviving the formula is not thereby just: that question stays with Parliament and conscience. Repaired: “Law and morality may be separable in identifying most law, but validity never settles obedience; extreme injustice is the narrow case where the two questions meet.” For the judge, this means applying the rule unless its injustice is intolerable, and refusing it on legal grounds if it is.

Watch: E38 answers the citizen's version (a moral link licenses disobedience). If E38 is in the same script, the threshold block stays there and this essay runs on the observer and participant, Alexy's replies and candour.

Using it in the hypo

(i) Hart's honest option is a frankly retroactive law, but nulla poena (no punishment without a prior law) weighs against it. (ii) Decree 9 was secret, which fails Fuller's promulgation, and its injustice was evident at the time, so on Alexy's view the court finds what the law then was. (iii) "My conscience belongs to my private life" is Radbruch's pre-war positivism; Decree 14 betrays equality (E43.6), and the risk effect reaches Ferro, who was neither a saint nor a hero.

Memory line

Observer or participant? The formula is a threshold, not a licence; candour is the strongest objection, not a fatal one.

Before the war.

How these essays fit together

E38 is the citizen's question: may I disobey? E43 is the judge's and observer's question: is it law? Both rest on the narrow link (only intolerable injustice) and on Hart's candour. If both are on one paper, give E38 the duty to obey (the three questions, the pigeon feeder, the threshold) and give E43 the observer and participant, Alexy's replies and the grudge informer case. "The more extreme, the more certain" belongs to E43.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • E38: a narrow link is serious, not absurd; strict separation gives fidelity no coherent meaning (Fuller).
  • "A recipe for disaster" is a reason to punish, not to obey; three questions about obedience.
  • The pigeon feeder: fair play, not deterrence, grounds the duty, for positivists too.
  • The formula is a threshold, not a licence; Hart leaves the citizen the same question.
  • E43: observer or participant? For the participant, extreme injustice defeats the claim to correctness.
  • Alexy's replies: clarity, effectiveness, certainty, relativism, democracy, dispensability.
  • Candour: the strongest objection, not fatal; the grudge informer case.
  • In a stable democracy: a narrow check, untested against a sitting legislature.
  • Repaired: validity never settles obedience; extreme injustice is where the questions meet.

Video 18More than force: legitimacy and the rule of lawWhat law is

E39

Law against force, and legitimacy

The question exam-style

"The distinction between law and force is hollow. Every attempt to justify law's legitimacy is either an unprovable moral assertion or a mere formula for effectiveness."

The hypo Hypo 11(iv)

A former Council official argues that Decrees 9 and 14 were law, because the Council was habitually obeyed and its courts accepted them, and that "law is whatever is enacted". A scholar replies that the whole debate is a quarrel about words. Evaluate both.

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

"Either … or" is a false dilemma, and the statement refutes itself: its own claim is no more provable than the justifications it dismisses. The marker wants the two horns taken apart and a third answer named.

The answer in one breath

By legitimacy I mean a right to rule that grounds a moral duty to obey, not mere power to compel. By "hollow" the statement means that nothing real separates law from a gunman's orders. The distinction between force and law is thin in an unjust system, but not hollow: law governs by rules that bind the ruler, no theory treats effectiveness as legitimacy, and a justification that cannot be proved can still be examined. And "either … or" hides a third answer, reciprocity: terms between rulers and ruled, neither a moral proof nor a measure of effect.

Section 1: force and law

Law differs from force first in its form. On Austin's account a warlord's orders would count as law, which is why Hart supersedes him. Use the will kit (E37.2).

  • Austin: law is the command of a sovereign, habitually obeyed and obeying no one, backed by a sanction. His strength is candour: law's existence is one thing and its merit another. But habits miss the internal "ought", and power-conferring rules enable rather than require.
  • Fuller: the command view confuses fidelity to law with deference to authority. Legal form binds rulers too. King Rex, who failed all eight of Fuller's requirements, made no law at all: his subjects were faithful to him, not to his law.
  • Hart: law is more than commands obeyed from habit. Its rules carry an internal "ought", and secondary rules of recognition, change and adjudication bind the will that makes law.

Close: even a selfish ruler, Fuller argues, needs the "minimum self-restraint" that connects his words to his actions. Rules that bind the ruler are what a gunman never gives.

Section 2: effectiveness

The effectiveness horn confuses existence with legitimacy: an unjust system can meet Hart's test for a legal system, since acceptance is not approval. Use the legitimacy kit (E38.3).

  • Austin takes the effectiveness horn: law is the command habitually obeyed. But that cannot explain why officials treat law as binding, or why its subjects can criticise it by its own rules.
  • Hart says when law exists, not that existing makes it legitimate. A system exists when primary rules are generally obeyed and officials accept secondary rules. Describing law is not praising it, and his minimum content of natural law, drawn from truisms such as human vulnerability, is a practical necessity, consistent, Harris notes, with institutionalised slavery.
  • From Hart's social-fact cell: acceptance is not approval; it can come from self-interest, habit or tradition. And from his duty-to-obey cell, in a clause: no moral duty follows from validity alone.

Close: Finnis comes nearest this horn: law's central case needs an effective authority, but the common good, not effectiveness, justifies it. Nor is legality mere efficacy, like a knife's sharpness. It respects people as responsible agents, the state's power rests on a tacit reciprocity (Fuller), and published law exposes power to criticism.

Section 3: "unprovable"

The moral horn assumes that what cannot be proved is arbitrary. If Finnis crosses the is–ought gap, Harris replies, "we are not dealing with logical necessities", yet it is still reasoning. From the pivot (E39.1): unprovable is not arbitrary. A justification short of proof is still a reason open to challenge, which a gunman never gives. Law differs from force in its form (rules that bind the ruler), its claim (correctness, Alexy) and the reasons it owes (Rawls, Finnis, TSH).

Then Finnis, TSH and Rawls from the legitimacy kit: acceptance cannot alone found legitimacy. For a Constitution, the framers' will begs the question why it still binds us; treating non-revolt as acquiescence is artificial; and only co-equal dignity makes consent meaningful (TSH). Rawls's essentials, which all may reasonably endorse, are moral though political. The unproven is not the false.

Close: the statement's own claim is no more provable than the justifications it dismisses, so by its own test it would be hollow too. So the moral horn fails as well.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is Austin's. A command theory cannot tell a legislature's orders from a warlord's, and even Rawls grants that political power is always coercive power backed by sanctions. The crits add that doctrine legitimates hierarchy. This shows that law can be force in an unjust system. It does not show that it always is, or that every justification is hollow: law has its own character, applying its criteria by standards of universality and equity, so even rulers who use it can be held to it.

My weak point (the last three sentences of E39.1): I concede that in an unjust system the distinction thins. My answer rests on reasons I cannot prove. But that shows only that legitimacy is argued, and arguing is exactly what force never does.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right that legitimacy cannot be proved, and wrong that it is therefore hollow. Hart, Fuller, Finnis, TSH and Rawls all deny that effectiveness is legitimacy; they split on what supplies it: legality's reciprocity (Fuller), reasons all may reasonably endorse (Rawls) or co-equal dignity (TSH). Repaired: “Law is more than effective force, because it governs by rules that bind rulers too and owes citizens reasons they can examine; those reasons are argued, not proved, and where they fail obedience is no longer owed.” For the citizen, this means judging a government by whether its rules bind its rulers and its reasons can be examined, not by whether its legitimacy can be proved.

If the statement ends "so obey" and starts from will, use E41; otherwise use the valid-law kit (E38.1, Hart and Fuller) as section 3.

Using it in the hypo

The official's test of law is Austin's, which Hart supersedes. But on Hart's own test the Decrees may have been law, since officials accepted them, and identifying them as law is what lets us condemn them (E42.2). Decree 9's secrecy fails promulgation (E44.1). The label is no mere quarrel about words: it decides who may lawfully refuse (E43.3).

Memory line

Unprovable is not arbitrary; effective is not legitimate; arguing is what force never does.

  2. That is the conclusion, not the horns.

  4. Correct. The statement's two horns: any justification of legitimacy is either a moral claim that cannot be proved, or just a formula for whatever is effective.

key ideaWhy is effective not legitimate?
  1. Legitimacy needs reasons, not just legality.
  2. Austin takes the effectiveness horn; Hart supersedes him.

  4. That is not the argument. The point is that existing and being legitimate are different questions.

  2. These are not the essay's three. Law differs from force in its form, its claim to correctness and the reasons it owes.
  3. A warlord can have all three, so these do not separate law from force.
  4. These are not the essay's three. Law differs from force in its form, its claim to correctness and the reasons it owes.
Form, claim, reasons.
hypoHypo 11(iv): a scholar says the debate is "a quarrel about words". Reply?
  1. Judges after the regime must decide.
  2. Correct (E43.3). Whether the decrees count as law matters in practice: it decides who could lawfully refuse them and who may shelter behind them.
  3. They disagree about the participant's position.
  4. That is the official's Austinian view.
What turns on the label?
E44

The rule of law: efficiency, justice or slogan

The question exam-style · 2014-15 Q1 relative

"The rule of law is mere efficiency, which any tyrant could follow." Or: "The rule of law guarantees justice." Or: "The rule of law is a ruling-class slogan." Or rank, test or apply Fuller's requirements.

The hypo Hypo 12

The Law Minister proposes: mandatory caning for third-time littering ("treating like cases alike"); a new offence of lending bank accounts to scammers, applying to accounts lent since last January; a statute validating 2,000 marriages defective only because the registrar's appointment had lapsed; keeping an unused 50-year-old offence "as a signal"; and asking the court to overrule its precedent so that "life imprisonment" means natural life. Which would you support, and how would you rank the rule-of-law concerns?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

"Rule of law" has two readings, formal and substantive; a substantive sense would answer the question by definition. One thesis serves every form: legality is more than efficiency and less than justice. Choose sections by form: efficiency or justice uses the efficiency section then the justice section; slogan uses the slogan section then justice; a Fuller task uses the requirements and one pivot, then applies them.

The answer in one breath

By the rule of law I mean its formal sense, Fuller's eight requirements, the conditions under which people can follow rules as made and applied. For Fuller, total failure of one requirement leaves no legal system at all; short of that, legality is a matter of degree. I agree with the statement only in part. The rule of law is a real but thin morality: it binds rulers, respects subjects as responsible agents and exposes aims to criticism, but it cannot make bad laws just.

Fuller's checklist

Can people follow this rule as made and applied? Is it (1) general, (2) promulgated, (3) prospective, (4) clear, (5) consistent, (6) possible to obey, (7) constant over time, and (8) congruent with official action? Rex failed all eight and made no law. Traps: generality means rules at all, not breadth, so case-by-case justice and unfettered discretion fail it; promulgation needs access, not universal knowledge; and the bar on retroactivity is aimed at abuse.

Section 1: moral or not

Legality is moral, but thin. Use the law-and-justice kit (E20.4).

  • Fuller: legality is a real but thin morality. Total failure of one of its eight requirements leaves no legal system; short of that, it comes in degrees. It binds even a self-seeking ruler and treats people as responsible agents. But it demands rules, whether fair or unfair, and unjust aims can run through clear, public rules.
  • Hart separates them, for candour. Justice in administering the law treats like cases alike by any standard of "like", so it is compatible with very iniquitous law.
  • Radbruch and Alexy: a narrow link. Positive law prevails even when unjust, unless its injustice reaches an intolerable degree.

Close: legality binds even a self-seeking ruler, yet unjust aims can run through clear, public rules.

Section 2: "mere efficiency"

The charge, chiefly Hart's, is that legality concerns efficacy, not morality, like the rules of good carpentry or a knife's sharpness, which serve good ends and bad alike. From the soundness card: is Fuller's legality morality or mere efficacy? It respects responsible agents and rests on reciprocity, but cannot make laws just. And from E44.5: reciprocity grounds the duty to obey; where officials disregard rules, obedience at some point becomes futile, like casting a vote that will never be counted.

Close: Fuller's reply is that law, unlike carpentry, governs human beings, so its craft cannot be morally idle. Efficiency would not explain why legality binds the ruler too, or why its failure releases the citizen from the duty to obey. So legality is thin, but it is a morality, not a tool.

Section 2: "a ruling-class slogan"

On the Marxist view law is superstructure, the instrument of the ruling class, and the crits' charge is that legality presents a hierarchy as neutral rules. From the politics kit: each principle meets a counterprinciple (Unger), and doctrine legitimates hierarchy even in progressive hands; Harris replies that the crits must show more "incremental justice" than reformed legalism.

Close: law has its own character, applying its criteria by standards of universality and equity. A slogan binds no one, and legality binds the ruler too: published, prospective rules can be held against those who made them.

Section 3: "a guarantee of justice"

Legality secures formal justice, treating like cases alike, and that is compatible with very iniquitous law (Hart).

Close: Fuller grants that legality can serve a wide variety of aims, though not every aim without cost to legality. The Nazi regime he describes needed secret laws, a retroactive statute ratifying its own past lawless acts, and statutes undermined by uncontrolled discretion. Ordinance 11, which stripped citizenship on racist criteria, had legal form, yet the Federal Constitutional Court found it had nothing in common with law and justice. So legality is a condition of justice, not a guarantee.

The Fuller task: the requirements, and one pivot

The requirements are not equal, and they can conflict, as curative retroactivity shows. Take the one pivot the task names.

  • Ranking (E44.2): Fuller lets priority vary by branch of law. Rank prospectivity in crime first: he finds the retrospective criminal statute "universally condemned". What ranks low is an isolated breach: the non-enforcement of s 377A strained congruence for one rule but left the system standing. Congruence itself ranks high, since without it the other seven are idle.
  • Discretion (E44.3): only uncontrolled discretion turns law into rule by men. Enforcement only against critics approaches it. Hart adds the cost of laws kept but not enforced: "formidable discriminatory powers". Discretion fits legality if bounded by law and reviewable; prosecutorial discretion is subject to equality (Ramalingam). Nor are clear rules always better: specious clarity can harm more than honest vagueness.
  • Retroactivity (E44.4): the core objection is the new crime, commanding someone today to do something yesterday, which even Hart grants may be "odious". His honest option for the grudge informer case he and Fuller debated was a frankly retrospective statute, a choice between two evils. Overruling in crime differs: it would leave people "branded as criminals" for relying on an earlier interpretation, which is why the Court of Appeal in Abdul Nasir (1997) changed the meaning of a sentence prospectively only. Finnis reaches the abuse from substance: law treats citizens as partners in public reason. Retroactivity that lightens a sentence is unobjectionable.
  • Crisis and secret law (E44.5): "grim necessity" may justify a secret weapons appropriation, but no emergency justifies a secret law creating a new crime (Fuller). A single secret or retroactive rule grounds no moral duty.
  • Unenforced law (E44.6): calling it "a sham" overstates. Open non-enforcement strains congruence, yet one rule, unlike a system, survives rare enforcement. For TSH the 2007 announcement breached Fuller's requirement but sent a competing symbolic message; Tan Seng Kee later gave the promise legal force. Law can exempt the trivial openly, as the Penal Code does for slight harm.

Close: breaches compound, since unclarity may force retroactive laws, so a low rank never licenses neglect.

Applied, Fuller task

Asked of a given law, Fuller's question is whether people can follow it as made and applied. Close: if the law fails a requirement, it is less a law, not no law. Rex failed all eight and made no law; one secret or retroactive rule makes only that rule less a law.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

For efficiency or slogan, the best case is that legality is not moral (E44.7): a tyrant could meet every requirement, so the label "morality" seems misapplied; a clear, public, prospective law can still be unjust. That shows legality is thin, not that it is morally empty: it binds the ruler too and rests on reciprocity. For justice, the best case is that legality is enough (E44.8): law brings power into the light, so a government that announces its aims openly can be criticised, organised against and fled, while arbitrary power shows no pattern. That shows thin legality protects, not that it guarantees justice: rules also legitimise and spread injustice.

My weak point: legality is thin. A clear, public, prospective law can still be unjust, and Fuller did not adopt the Radbruch formula: even in the grudge informer case, he argued chiefly from the Nazi regime's collapse of legality, not from the injustice of the statute's content. I concede it. So I claim for legality only what it can do, bind rulers and expose their aims, and leave intolerable content to the formula.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right only in part. Fuller, Hart, Radbruch and Alexy all accept that legality matters; they split on whether it is a morality (Fuller), a condition of efficacy (Hart), or a floor beneath a narrow test of content (Radbruch and Alexy). Legality is thin and cannot guarantee just laws: intolerable injustice is the Radbruch formula's work. Repaired: “The rule of law is a real but thin morality that binds rulers and comes in degrees; it cannot make bad laws just.” For the legislator, this means that keeping Fuller's requirements is necessary to make law, and never enough to make it just. If the statement draws an "ought" from legality, close on Hume's gap (E37.6).

Using it in the hypo

Rank the measures. The retroactive new offence is worst (E44.4); the curative statute validating marriages is fine; the signal offence strains congruence (E44.6); and overruling in crime against the accused should apply only to the future, as in Abdul Nasir. Mandatory caning treats like cases alike by any standard of "like" (Hart), which gives certainty, not justice (E45.7).

Memory line

More than efficiency, less than justice: a thin morality that binds the ruler too. Rex made no law.

Who does it bind?

How these essays fit together

E39 asks whether law differs from force at all; E44 asks what legality is worth. Both lean on Fuller: legal form binds the ruler, and legality rests on reciprocity. E39's middle horn, effectiveness, is answered partly by E44's reply to "mere efficiency". If both are on one paper, give E39 Austin, Hart's acceptance and the legitimacy kit; give E44 Fuller's eight requirements, the efficiency, slogan and justice sections, and the pivots. Write King Rex once.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • E39: a false dilemma; reciprocity is the third answer.
  • Law differs from force in form (rules that bind the ruler), claim (correctness) and reasons owed.
  • Effective is not legitimate; acceptance is not approval.
  • Unprovable is not arbitrary; the statement fails its own test.
  • E44: legality is more than efficiency and less than justice.
  • Fuller's eight: general, promulgated, prospective, clear, consistent, possible, constant, congruent.
  • Not mere efficiency: law governs people, binds the ruler, and its failure releases the citizen.
  • Not a slogan: legality binds those who made it. Not a guarantee: unjust aims can run through clear rules.
  • Repaired: a real but thin morality, in degrees; it cannot make bad laws just.

Video 21Liberty sacrificed, and scepticism to legitimacyLaw-making

E6

Liberty inevitably sacrificed

▲ likely

The question exam-style

"In the pursuit of moral values in law-making, liberty is inevitably sacrificed." Do you agree? Discuss with reference to the theories you have studied.

The hypo Hypo 5, MP limb

A bill makes it an offence (up to five years) to publish anything "offensive to the religious feelings of any group", prosecutions needing the Minister's consent with no guidelines, and lets the Minister order corrections of statements he thinks false. Its mover says: "Protecting harmony and truth inevitably costs some liberty; that is the price." How would you vote?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

The statement sets moral values against liberty, as if liberty were not one of them. And "inevitably sacrificed" claims more than a cost: "inevitably" is an absolute, and "sacrificed" has two readings.

The answer in one breath

The statement is right that every criminal prohibition takes some liberty from someone, so law-making that punishes in the name of morality always pays in liberty. By the pursuit of moral values I mean law-making aimed at what is right or good, whether by punishing, regulating or promoting. It is wrong in three ways. Liberty is itself a moral value, so pursuing moral values can protect or widen it. "Pursuit" need not mean punishment: law can regulate or promote at far less cost to liberty. And "inevitably sacrificed" overstates: the basic liberties need not be touched at all, and freedom from coercion is limited, not sacrificed, when the limit rests on reasons as strong as its coercion.

Section 1: moral values against liberty

The statement sets moral values against liberty, as if liberty were not one of them (block E1.8). It is one: Hart's case against enforcing morality is itself a critical morality, since coercion is "prima facie objectionable" and free choice may be valuable in itself. So a legislature protecting liberty pursues a moral value, and "inevitably" fails. Some moral law-making widens liberty: Shanmugam called repealing s 377A the right thing to do.

Then every theorist values liberty, for a different reason. For Rawls the basic liberties are political values, and political values are themselves moral; critics add that liberal neutrality itself assumes a view of the good, a life chosen from among many. Finnis places self-determination within practical reasonableness, and his common good is a set of conditions that lets each member realise the goods for himself. Even Devlin asks for the maximum freedom consistent with society's integrity. And the case for liberty is argued in moral terms too: in 2007 both camps spoke of right and of rights, and an opponent of s 377A rejected disgust as a ground once used to justify slavery (TSH).

Close: the conflict is between moral values, liberty among them, not between morality and liberty.

Section 2: "pursuit"

"Pursuit" need not mean punishment, and the cost to liberty falls with the coercion used. Use the ladder (E1.2) from Hart's insight: coercion is "prima facie objectionable", since it punishes offenders and constrains everyone else, so a good reason to regulate is not yet a reason to imprison. Promotion coerces less, though it is not free: school curricula, media rules and adoption law bear on citizens, so it too needs public reasons. Singapore's 2022 package fits: the criminal law withdrew from private consensual conduct, while Article 156 lets Parliament protect and promote marriage.

Close: Finnis is liberal in means for the same reason. Every society takes a public stand, yet his state keeps only peace and justice and does not set out to make people integrally good. So "inevitably" holds for punishment, not for regulation or promotion.

Section 3: "inevitably sacrificed", two senses of liberty

Liberty has two senses.

Narrowly, it means the basic liberties. The Constitution's rights chapter protects them against Parliament through Article 4, and for Rawls denying them "normally" breaks reciprocity, so a law pursuing moral values on public reasons need not touch them. That protection is thinner than it looks: Lim Meng Suang left the Hart and Devlin debate to Parliament, and Article 156 shields the man–woman definition of marriage from challenge under the rights chapter.

Broadly, liberty means freedom from coercion, and here every criminal law takes some. Even the Wolfenden Report kept the law out of private lives only "further than is necessary". But "sacrificed" has two readings. If it means some freedom is given up, the statement holds but is modest: it is true of every law, a rule on which side of the road to drive included, so it says nothing special about moral values. If it means liberty is overridden without due weight, it fails wherever the limit rests on reasons as strong as its coercion, as a law against harm to others does, since that law protects others' liberty.

Finnis shows the weighing: free speech serves knowledge and friendship, a law against hate speech protects other goods, and prudence asks whether it chills more than it protects. So the choice is between goods.

The statement is exactly right of one kind of morals law: punishing conduct because others are distressed to know of it. Hart shows that this would leave only the liberty to do what no one seriously objects to, which is "quite nugatory". So valuing liberty means at least refusing to count that distress as harm.

Close: liberty is inevitably limited, not inevitably sacrificed, and each limit needs reasons as strong as its coercion.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is the record of morals law. Devlin sets no theoretical limit to the law's power against immorality, and he names the cost: a common morality is part of the "bondage", and "the bondage is part of the price of society". In 2007 majority feeling kept s 377A, and announced non-enforcement left "formidable discriminatory powers" with police and prosecutors (Hart), who adds that coercion in intimate matters may cause misery of a special degree. This shows that morals law costs liberty, often more than its makers admit. It does not show that every pursuit of moral values does, or that the cost can never be justified.

My weak point: my answer says that a justified limit is no sacrifice, which risks defining the statement's worry away. I concede that who judges the reasons is itself contested: in 2007 both camps were arguably sure they were right. So I keep the ladder as a check that does not depend on winning that argument: punish last, and only on reasons others can examine.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right that every coercive law costs someone some liberty, and wrong that liberty is inevitably sacrificed. Liberty is itself a moral value, law can pursue values without punishing, and a limit resting on reasons as strong as its coercion is a justified limit, not a sacrifice. All four value liberty and all four accept some coercion; they split on what justifies it: harm and critical morality (Hart), the limits of tolerance (Devlin), public reasons (Rawls) or the common good (Finnis). Repaired: “Every coercive law limits someone's liberty, so law-making that pursues moral values must justify each limit by reasons as strong as its coercion, and some moral law-making widens liberty.” For the legislator, this means promoting or regulating before punishing, and punishing only on reasons others can examine.

Watch: E6 runs on E1's blocks, so never put it in the same script as E1, E5 or E8.

Using it in the hypo

Harmony and truth are moral values, and so is free speech, so the choice is between goods (Finnis), and prudence asks whether the bill chills more speech than it protects. Punishing offence to feelings counts distress as harm, which Hart says would leave a "quite nugatory" liberty. Unpublished guidelines and the Minister's discretion fail Fuller (E44.3). Vote against the bill and regulate incitement narrowly instead.

Memory line

Liberty is a moral value too; limited, not sacrificed, when the reasons match the force.

Distress at the bare knowledge.
E7

Scepticism, who decides and legitimacy

▲ likely

The question exam-style

Form (a): "No one can know what is morally right, so each person must decide for himself, so enforcing morality is illegitimate." Form (b): "No one can know better than anyone else, so the majority must decide, so enforcing its morality is legitimate."

The hypo Hypo 14, adapted

At the surrogacy commission, Commissioner A says: "Morality is just opinion, so each woman must decide for herself; any ban would be illegitimate." Commissioner K replies: "Precisely because no one knows better, the majority must decide, and 70% want a ban, so a ban is legitimate." Evaluate both.

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

A chain from scepticism to who decides to legitimacy. The flaw is the gap at each link: the conclusion about who decides does not follow from the premise, and the conclusion about legitimacy does not follow from who decides. Test each link in turn.

The answer in one breath

The statement draws a conclusion about who decides, each person or the majority, from a premise that no one can know what is right. By scepticism I mean doubt that moral questions have a right answer, or that it can be known, as distinct from doubt about a particular answer. By legitimate I mean enforcement the state may rightly undertake. Scepticism supports neither private autonomy nor majority rule, since each is itself a moral claim, and legitimacy turns on reasons and coercion, not on who decides alone.

Section 1: scepticism

The sceptical premise defeats itself, but its insight, fallibility, survives. Use the equal-validity kit (E2.1), Devlin and Rawls.

  • Devlin is a dangerous ally. He treats a society's morality as a question of fact, honest belief "right or wrong", not truth. On that footing every society's morality is equally entitled to enforcement, so equal validity leads to enforcing majority morality as easily as to tolerance.
  • Rawls is no sceptic. No doctrine is privileged in advance, but unreasonable doctrines are then set aside, and "unreasonable" means refusing fair terms of cooperation, not being false. He brackets moral truth for political purposes; he does not deny it.

Add the second sentence of E2.2: if all moral opinions are equally valid, the demand that legislators tolerate them is one more opinion with no claim on anyone. And the kit's example: in 2007 both camps arguably claimed to be absolutely right, so the task was to manage rival certainties, not to declare all opinions equal.

Close: nor does the sceptic's evidence help. What cannot be proved need not be false, and worry about past moral error presupposes a right answer that was missed. So scepticism yields no conclusion about who decides; fallibility counsels caution in coercion, not silence.

Section 2: who decides

"Each person" or "the majority" deciding does not follow from scepticism, since each is itself a moral claim. Take one route only, never both.

  • For form (a), use the right-to-decide kit (E1.1), Finnis and Hart. Finnis: appealing to subjective preference, then grounding it in the intrinsic value of self-governance, is "outright self-contradiction". Hart: a strong presumption for free choice, which may be valuable in itself, but not an absolute right. Close: each person's right to decide is a claim about the value of autonomy, which the sceptic cannot ground.
  • For form (b), use Devlin from the majority kit (E2.3): the theorist most willing to follow popular morality refuses to count heads; his test is what twelve people at random would, after discussion, accept unanimously, and a majority's dislike is "not nearly enough". Close: majority rule is a claim about equality amid disagreement, and it settles who decides, not what is right.

Section 3: legitimate

Legitimacy turns on reasons, coercion and who bears it, not on scepticism. Use the legitimacy kit (E1.4), Hart and Finnis. Hart: enforcing on critical-morality grounds (harm, defective consent, public offence) is legitimate; punishing immorality as such is not. His objections are moral and prudential, not sceptical. Finnis: the state may favour the basic goods and takes a public stand whatever it does; its limits are moral (peace and justice, the absolute rules) and prudential.

Close, by form: (a) so enforcement can be legitimate though no one is certain; (b) so a majority's enforcement is legitimate only when its reasons and coercion are, not because it is a majority.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

For form (a), the best case is liberty and humility (E1.6): political power belongs to free and equal citizens together, and we have been wrong before, so the state must not make one doctrine official. For form (b), the best case is democratic (E2.5): majority rule treats citizens as equals amid disagreement, and the legislature is the proper forum. Each shows something real about the forum or the doctrine; neither shows that scepticism settles legitimacy.

My weak point, for form (a), is E2's own-side line: my answer rests on a line between denying moral truth and doubting who knows it, and in practice the two can look alike. I concede the overlap in outcome, but the doubter can be asked for reasons and shown wrong, while the sceptic has none to give. For form (b), use E2.6: demanding public reasons is itself a contested moral position, which confirms that every answer takes a stand.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is unsound at its first step, and each later step needs a moral premise the sceptic cannot supply. The four agree that their limits on enforcement are not sceptical: Hart's are moral, Devlin's prudential, Rawls's moral though political, and Finnis's both. They split on whose reasons count. Repaired: “Enforcing morality is legitimate when its reasons are accessible and sound and its coercion proportionate; scepticism is no objection, but fallibility is a reason for restraint.” For the legislator, this means caution in coercion, and reasons the minority can examine.

Watch: E7 runs on E2's blocks, so never put it in the same script as E2, E4 or E9.

Using it in the hypo

A's "just opinion" is non-cognitivism, and "so each must decide" is itself a moral claim about autonomy; A also ignores the child, who is a third party (E1.1). K's "no one knows better" cannot turn 70% into a reason, because a head-count settles who decides, not what is right (E2.3, E2.4). Both skip section 3: does a ban rest on reasons everyone can examine, with coercion graded to them?

Memory line

Scepticism decides nothing about who decides; every link needs a moral premise the sceptic cannot supply.

Sheet blocks to copy from

E2.1E2.2E1.1E2.3E1.4E1.6E2.5E2.6

  2. That "must" is itself a moral claim the sceptic cannot ground.
  3. Correct. Each later link then needs a moral premise the sceptic cannot supply.
  4. Who decides does not follow from scepticism in either direction.
Test the first link before the rest.
key ideaIn form (a), why does "so each person must decide" not follow from scepticism?
  1. The point is about the premise, not about the forum's authority.
  2. The sceptic cannot show that either; the point is that "each decides" is a moral claim.
  3. Correct. Finnis adds that grounding a subjective choice in self-governance's value is "outright self-contradiction".
  4. Rawls's point concerns the highest-value form of autonomy; the essay's point is about grounding.
Autonomy is a value.
key ideaIn form (b), what is wrong with "no one knows better, so the majority decides, so its enforcement is legitimate"?
  1. Fairness of the forum does not make the enforcement's reasons sound.
  2. Correct. Even Devlin refuses to count heads.
  3. The legislature may decide moral questions; it must do so on reasons.
  4. The size of the majority is not the issue.
Who decides is not what is right.
hypoAt the surrogacy commission, A says each woman must decide, and K says the 70% must decide. What do both skip?
  1. Scepticism defeats itself, so the first link fails for both.
  2. Neither the essay nor the theorists rest legitimacy on faith.
  3. Cost is not what either skips; legitimacy is.
  4. Correct. A also ignores the child, a third party; K mistakes a head-count for a reason.
Reasons and coercion.

How these essays fit together

E6 is built from E1's blocks (liberty as a moral value, the ladder, harm), and E7 from E2's (equal validity, scepticism answered, the majority kit, legitimacy). Both say the same thing from different starting points: no route to "law must keep out of morality" works without a moral premise. They can sit together in one script only if the other answer is not E1, E2, E4, E5, E8 or E9. If both are on one paper, give E6 the ladder and the two senses of liberty, and give E7 the scepticism material.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • E6: liberty is itself a moral value; the conflict is between values, not between morality and liberty.
  • Every theorist values liberty, for a different reason.
  • "Pursuit" need not mean punishment: the ladder of promote, regulate, punish.
  • Two senses of liberty: the basic liberties need not be touched; freedom from coercion is limited, not sacrificed.
  • Hart: counting distress as harm would leave only a "quite nugatory" liberty.
  • E7: scepticism defeats itself; fallibility survives and counsels caution.
  • Who decides is itself a moral claim, autonomy or equality, which the sceptic cannot ground.
  • Legitimacy turns on reasons, coercion and who bears it.
  • Repaired: legitimate when reasons are accessible and sound and coercion proportionate; fallibility counsels restraint.

Video 22Judging as politics: the cynic's chainsJudges and lawyers

E25

Judges, politics, theory and the lawyer

The question past-paper chain, paraphrased

"Judges claim only to apply the law, but judging is politics. So legal theory is idle, and the lawyer simply serves the client."

The hypo Hypo 10 with Hypo 9(b)

Your supervising partner cites the study finding that similar District Court cases are decided differently by different judges: "Judging is just politics. Theory is for academics. Leave your conscience at the door; if the conduct rules don't forbid it, we do it for the client." Evaluate his advice.

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

The cynic's chain moves from a truth about rules to a cynicism about everything built on them. Each "so" is a gap: the statement fails at the link.

The answer in one breath

By politics the statement may mean political morality or partisanship, and only the first is true of judging. Rules leave some cases open, but that makes neither judging partisan nor practice free of theory, because judges must justify their decisions, and lawyers act on a view of what law is and what their role permits. Take each link of the chain.

Section 1: "apply the law" and "politics"

Judges do apply the law in easy cases, and where rules run out they choose between principles, not parties. Hart: rules have a core of settled meaning where morality sits idle; only in the penumbra may the judge have to reach beyond the law. Then the shared kit: the realists, Dworkin, then Kennedy.

Close: in Cheng San, literal and purposive readings of "outside" and "at or near" could split, and each side argues from the law, not from a party line. Pressed, even the cynical practitioner concedes that judges reach the result they want whatever the argument only in some kinds of dispute, and that for the most part well-reasoned advocacy decides; otherwise no one would press for legal aid before tribunals (Harris). The cynic's mistake is a flat choice between finding law already there and making it up wholesale, and interpretation is neither (Dworkin). So judging is political in Dworkin's sense, not in the partisan sense the cynic needs.

Section 2: "theory is idle"

If judging turns on rival principles, theory is not idle; it is what the hard case is argued about. Use the theory kit (E20.2).

  • Thunder names where the "just do your job" view gets its appeal: two "unwitting collaborators", legal realism and a rule-bound moral philosophy, which make role-based reasons suspect and prudence amoral.
  • Positivism, even on its own terms: identifying law involves an evaluation, since a lawyer must accept or reject the separation of law and morality before saying what the law requires.
  • Fish: marshalling rules is purposive; without a sense of the general project, rehearsing them is "an empty gesture", though citing Rawls in court would look like window dressing.

Add Llewellyn's "eight times out of ten" (E20.7): easy cases are easy because lawyers share a theory, and the tenth case is where that theory is argued. And from E20's first section: to say the law is whatever the courts say glosses over the principles that should guide them; the purpose of theory is to guide practice and to give a standard by which it may be judged (TSH).

Close: if rules and principles never decided anything, law reform would be pointless, and the only reform left would be to appoint officials with better prejudices (Harris). The choice is between examined and unexamined theory.

Section 3: "the lawyer simply serves the client"

Even if judging were mere politics, the lawyer would still need a view of what the role serves, so the chain's last link does not follow. Serving only the client is itself a theory of the role, the one Thunder traces to legal realism. Use the lawyering kit (E20.3): Pepper's case is moral (autonomy, access, equality); Pepper's own limits keep choice of client, dialogue and objection; Thunder says the role is justified only by its contribution to justice. Then the floor: as Devlin says of the criminal law, law sets the minimum, not the maximum; what is not yet unlawful is not thereby good.

Close: the law is the lawyer's floor; above it, dialogue and rare objection remain.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is the crits'. Each principle meets a counterprinciple that the dominant view files as an anomaly (Unger); doctrine legitimates hierarchy even in progressive hands; and some crits, Kennedy among them, renounced high-level theory altogether (Harris). So talk of theory may only dress up choices already made, and a lawyer can do no more than serve. This shows that doctrine hides choices and that a judge's claim simply to apply the law can mislead. It does not show that judges need not justify, or that the lawyer needs no view of the role: condemning hidden interests needs a standard of its own, and so does serving only the client.

My weak point (E21.4): my answer relies on Dworkin, and fit often yields no unique reading, so whose political morality decides? I concede no answer is demonstrable. But the choice is political, not personal: it must be justified in public reasons, it is tested on appeal, and it starts from the text, not the goal.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right that rules run out, and wrong about what follows. The realists, Dworkin, Pepper and Thunder agree that law leaves room for choice; they split on what fills it: the outcome sought (Kennedy), political morality (Dworkin), the client's lawful wishes (Pepper) or the role's point (Thunder). Repaired: “Rules leave some cases open, where judges must justify by political morality, not preference; lawyers need not cite theory, but they cannot practise without one.” For judges and lawyers, this means examined theory, not none.

Using it in the hypo

The variance study shows open cases at the margin, not that law is politics (E32, section 1), and a judge must justify, not predict. "Theory is for academics" is itself an unexamined theory (E33.2), and "if the rules don't forbid it" mistakes the floor for the whole building (E33.3). Serve the client above that floor, with dialogue.

Memory line

Political, not partisan; examined theory, not none; the client is served above a floor.

E26

Politics, fidelity and staying in role

The question past-paper chain, paraphrased

"Adjudication is politics. Judges' talk of fidelity to law and of justice is a cover. So judges should simply keep to their role."

The hypo Hypo 9(c), adapted

Judge Wong wants to rule for striking drivers lying across a depot entrance though trespass plainly applies. A colleague says: "Judging is politics anyway, and 'fidelity to law' is a cover. Keep to your role: apply the rule and leave the rest to Parliament." Is the colleague right, and what may Judge Wong do?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

The chain runs from scepticism to deference. The flaw is that it refutes itself: if fidelity is a cover, so is the duty to stay in role. And "politics" again has two readings.

The answer in one breath

By fidelity I mean faithfulness to law as law, not deference to whoever rules; by politics, political morality or partisanship. Adjudication is political only in the sense of political morality, so fidelity and justice are not pretences, and the judge's role has moral work of its own. The chain also undoes itself: if fidelity is a cover, so is the duty to stay in role.

Section 1: "politics"

Rules leave some cases open, not all. Use the shared kit: the realists, Dworkin, then Kennedy.

Close: adjudication is political in the sense of political morality, not partisanship.

Section 2: "a cover"

Fidelity is a cover only if the law never constrains, and judges report that it does. First the evidence. A judge who writes "alas" and still finds for the defendant reports that the materials beat his preference. Kennedy's judge sometimes meets a rule he "can't budge". And a flimsy judgment is costly: reputation, reversal, time and credibility keep judges close to the rules.

Then the law-and-justice kit (E20.4). Hart separates law and justice for candour: validity leaves open the final moral question of obedience. Radbruch and Alexy: positive law prevails even when unjust, unless its injustice reaches an intolerable degree; every judicial decision claims correctness, which extreme injustice cannot meet.

Close: Dworkin concedes the cynic's half-truth. Appeals to the framers' intention can be bluster that hides a judge's own political convictions. His cure is candour, since reliance on political theory is part of what interpretation means, not a corruption of it. For Fuller, fidelity embraces the responsibility to make the law what it ought to be, so it is a duty with moral content, not a mask over its absence. Hart asks only that refusal, where it comes, be open, and Radbruch and Alexy confine it to intolerable injustice; neither is a cover for preference.

Section 3: "stay in role"

If fidelity were a cover, the duty to stay in role would be one too, so the conclusion undoes the premise. Use Fuller from the role kit (E21.1): officials, like citizens, owe fidelity to law, not to rank. King Rex's subjects stayed faithful to him as king, not to his law, for he never made any. Respect for constituted authority must not be confused with fidelity to law. Then the twin vices (E21.2): every role can fail twice, by doing others' work or by refusing its own. TSH names both for judges: in bad faith they would legislate judicially or rubber-stamp whatever Parliament passes. Lord Bridge called stopping at decided cases "an unwarranted abdication".

Close: the statement also borrows its premise from those who reject its conclusion. Frank, the most extreme realist, told judges to aim at justice in the case before them, not at justice according to law, and Unger would abandon the belief in "inherent institutional roles" (Harris). And a role is itself made of rules: without them we could not even say who the officials are (Harris). Restraint without candour abdicates, and candour without restraint usurps.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is the realists' at its strongest: if reasons follow results, a judge who speaks of fidelity may be reporting a choice already made, and a judge who claims little and keeps to the role does least harm. This shows that talk of fidelity can hide choices, and that restraint is a sound habit. It does not show that fidelity is always a cover: the costs of a flimsy judgment, and the judge who writes "alas", show the law constraining.

My weak point (E21.4): my answer relies on Dworkin's best reading, which is political, not provably unique. I concede it, and answer that the choice is justified in public reasons and tested on appeal.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is wrong to treat fidelity as a cover, and its own conclusion depends on fidelity being real. The theorists agree that judges work inside constraints; they split on their source: rules (Hart), principles (Dworkin), legal character itself (Radbruch and Alexy) or fidelity to law, not to rank (Fuller). Repaired: “Adjudication is political in the sense of political morality, not partisanship; where the rules leave a case open, judges must justify, not pretend, and keep to a role that includes refusing intolerable injustice.” For the judge, this means candour about the choice and restraint in making it.

Using it in the hypo

The colleague's claim that fidelity is a "cover" undercuts his own advice to keep to the role. The trespass rule plainly applies and is not intolerably unjust, so fidelity binds. Kennedy's covert courses are ruled out by candour (E21.1); Wong may go along and say openly what she thinks (E24.1).

Memory line

If fidelity is a cover, so is the role: the chain undoes itself. Political, not partisan.

How these essays fit together

E25 and E26 start from the same premise and the same kit (E23.1). E25 ends with the lawyer and theory; E26 ends with the judge and the role. They can never share a script, and neither can sit beside E22 or E23. If a paper has a "judging is politics" chain, decide by its last link: theory idle or the lawyer serves, use E25; fidelity a cover or stay in role, use E26. If the statement only says adjudication is politics, use E23's variant instead.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • Both: "politics" means political morality (true) or partisanship (false).
  • The realists: rules leave some cases open, not all; a flimsy judgment is costly.
  • Kennedy works the field but sometimes "can't budge" a rule; Dworkin: political, never partisan.
  • E25: theory is what the hard case is argued about; examined or unexamined theory.
  • Harris: the cynic still asks for legal aid; without rules, reform means better prejudices.
  • The lawyer's "just serve" is itself a theory of the role; law is a floor.
  • E26: fidelity is not a cover; judges report the law constraining ("alas").
  • If fidelity is a cover, so is the role: the chain refutes itself. Fuller: fidelity to law, not rank.
  • Repaired: political morality, not partisanship; justify, don't pretend; keep a role that includes refusing intolerable injustice.

Video 23Law, justice and theory in judging: three practice statementsJudges and lawyers

E27

Judging according to law, and justice

▲ likely

The question FQ3 practice statement

"Adjudicating according to the law serves justice. Judges should therefore avoid lofty concerns of morality, which bring too much subjectivity."

The hypo Hypo 8, Justice Lim

The Succession Act gives an intestate estate "in equal shares to the deceased's children". Anil helped his terminally ill father die at his request, was convicted and fined, and claims the estate. Justice Lim: "I adjudicate according to the law, and that is what justice requires; the words are plain, and lofty moral principles like 'no one may profit from his own wrong' bring too much subjectivity." Is he right?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

The statement makes a promise (according to the law serves justice) and gives an instruction (avoid morality). They pull apart. Break the quotation down: on one reading the promise fails; on the other the instruction fails.

The answer in one breath

"According to the law" has two readings: the sources alone, or the law with its moral floor. "Justice" has two senses: formal justice, treating like cases alike, and substantive justice, the content of what is done. On the positivist's reading the promise fails, since applying valid law secures formal justice only, the like treatment of like cases "by whatever elevated or perverted standards" like is defined (Hart, in Fuller's report). On the natural lawyer's reading the promise holds, but only because law already carries morality, so the instruction to avoid morality fails. No reading saves both sentences. Moral judgement is unavoidable at the margins, and the cure for subjectivity is discipline, not abstinence.

Section 1: "according to the law … serve justice"

Read as the positivist reads it, adjudicating according to the law promises no justice beyond treating like cases alike. From the law-and-justice kit (E20.4): Hart separates law and justice for candour; validity leaves open the final moral question of obedience, and justice in administering the law treats like cases alike by any standard of "like", so it is compatible with very iniquitous law. Fuller: legality is a real but thin morality; it binds even a self-seeking ruler, but it demands rules, whether fair or unfair.

Close: no positivist promises more. For Hart validity never settles the final moral question, and legality's own minimum asks only for rules "however fair or unfair they may be" (Fuller).

Section 2: "justice"

Read as the natural lawyer reads it, the first sentence holds, because law already carries a moral floor. Radbruch and Alexy: positive law prevails even when unjust, unless its injustice reaches an intolerable degree; then, for the participant, it is not law. Every judicial decision claims correctness, which extreme injustice cannot meet. The example: the Federal Constitutional Court (1968) found Ordinance 11, the Nazi decree stripping citizenship on racist criteria, "intolerable" and "evident", with nothing in common with law and justice.

Close: so the court that denied Ordinance 11 legal character adjudicated according to the law on this reading, and could not have done so while avoiding morality.

Section 3: "lofty … too much subjectivity"

"Too much subjectivity" assumes that moral judgement in judging is private. From E20.8: morality in judging is disciplined, not private. Fit and public reasons are tested on appeal, and a rights consensus and broad historical consensus check personal ideology, which is Alexy's answer to Hoerster's objection and TSH's test. A judge's morality may still be wrong, which is why the threshold for overriding the law is high. Then Dworkin from the judging kit: law is interpretive, so judges read the legal record by fit and political value.

Close: legal realism is a claim about how judges decide, not about whether morality is real, so a realist may hold moral beliefs and still decide by the rules. Rawls binds judges most strictly by public reason, a discipline on moral argument, not its absence. Lim Meng Suang shows the discipline at work: the Court of Appeal defended its deference by a theory of adjudication, not by avoiding morality.

The best case and my own weak point

The best case is restraint (E20.9): rules have a core of meaning where morality sits idle, and deciding by personal morality would make each judge a law unto himself. It shows judges must not decide by private conviction; it does not show law can be applied without morality, or that intolerable injustice must stand. My weak point (E38.8): my answer relies on the Radbruch formula, which is only as sound as its minimum moral core; broad historical consensus is evidence, not proof.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right that judges should not decide by private conviction, and wrong that adjudicating according to the law either avoids morality or guarantees justice. The theorists agree that a judge's personal conviction is no ground of decision; they split on where morality enters: at the margins (Hart), through legality (Fuller), in every proposition of law (Dworkin), or only at intolerable injustice (Radbruch and Alexy). Repaired: “Judges serve justice by applying the law where it is clear and tolerably just; where it is open or intolerably unjust, they need moral judgement, disciplined by fit, public reasons and a high threshold.” For the judge, this means fit first, candour always and refusal only at intolerable injustice.

Using it in the hypo

Lim's "plain words" secure formal justice only. The maxim that no one may profit from his own wrong is already part of the common law, so applying it is adjudicating according to law, not lofty morality (E30, section 2). Whether a requested death fits the maxim's point is a question of principle, argued by fit, not by taste (E30.1).

Memory line

No reading saves both sentences: discipline, not abstinence, cures subjectivity.

  2. Personal conviction is no ground on any theory; the point is the two readings.
  3. The marker wants the quotation broken down, not dismissed.
  4. Correct. On the positivist's reading the promise fails; on the natural lawyer's, the instruction fails.

  2. Hart keeps formal justice, the like treatment of like cases.
  3. Validity never settles the final moral question for Hart.
  4. Correct (Hart, in Fuller's report). It is compatible with very iniquitous law.

key ideaWhy does "too much subjectivity" not follow from bringing morality into judging?
  1. Judges have views; the point is that they are disciplined.
  2. Disagreement remains; discipline, not certainty, is the reply.
  3. There is no such review; appeal and fit are the discipline.

  2. Correct. Rawls, too, disciplines moral argument rather than banning it.
  3. Moral realism has nothing to do with legal realism.
  4. Legal realism is not natural law.
