E28

Theory and morality in common law and statutes

▲ likely

The question FQ2 practice statement

"Theories of law and considerations of morality are necessarily implicated when judges decide cases on the common law and on statutes in a constitutional democracy." To what extent do you agree?

The hypo Hypo 23

Three appeals: (a) a 1962 Act lets state schools reserve places by sex; the drafters of the 1960 Constitution thought single-sex schooling consistent with "equal protection", and the AG says their intention is conclusive; (b) a mother watching her daughter struck by a negligent driver on a live video call claims for psychiatric illness, where precedent covers only the scene or its immediate aftermath; (c) a 1950 by-law bans "vehicles" from parks, and fines are imposed for an electric unicycle and a memorial truck. Justice Koh: "I simply find out what the rule-makers meant." To what extent are theory and morality implicated in each?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

"Implicated" has two senses, and "necessarily" is an absolute. The answer is a matter of extent: true to a large extent of theory, to a limited extent of morality, and different for each source.

The answer in one breath

By theories of law I mean accounts of what counts as law; by considerations of morality, judgements of what is right or just, as distinct from what the sources say. "Implicated" has two senses: presupposed, or operative as a ground of decision. Theory is always implicated, since a judge who simply applies the statute has taken a side on what counts as law. Morality is always presupposed, in legality and in every claim that a ruling is correct, but operative only in hard cases and at intolerable injustice. So the statement is true to a large extent of theory and to a limited extent of morality, and the source matters.

Section 1: "theories of law … necessarily"

Even the most positivist judge holds a theory of law. From the judging kit (E20.1): Hart's judge only applies the law in easy cases; in the penumbra he may have to reach beyond it. Dworkin: law is interpretive, so morality enters every proposition of law. Then E21.3 from Hart: for Hart morality enters at most where rules run out; for Fuller, in fidelity joined to the judge's responsibility for making law what it ought to be, since reading a statute by its mischief, like a son finishing his father's invention, makes creativity unavoidable; for Dworkin, in every proposition of law.

Close: theory is necessarily implicated; the only choice is which one, and whether it is examined.

Section 2: common law and statutes

The two sources leave different room (E22.2). At common law only a principle says which differences matter: whether a mother reaching hospital two hours after an accident is like one at the scene (McLoughlin) turns on foreseeability or physical risk, and treating like cases alike needs a moral test of likeness. In statutes the words usually decide, yet maxims and purposes can divide judges: in Riggs, Elmer, who had poisoned his grandfather, was denied the estate on the maxim that no one may profit from his own wrong, against a dissent that would not add to the penalties. Purpose may settle a word such as "vehicles", but not a contested word like "law" (TSH). The rights chapter uses moral terms: "law", "life", "liberty", "equal".

Close: morality decides only where the material runs out or is intolerably unjust, which happens more often at common law than in statute.

Section 3: "considerations of morality … constitutional democracy"

A constitutional democracy adds moral terms to the law, and a reason for restraint in reading them. Radbruch and Alexy: positive law prevails even when unjust, unless intolerably so. TSH: in a stable democracy the formula is a narrow check, not an unelected veto; legislation stands unless intolerably unjust by broad historical consensus. And TSH's point from the judging kit: the rights chapter's "law", "life", "liberty" and "equal" are contested moral terms.

Close: at the extreme, morality is necessarily implicated; in ordinary cases it is presupposed but not operative. The judge reads the rights chapter's moral terms by fit and leaves contested questions to Parliament, as Lim Meng Suang did.

The best case and my own weak point

The best case for the statement is indeterminacy and moral terms (E22.1): rules are picked or construed to fit results reached on other grounds, and each principle meets a counterprinciple (Unger). It shows law does not decide every case mechanically, not that morality decides all of them. My weak point: my line between presupposed and operative may look like having it both ways. I concede that it blurs in hard cases, where a judge's view of law is what decides. But it marks a real difference: in an easy case a judge with another theory would reach the same result; in a hard case he might not, and that is where morality becomes a ground.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is true to a large extent of theories of law and to a limited extent of morality. The theorists agree that judging presupposes a view of law; they split on how often morality is a ground: in every proposition of law (Dworkin), in the penumbra (Hart), or only at intolerable injustice (Radbruch and Alexy). Repaired: “Every judicial decision presupposes a theory of law and a moral view of the role; moral judgement decides only where the sources run out or the law is intolerably unjust.” For the judge, this means fit first and refusal only at intolerable injustice.

Using it in the hypo

(a) A statute read against Part 4's "equal": the drafters intended equality in the abstract, and which description of it governs is a political question (Dworkin, E35.2), so morality is presupposed and perhaps operative. (b) At common law, a principle decides which differences matter (E22.2), so morality is operative. (c) A by-law: purpose settles the unicycle, but the memorial needs the rule's point, so morality is presupposed. Koh's "simply apply it" hides a theory (E21).

Memory line

Theory always; morality presupposed always, operative rarely; common law leaves more room than statute.

E29

Theory guides judges and lawyers

▲ likely

The question FQ6 practice statement

"Theory necessarily guides the practice of judges and lawyers, including how they may weigh the justice of the case." Discuss, assessing the soundness of the theories you use.

The hypo Hypo 21

On Ruritania's Bar Council you must vote on (a) a cab-rank rule barring refusal on moral grounds, (b) a conscience clause letting any lawyer drop a matter she finds objectionable, and (d) a duty to report clients planning lawful but seriously harmful conduct. A member says: "Theory is irrelevant here; we just write practical rules." Is he right, and how would you vote?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

"Guides" has two senses: frames, setting what counts as a reason, or directs, settling each outcome. The statement is right in the first sense and overstated in the second. The soundness limb asks you to test only the parts of each theory you used.

The answer in one breath

I agree with the statement in a sense it does not choose. By theory I mean a view of what law is and of what a role permits. Theory always frames: every judge and lawyer acts on a view of what law is and what the role allows, and the only choice is between examined and unexamined theory. It directs only at the margins, in hard cases and at intolerable injustice, and there it decides how far the justice of the case may count. The theories that do the guiding are each sound for a different job.

Section 1: "necessarily guides", judges

For judges, theory frames every case and decides only some. From the theory kit (E20.2): Dworkin says easy cases are easy because lawyers share a theory, and hard cases turn on rival ones; which arguments count as law is the point in dispute. Positivism, even on its own terms, involves an evaluation in identifying law. Then Llewellyn's "eight times out of ten" (E20.7): the tenth case is where the shared theory is argued.

Close: "necessarily" holds for theory as a frame, not as a script for each outcome.

Section 2: lawyers

For lawyers, the theory in play is a theory of the role. From the lawyering kit (E20.3): Pepper defends the role on moral grounds (law is a public good, autonomy is "a moral good", access needs lawyers); Thunder says the role is justified only by its contribution to justice and flourishing; and Pepper's own limits keep choice of client, dialogue and conscientious objection.

Close: Pepper and Thunder differ more in mindset than in outcome, but the mindset decides how a lawyer meets the hard case, so theory guides even where it changes little.

Section 3: "the justice of the case"

The justice of the case is where theory guides most, because each theory says when justice may count against the law or the client. From the law-and-justice kit: Hart says validity never settles obedience, and asks for candour; Radbruch and Alexy let justice defeat the law only at intolerable injustice. Then the three scenarios for lawyers: is the system by and large just; if so, is this rule intolerably unjust; if not, is this outcome unjust?

Close: theory decides not whether a judge or lawyer cares about justice, but when it may override the rule, and that is guidance of the most practical kind.

Soundness

Test only the parts used (E18.2). Dworkin is sound on why hard cases are argued as law, but fit leaves readings open, so value can look like the judge's politics; he concedes that fit "will often fail to produce a unique interpretation", not that law is "personal or partisan politics". Hart is sound on candour, but his penumbral judge needs a theory of discretion that positivism alone does not supply. Radbruch and Alexy give a sound narrow backstop, if the minimum moral core can be defended without counting votes. Pepper justifies access to law, not every service; Thunder says better what lawyers are for. So rank by job: Dworkin for hard cases, Radbruch and Alexy for the extreme, Thunder for the lawyer's role.

The best case against, and my own weak point

The best case against the statement is practice itself. Lawyers rarely cite theory, most cases are easy, and Rawls cited in court will likely seem "window dressing" (Fish). Thunder traces the "just do your job" view to two "unwitting collaborators", which shows how natural it feels. This shows that theory seldom appears on the surface of practice. It does not show that practice runs without it: Fish himself calls marshalling rules without a sense of the general project "an empty gesture".

My weak point: I lean on Thunder for the lawyer, and he admits that virtue under-determines particular cases. I concede it: his theory guides character more than cases. But that is guidance all the same, since it decides which questions a lawyer asks before acting, and dialogue comes before refusal.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is right that theory necessarily guides practice, and overstated if it means that theory settles each outcome. Theory frames every case, decides the hard ones and sets when the justice of the case may count. The theorists agree that practice runs on some theory of law and of the role; they split on how much it decides: every proposition of law (Dworkin), the penumbra (Hart), the extreme (Radbruch and Alexy), the client's lawful wishes (Pepper) or the role's point (Thunder). Repaired: “Theory necessarily frames the practice of judges and lawyers and decides how far the justice of the case may count; it settles outcomes only in hard cases and at intolerable injustice.” For judges and lawyers, this means examining the theory they already use.

Using it in the hypo

Each proposed rule embodies a theory of the lawyer's role. (a) is Pepper's access argument. (b) is Thunder's conscience, which risks Pepper's "oligarchy of lawyers". (d) turns the lawyer into a policeman, which both reject (E33.6). The member's "just practical" is itself an unexamined theory (E33.2). Vote for a cab-rank rule with a narrow objection clause, and dialogue before any refusal.

Memory line

Theory frames every case, decides the hard ones, and sets when justice may override the rule.

How these essays fit together

All three ask how much theory and morality enter judging, through a different word: "according to the law" (E27), "implicated" (E28), "guides" (E29). All three answer with one distinction: presupposed or framing always, operative or deciding only at the margins. E27 leads with law and justice (E20.4); E28 with the sources (E22.2); E29 with the theory kit, the lawyer, and a soundness limb. Pick the one whose key word matches the statement, and never write two of them in one script.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • E27: "according to the law" read two ways; no reading saves both sentences.
  • Positivism promises only formal justice; natural law's promise holds only because law carries morality.
  • Subjectivity is cured by discipline (fit, public reasons, a high threshold), not abstinence.
  • E28: theory always implicated; morality presupposed always, operative rarely.
  • Common law needs a principle of likeness; statutes usually decide by their words.
  • A constitutional democracy adds moral terms and a reason for restraint.
  • E29: theory frames every case and directs only the hard ones.
  • Lawyers: the mindset decides how a lawyer meets the hard case.
  • Soundness: rank by job; Dworkin for hard cases, Radbruch and Alexy for the extreme, Thunder for the role.

Video 24Fact, will and the citizen's conscienceWhat law is

E40

Fact, will, opinion and conscience

The question past-paper chain, paraphrased

"Law is a fact made by will, and opinion settles what it should be. So private conscience has no claim against it."

The hypo Hypo 24(a)

In Borduria under the Council, Decree 14 requires every citizen to report any Ostran neighbour who has hidden property. Mila is told: "Decree 14 is the Council's law, a fact; most Bordurians support it; your conscience has nothing to say." Is she under any obligation to report, and does her conscience have a claim?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

Each "so" is a gap: from what law is, to what it should be, to what the citizen may do. And the chain undoes itself: if law is only a fact, the fact settles nothing about obedience.

The answer in one breath

By conscience I mean a person's own judgement of whether a law may rightly be obeyed. Law's sources are facts, but will makes law only through rules that bind it, and opinion is evidence of what people want, not of what law should be. And conscience keeps its claim on every theory of what law is: the positivist's own separation leaves the question of obedience open.

Section 1: "a fact made by will"

Law's sources are facts about what officials accept, not about bare will. From the social-fact kit (E37.1): Hart says law is the union of primary and secondary rules that officials accept from the internal point of view, so its sources are facts about that acceptance; but acceptance is not approval, and describing law is not praising it. Natural law, in a clause: human law is made, but law contrary to natural law is defective as law (Aquinas) or lacks power to bind in conscience (Finnis). From the will kit's verdict (E37.2): will makes law only through rules that officials accept and that also bind the will. And in a clause: Article 4 is a master rule only because it is accepted.

Close: law is a fact, but a fact about rules that bind the will that makes them.

Section 2: "opinion settles what it should be"

Facts about opinion fix the stakes; they cannot settle what the law should be. From the kit on facts about opinion (E37.4): Hart says whether the bare fact that positive morality condemns conduct is any reason to punish it is a question of critical morality, not of fact. Finnis infers no value from fact: the basic goods are self-evident and grasped, and practical reasonableness, not the "power of numbers", decides what the common good requires. Then Hume's gap (E37.6): no ought follows from an is alone; the gap is only logical, so facts fix the stakes once an ought-premise is agreed, but the premise must be argued, not read off the poll.

Close: even the reason opinion counts is moral. Each person whose view adds to a consensus has moral dignity (a point from the lectures), so TSH calls consensus perhaps the best gauge of the moral law, not its basis. No ought follows from an is alone, so opinion is evidence to weigh, never the answer.

Section 3: "conscience"

The positivist's separation is what keeps conscience open. From the disobedience kit (E38.2): Hart leaves the citizen the same question, stated honestly. Validity never settles obedience, so the positivist citizen too must ask whether a law is too evil to obey; separating law from morality moves the question of disobedience, it does not remove it. TSH accepts that the real problem is who decides: natural law cannot say who rightfully pronounces its content, and she admits "no easy answer", but discernment, unlike fiat, must cohere and stay revisable. In a clause, from the valid-law kit's verdict: there is a strong default of obedience, a moral default, which grave injustice can defeat.

Close: Finnis makes following one's conscience the ninth requirement of practical reasonableness. Yet my duty to follow it does not stop others from preventing or punishing what I do, and if I know my judgement is ill-formed, it is reasonable to bow to contrary norms. At the extreme every theory vindicates conscience: against Ordinance 11, the Nazi decree stripping citizenship on racist criteria, Hart's citizen should refuse obedience, and for Radbruch there was no law to obey. There is a strong default of obedience, which grave injustice can defeat and mere disagreement cannot.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is order. If conscience may claim against the law, the law binds only those who agree with it, and others will copy the refuser and bring chaos. This shows that the default of obedience must be strong. It does not show that conscience has no claim: that others may copy her justifies punishing her, not a duty to obey.

My weak point: my answer keeps conscience a claim, and conscience may be wrong. That is Hoerster's point, and it binds my side too. I concede it. So I keep refusal open, rare and aimed at grave injustice, never at a law the citizen merely thinks unwise.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is wrong about conscience, and its premises, if true, support the opposite: if law is only a fact, the fact settles nothing about obedience. Hart, Finnis and TSH agree that validity never settles obedience; they split on whether extreme injustice also defeats legal character. Repaired: “Law's sources are matters of social fact, and citizens owe a strong moral duty to obey, which grave injustice can defeat and mere disagreement cannot.” For the citizen, this means obedience as the default and refusal only at grave injustice.

Watch: if E9 is in the same script, write the dignity point here and leave it out of E9.

Using it in the hypo

That Decree 14 is a fact of Borduria's legal system says nothing about whether Mila should obey it (Hart). Majority support is evidence, not a reason. Stripping a minority of citizenship and property is intolerable injustice by any test (E43.6), so every theory vindicates her refusal, though she bears the risk while the Council lasts (E38.7).

Memory line

If law is only a fact, the fact settles nothing about obedience: conscience keeps its claim, rarely and openly used.

E41

Will, legitimacy and obedience

The question past-paper chain, paraphrased

"Law is what the majority wills. No further legitimacy can be shown. So valid law must simply be obeyed."

The hypo Hypo 24(c)

Sergeant Voss is ordered to clear an Ostran village under Decree 14. He reasons: "The Council's decrees are the law of the land; nobody can prove a law legitimate anyway; so I must simply obey." Is he right?

Read the full answerHide the answer

What the question is really testing

The chain runs from will to obedience, and each step drops something. "Simply" is an absolute. And the chain undoes itself: if no legitimacy can be shown, nothing shows that valid law must be obeyed.

The answer in one breath

By legitimacy I mean a right to rule that grounds a moral duty to obey, not mere power to compel. Will makes law only through rules that bind it; legitimacy can be argued, though not proved; and obedience to valid law is a strong moral default, not an automatic duty.

Section 1: "what the majority wills"

Majority will settles who may make law, within rules that bind it. From the will kit (E37.2): Austin says law is the command of a sovereign, habitually obeyed and obeying no one; his strength is candour, but habits miss the internal "ought", and power-conferring rules enable rather than require. Hart: law's rules carry an internal "ought", and secondary rules say whose will counts and how, so they bind the will that makes law. TSH, in a sentence: Article 4 voids statutes inconsistent with the Constitution, so the sovereign is not simply Parliament.

Close: law is more than what the most want, which is why we can criticise them by it. Law with any content the majority wills makes the majority the ultimate lawgiver over its fellow citizens, a point from the lectures.

Section 2: "no further legitimacy can be shown"

Legitimacy cannot be proved like a theorem, but that does not make it arbitrary. From the legitimacy kit (E38.3): Hart says when law exists, not that existing makes it legitimate; describing law is not praising it. Finnis, TSH and Rawls give moral answers, argued, not proved: acceptance cannot alone found legitimacy; the framers' will begs the question why it still binds us; treating non-revolt as acquiescence is artificial; and only co-equal dignity makes consent meaningful (TSH). Rawls's essentials, which all may reasonably endorse, are moral though political. The unproven is not the false.

Close: stopping at will lets an oppressive majority cite its will as legitimacy, a real possibility (TSH). So "no further legitimacy can be shown" confuses argued with proved.

Section 3: "simply be obeyed"

Validity tells the citizen the rule is law; whether to obey is a moral question on every theory. From the valid-law kit (E38.1): Hart says there is a legal duty to obey, but no moral duty follows from validity alone, and past a certain degree of iniquity there is a moral duty to disobey. Fuller grounds obedience in reciprocity, which lasts until its final and complete rupture. The pigeon feeder: deterrence gives her no moral reason to obey; fair play does, since she gains from institutions others uphold, and a positivist has the same reasons.

Then the three questions (E38.4): is there a legal duty to obey, a moral duty to obey, and a moral duty to disobey? Hart: a legal duty, no moral duty from validity alone, a duty to disobey past a certain iniquity. Fuller: a moral duty from reciprocity until its rupture. Radbruch and Alexy: no legal duty past intolerable injustice. Finnis: no prima facie moral duty from unjust law, though one can arise lest a largely just system weaken.

Close: the duty to obey valid law is a strong moral default that rests on examinable reasons, and only grave injustice defeats it.

The best case for the statement, and my own weak point

The best case is order, as in E40: if conscience may claim against the law, the law binds only those who agree with it. Add here that the citizen's morality may itself be wrong (Hoerster), and Radbruch warned of the formula's "frightful dangers". This shows the default of obedience must be strong; it does not show obedience is automatic.

My weak point (E39.1's last three sentences): I concede that in an unjust system the distinction between law and force thins. My answer rests on reasons I cannot prove. But that shows only that legitimacy is argued, and arguing is exactly what force never does.

Conclusion and the repaired statement

The statement is wrong to make obedience automatic, and its second premise undoes its conclusion: if no legitimacy can be shown, nothing shows that valid law must be obeyed. Hart, Fuller and Finnis agree that obedience needs a moral reason; they split on its ground: none from validity alone (Hart), reciprocity (Fuller), or the goods a largely just system secures (Finnis). Repaired: “Citizens owe a strong moral duty to obey, which grave injustice can defeat and mere disagreement cannot.” For the citizen, this means obedience as the default and refusal only at grave injustice.

Using it in the hypo

Validity never settles obedience: Voss owes fidelity to law, not to rank (E21.1). His "nobody can prove legitimacy" undoes his own "must obey". Clearing a village under a decree that betrays equality is intolerable injustice, and Alexy's risk effect reaches an officer who is "neither a saint nor a hero" (E43.2). He should refuse openly.

Memory line

If no legitimacy can be shown, nothing shows you must obey: the chain undoes itself; obedience is a strong moral default.

How these essays fit together

E40 ends with conscience; E41 ends with obedience. Both reach the same repaired line, so never write both in one script. Pick by the statement's last link: "conscience has no claim" is E40; "must simply be obeyed" is E41. Both draw on E37 (fact and will) and E38 (obedience), which is why neither can share a script with E37, E38, E39 or E10. The ultimate-lawgiver point belongs to E41, and the dignity point to E40.

Recap of the main pointsHide the recap
  • E40: law is a fact about rules that bind the will, not bare will.
  • Opinion is evidence, never the answer; even its weight rests on dignity, a moral premise.
  • Conscience keeps its claim on every theory: validity never settles obedience.
  • Finnis's ninth requirement; others may still prevent or punish; bow if your judgement is ill-formed.
  • E41: majority will settles who makes law, within rules; otherwise the majority is the ultimate lawgiver.
  • Legitimacy is argued, not proved; the unproven is not the false.
  • Three questions of obedience; the pigeon feeder's fair play.
  • Both chains undo themselves. Repaired: a strong moral duty to obey, defeated only by grave injustice.
